{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_013w3oRrZbQtMD8w1k3v6DH8","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t\\begin{abstract}\n2\tTraining-free LLM information extraction degrades as schema complexity grows. To our knowledge, we present the first controlled study isolating type confusability from type count in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE, and event detection across four datasets. We introduce the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) and two experimental paradigms: count curves (varying schema size while randomizing confusability) and BCS-controlled experiments (fixing size while varying confusability).\n3\t\n4\tWe identify two degradation channels: count-driven recall decline and confusability-driven type substitution. In typical random batches, confusability accounts for ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp accuracy difference---markedly smaller than count effects ($-$49\\,pp for document-level RE)---while batch-level BCS explains 22--53\\% of cross-batch variance. The channels exhibit bounded interaction in RE (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) but are coupled in NER where boundary and type ambiguity interact. Effects are directionally consistent across prompt designs and model scales, though Channel~2 attenuates on smaller models. Schema decomposition via oracle routing recovers substantial RE gains, but confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random.\n5\t\\end{abstract}\n6\t"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"f6a41211-dbb9-4d83-9c05-a260c0f338e7","timestamp":"2026-05-17T07:59:07.067Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/abstract.tex","content":"\\begin{abstract}\nTraining-free LLM information extraction degrades as schema complexity grows. To our knowledge, we present the first controlled study isolating type confusability from type count in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE, and event detection across four datasets. We introduce the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) and two experimental paradigms: count curves (varying schema size while randomizing confusability) and BCS-controlled experiments (fixing size while varying confusability).\n\nWe identify two degradation channels: count-driven recall decline and confusability-driven type substitution. In typical random batches, confusability accounts for ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp accuracy difference---markedly smaller than count effects ($-$49\\,pp for document-level RE)---while batch-level BCS explains 22--53\\% of cross-batch variance. The channels exhibit bounded interaction in RE (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) but are coupled in NER where boundary and type ambiguity interact. Effects are directionally consistent across prompt designs and model scales, though Channel~2 attenuates on smaller models. Schema decomposition via oracle routing recovers substantial RE gains, but confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random.\n\\end{abstract}\n","numLines":6,"startLine":1,"totalLines":6}}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01RCikrNVFND6Z7EC1c13yv7","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t\\section{Introduction}\n2\t\\label{sec:introduction}\n3\t\n4\tTraining-free\\footnote{We use ``training-free'' and ``zero-shot'' interchangeably to denote prompting without parameter updates.}\\footnote{Code and data will be released upon acceptance. Anonymous review materials: \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/schema-dag-extraction}} LLM information extraction---prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning---enables rapid deployment across new domains and ontologies. This zero-shot paradigm eliminates the need for labeled data, making it attractive for applications where annotation is expensive or schemas evolve frequently \\cite{llm4ie-survey-2024}. Yet the paradigm carries a critical limitation: performance degrades sharply as the number of candidate types in the prompt schema grows. On named entity recognition, recall drops from 0.72 with a single candidate type to a plateau of ${\\sim}$0.27 across intermediate schema sizes (22--44 types) before partial recovery at full-schema. On relation extraction, accuracy falls from 85.7\\% with one relation to 49.5\\% with 80 relations (sentence-level) and from 88.0\\% to 38.7\\% with 96 relations (document-level)---near or exceeding halving. This degradation is not academic: real-world ontologies routinely define 50--200+ types, placing them squarely in the regime where training-free extraction becomes unreliable.\n5\t\n6\tWhat explains this degradation? Prior decomposition strategies \\cite{uniner-2024,llm-pee-2025,parse-2025} conflate two mechanisms: having more types to discriminate among (count) and having semantically similar types to confuse (confusability) (hereafter Channel~1 and Channel~2). None isolates which factor predicts the gains (\\S\\ref{sec:related_work}).\n7\t\n8\tWe ask three questions. \\textbf{RQ1}: Does type confusability independently predict extraction degradation beyond the effect of type count? \\textbf{RQ2}: Through what mechanisms do count and confusability each degrade performance? \\textbf{RQ3}: Can confusability-aware schema decomposition improve extraction quality?\n9\t\n10\tTo our knowledge, we present the first controlled study isolating type confusability from type count via stratified design in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE, and event detection (ED). We operationalize confusability via the \\emph{Schema Confusability Score} (SCS)---pairwise embedding cosine similarity---and its batch-level aggregation, the \\emph{Batch Confusability Score} (BCS). We evaluate on Few-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021}, FewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018}, DocRED \\cite{docred-2019}, and MAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} using Qwen3-32B (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental_setup}).\n11\t\n12\tOur experiments yield five findings: (1)~degradation operates through two channels---count-driven recall decline (up to $-$49\\,pp at intermediate schema sizes for document-level RE) and confusability-driven type substitution (typical-batch gap ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp; quartile $\\Delta = -5.3$\\,pp)---an asymmetric relationship where count dominates, consistent with bounded interaction in RE (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) but coupled in NER where NER recall exhibits non-monotonic recovery at full-schema; (2)~pairwise SCS predicts type confusion ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), the first factor empirically isolated in controlled zero-shot IE; (3)~effects are consistent across schema sizes and granularities (BCS slopes: $p = 0.60$; DocRED: slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), and directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts; (4)~per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas; (5)~NER decomposition fails due to merge amplification; RE confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) despite oracle routing recovering +29.8\\,pp.\n13\t\n14\tOur contributions are:\n15\t\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt,topsep=2pt]\n16\t    \\item \\textbf{Count dominates confusability by ${\\sim}20\\times$ in typical batches.} Through stratified design isolating count from confusability, we show count effects ($-$42\\% to $-$56\\% recall) dwarf typical confusability effects (2.2\\,pp), counter to decomposition literature's implicit assumption that confusability is the primary bottleneck. Interaction is bounded (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) in RE but not in NER, where boundary and type ambiguity couple (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}).\n17\t\\item \\textbf{Pairwise embedding similarity predicts type confusion.} SCS correlates with confusion rates ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), with centrality confound identified on homogeneous schemas---invalidating naive per-type aggregation (\\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}).\n18\t\\item \\textbf{BCS generalizes across tasks.} BCS--accuracy slopes are consistent across schema sizes ($p = 0.60$) and replicate at document level (DocRED: $R^2 = 0.22\\text{--}0.53$) (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n19\t\\item \\textbf{Schema decomposition exposes systematic routing failure.} Naive merge strategies (union, confidence-gated) collapse NER precision from 0.56 to 0.21. On RE, we evaluate seven routing strategies (oracle, five practical heuristics, random); all practical strategies fall below the single-pass baseline (best 36.5\\% vs.\\ 50.4\\%), because constrained decoding eliminates reliable sub-schema confidence signals. Oracle routing recovers +29.8\\,pp, but confusability-aware grouping adds only +1.4\\,pp ($p \\approx 0.19$) over random, leaving deployable routing as an open challenge (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}).\n20\t\\end{enumerate}"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"c1fa2f26-e5ca-45e2-8a88-780a0ff44a2a","timestamp":"2026-05-17T07:59:07.418Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/introduction.tex","content":"\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nTraining-free\\footnote{We use ``training-free'' and ``zero-shot'' interchangeably to denote prompting without parameter updates.}\\footnote{Code and data will be released upon acceptance. Anonymous review materials: \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/schema-dag-extraction}} LLM information extraction---prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning---enables rapid deployment across new domains and ontologies. This zero-shot paradigm eliminates the need for labeled data, making it attractive for applications where annotation is expensive or schemas evolve frequently \\cite{llm4ie-survey-2024}. Yet the paradigm carries a critical limitation: performance degrades sharply as the number of candidate types in the prompt schema grows. On named entity recognition, recall drops from 0.72 with a single candidate type to a plateau of ${\\sim}$0.27 across intermediate schema sizes (22--44 types) before partial recovery at full-schema. On relation extraction, accuracy falls from 85.7\\% with one relation to 49.5\\% with 80 relations (sentence-level) and from 88.0\\% to 38.7\\% with 96 relations (document-level)---near or exceeding halving. This degradation is not academic: real-world ontologies routinely define 50--200+ types, placing them squarely in the regime where training-free extraction becomes unreliable.\n\nWhat explains this degradation? Prior decomposition strategies \\cite{uniner-2024,llm-pee-2025,parse-2025} conflate two mechanisms: having more types to discriminate among (count) and having semantically similar types to confuse (confusability) (hereafter Channel~1 and Channel~2). None isolates which factor predicts the gains (\\S\\ref{sec:related_work}).\n\nWe ask three questions. \\textbf{RQ1}: Does type confusability independently predict extraction degradation beyond the effect of type count? \\textbf{RQ2}: Through what mechanisms do count and confusability each degrade performance? \\textbf{RQ3}: Can confusability-aware schema decomposition improve extraction quality?\n\nTo our knowledge, we present the first controlled study isolating type confusability from type count via stratified design in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE, and event detection (ED). We operationalize confusability via the \\emph{Schema Confusability Score} (SCS)---pairwise embedding cosine similarity---and its batch-level aggregation, the \\emph{Batch Confusability Score} (BCS). We evaluate on Few-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021}, FewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018}, DocRED \\cite{docred-2019}, and MAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} using Qwen3-32B (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental_setup}).\n\nOur experiments yield five findings: (1)~degradation operates through two channels---count-driven recall decline (up to $-$49\\,pp at intermediate schema sizes for document-level RE) and confusability-driven type substitution (typical-batch gap ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp; quartile $\\Delta = -5.3$\\,pp)---an asymmetric relationship where count dominates, consistent with bounded interaction in RE (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) but coupled in NER where NER recall exhibits non-monotonic recovery at full-schema; (2)~pairwise SCS predicts type confusion ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), the first factor empirically isolated in controlled zero-shot IE; (3)~effects are consistent across schema sizes and granularities (BCS slopes: $p = 0.60$; DocRED: slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), and directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts; (4)~per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas; (5)~NER decomposition fails due to merge amplification; RE confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) despite oracle routing recovering +29.8\\,pp.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt,topsep=2pt]\n    \\item \\textbf{Count dominates confusability by ${\\sim}20\\times$ in typical batches.} Through stratified design isolating count from confusability, we show count effects ($-$42\\% to $-$56\\% recall) dwarf typical confusability effects (2.2\\,pp), counter to decomposition literature's implicit assumption that confusability is the primary bottleneck. Interaction is bounded (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) in RE but not in NER, where boundary and type ambiguity couple (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}).\n\\item \\textbf{Pairwise embedding similarity predicts type confusion.} SCS correlates with confusion rates ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), with centrality confound identified on homogeneous schemas---invalidating naive per-type aggregation (\\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}).\n\\item \\textbf{BCS generalizes across tasks.} BCS--accuracy slopes are consistent across schema sizes ($p = 0.60$) and replicate at document level (DocRED: $R^2 = 0.22\\text{--}0.53$) (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\\item \\textbf{Schema decomposition exposes systematic routing failure.} Naive merge strategies (union, confidence-gated) collapse NER precision from 0.56 to 0.21. On RE, we evaluate seven routing strategies (oracle, five practical heuristics, random); all practical strategies fall below the single-pass baseline (best 36.5\\% vs.\\ 50.4\\%), because constrained decoding eliminates reliable sub-schema confidence signals. Oracle routing recovers +29.8\\,pp, but confusability-aware grouping adds only +1.4\\,pp ($p \\approx 0.19$) over random, leaving deployable routing as an open challenge (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}).\n\\end{enumerate}","numLines":20,"startLine":1,"totalLines":20}}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01F8FDVNo2U7RLtXANLaiCGm","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t\\section{Discussion}\n2\t\\label{sec:discussion}\n3\t\n4\t\\paragraph{Boundary conditions.}\n5\tThe decomposition applies to cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas exhibit centrality confounding (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). High-BCS accuracy converges to $\\approx$51\\% across models, suggesting a difficulty ceiling. RE's separation may partly reflect its closed-set format; the effect generalizes to document-level RE (DocRED slope $-$0.71). Prompt design modulates but does not eliminate the effect (v2 gap: $-$25.5\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}). Cross-scale evidence (32B vs.\\ 8B) shows Channel~1 replicates fully while Channel~2 attenuates on smaller models, dropping to near-zero $R^2$ for NER on Llama-3.1-8B (Tables~\\ref{tab:cross_model}--\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Count-driven degradation may partly reflect prompt length (per-type recall 0.586 vs.\\ single-pass 0.368, consistent with lost-in-middle effects; \\citealt{lost-in-middle-2024}).\n6\t\n7\t\\paragraph{Why does embedding similarity predict LLM confusion?}\n8\tSCS captures semantic proximity in LLMs' type-resolution space. The unconditional pair-level effect is modest ($r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$, as ${\\sim}89\\%$ of pairs never produce mutual confusion); among active-confusion pairs, $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ amplifies ${\\sim}3\\times$ at batch level (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim}), yielding $R^2$ of 22--53\\%. The NER U-shape (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}) reflects a dual-task burden absent in RE: partial schemas require simultaneous span detection and type-membership filtering; at full-schema, filtering becomes vacuous and recall recovers, confirming task-specific mechanistic pathways.\n9\t\n10\t\\section{Conclusion}\n11\t\\label{sec:conclusion}\n12\t\n13\tSchema-induced degradation in training-free LLM extraction operates through two channels (count-driven recall decline, confusability-driven type confusion) with bounded interaction in RE but coupling in NER. Channel~1 replicates across scales; Channel~2 attenuates on smaller models. BCS enables pre-hoc screening.\n14\t"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"7d4cc843-efff-45d5-83b8-9dd00594bb8a","timestamp":"2026-05-17T07:59:07.901Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/discussion.tex","content":"\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Boundary conditions.}\nThe decomposition applies to cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas exhibit centrality confounding (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). High-BCS accuracy converges to $\\approx$51\\% across models, suggesting a difficulty ceiling. RE's separation may partly reflect its closed-set format; the effect generalizes to document-level RE (DocRED slope $-$0.71). Prompt design modulates but does not eliminate the effect (v2 gap: $-$25.5\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}). Cross-scale evidence (32B vs.\\ 8B) shows Channel~1 replicates fully while Channel~2 attenuates on smaller models, dropping to near-zero $R^2$ for NER on Llama-3.1-8B (Tables~\\ref{tab:cross_model}--\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Count-driven degradation may partly reflect prompt length (per-type recall 0.586 vs.\\ single-pass 0.368, consistent with lost-in-middle effects; \\citealt{lost-in-middle-2024}).\n\n\\paragraph{Why does embedding similarity predict LLM confusion?}\nSCS captures semantic proximity in LLMs' type-resolution space. The unconditional pair-level effect is modest ($r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$, as ${\\sim}89\\%$ of pairs never produce mutual confusion); among active-confusion pairs, $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ amplifies ${\\sim}3\\times$ at batch level (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim}), yielding $R^2$ of 22--53\\%. The NER U-shape (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}) reflects a dual-task burden absent in RE: partial schemas require simultaneous span detection and type-membership filtering; at full-schema, filtering becomes vacuous and recall recovers, confirming task-specific mechanistic pathways.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nSchema-induced degradation in training-free LLM extraction operates through two channels (count-driven recall decline, confusability-driven type confusion) with bounded interaction in RE but coupling in NER. Channel~1 replicates across scales; Channel~2 attenuates on smaller models. BCS enables pre-hoc screening.\n","numLines":14,"startLine":1,"totalLines":14}}}

Under closed-set classification, non-\\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the positive correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}). The pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}); the problem is per-type aggregation, not the underlying relationship. Few-NERD's cross-domain schema does not exhibit this confound ($\\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$). Error bars show $\\pm 1$\\,SD across 3 seeds (42, 123, 456) $\\times$ 25 batches per seed. RE accuracy declines monotonically with count; NER recall exhibits a U-shaped pattern with minimum at intermediate counts. Full-schema points (rightmost, hollow markers) represent the complete type inventory with no batch sampling variance. \\textbf{(b)} BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. The BCS--accuracy slope is consistent across the independent 16-relation pool and within-pool 16-relation subsets (permutation $p = 0.60$); the wider BCS range available at 80 relations produces larger absolute gaps.}\n\\label{fig:main_results}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:main_results}a shows RE accuracy declines monotonically with schema size (0.86$\\to$0.50 for FewRel). NER recall declines through intermediate counts (0.51$\\to$0.27, plateauing across counts 22--44) before partial recovery at full-schema (0.41), where the elimination of type-filtering overhead restores extraction performance. DocRED replicates the monotonic RE pattern (88.0\\%$\\to$38.7\\%, $-$56\\%; Appendix). MAVEN exhibits a reversed trend attributable to centrality confounding (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). The effect replicates on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($-$43\\,pp on Few-NERD; per-batch $r = 0.725$; Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}), indicating schema configuration rather than model-specific factors predicts difficulty. The pattern replicates across three seeds (42, 123, 456; max $\\Delta = 10.7$\\,pp, within 1\\,SD); intra-batch variance exceeds inter-seed variance by ${>}$5$\\times$ at every count level (Appendix~\\ref{app:variance-decomposition}). Formal trend analysis confirms monotonic RE decline (Jonckheere-Terpstra $J = 109{,}707$, $p < 0.001$, pooled across three seeds). Count curves (25 batches per level) establish trend direction with error bars; magnitude estimation relies on the higher-powered BCS-controlled experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\n\\subsection{Pairwise Confusability and Type Confusion}\n\\label{sec:pairwise}\n\nWe test whether pairwise SCS predicts type-level confusion rates. For each type pair $(t_i, t_j)$, we compute the confusion rate---the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$---and correlate it with $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)$. Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Pearson correlations with bootstrap 95\\% CIs.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Embed.} & \\textbf{$n$ pairs} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$r^2$} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & Qwen3 & 1{,}953 & 0.112 & 0.013 & {[}0.076, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMAVEN & MiniLM & 11{,}026 & 0.129 & 0.017 & {[}0.104, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Pairwise SCS--confusion correlation. $r^2$ indicates the fraction of variance in type confusion explained by embedding similarity. Both correlations are modest in magnitude but robust across datasets and embedding models (both $p \\ll 0.001$). Confusion rate for each ordered pair $(t_i, t_j)$ is computed as the total bidirectional confusion count $C(t_i{\\to}t_j) + C(t_j{\\to}t_i)$ divided by the sum of gold frequencies, aggregated over all count conditions; CIs are pair-level percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$). FewRel ($r = 0.187$; \\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}) is excluded as RE's per-instance format precludes direct comparison of confusion rates. Type-level cluster bootstrap CIs: Few-NERD [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN [0.081, 0.185]; both exclude zero, confirming robustness to within-type correlation.}\n\\label{tab:pairwise}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe correlations are statistically significant but modest: $r^2 \\approx 1.3\\text{--}1.7\\%$. Type-level cluster bootstrap yields wider but still significant CIs (Few-NERD: [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN: [0.081, 0.185]; $B = 10{,}000$). Both datasets show significant positive correlations between pairwise SCS and type confusion rates. Spearman correlations corroborate: $\\rho = 0.144\\text{--}0.227$ across all pairs, strengthening to $\\rho = 0.27\\text{--}0.31$ among pairs with nonzero confusion (though conditioning on the dependent variable may inflate this).\n\nThe remaining variance likely reflects entity frequency, prompt position \\cite{lost-in-middle-2024}, mention ambiguity, and definition quality. Although pairwise $r^2$ is modest, the signal compounds at the batch level into quartile gaps of 3.9--5.3\\,pp under typical batching (\\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}). Notably, the independent MiniLM embedding yields a \\emph{stronger} SCS--confusion correlation on Few-NERD ($r = 0.172$ vs.\\ Qwen3 $r = 0.138$; Appendix), suggesting that shared-representation bias is conservative rather than inflationary.\n\n\\subsection{BCS-Controlled Experiments}\n\\label{sec:bcs_controlled}\n\nWe fix type count and vary BCS. FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per type) eliminates type frequency as a confound; the within-pool analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:within_pool}) controls for pool composition.\n\nIn BCS-controlled FewRel subsets, the high-BCS quartile shows $\\Delta=-5.3$\\,pp lower accuracy than low-BCS (quartile comparison; full data in Appendix~\\ref{app:bcs-controlled-table}). At 80 relations, extreme-batch construction amplifies this to $-$31.2\\,pp (CI: [17.7, 42.7]), stable across count levels. An intermediate count${}\\,=\\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\\,pp at count 16), confirming monotonic decline.\n\n\\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}\nThe effect replicates on DocRED's 96-relation schema: BCS gap $-$23.3\\,pp at count 16 (slope $= -0.713$, $\\rho = -0.661$, $p < 0.001$) and $-$17.6\\,pp at count 32 (slope $= -0.690$, $\\rho = -0.509$, $p = 0.002$). BCS explains 44--53\\% of cross-batch variance ($R^2 = 0.525$ at count 16), substantially higher than FewRel ($R^2 = 0.218$), possibly reflecting a wider confusability range in the DocRED schema, though document-level context may also provide richer cues for type confusion. Across all RE settings, BCS slopes range from $-$0.40 to $-$0.71, significant in all conditions with sufficient BCS range.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-pool controlled comparison.}\n\\label{sec:within_pool}\nWithin-pool analysis confirms slope consistency across pools (permutation $p \\approx 0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:within-pool-method}). The 80-relation extreme-batch gap (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) reflects wider BCS range, not stronger per-unit sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}\n\\label{sec:practical_magnitude}\nFor representative batches from the 80-relation pool ($n{=}100$ random 16-type subsets), the typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\\,pp (95\\% CI: [0.4, 4.0])---roughly 7\\% of the extreme-batch gap. Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\\% vs.\\ 78.9\\% for top-quartile ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp). The 31.2\\,pp figure reflects maximum BCS contrast, not the typical batch experience. Conditional pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ among non-zero confusion pairs (vs.\\ unconditional $r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$ across all pairs; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}; we note the conditioning inflates this estimate) aggregates to batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ ($\\sim$3$\\times$ amplification, consistent with a Monte Carlo simulation accounting for inter-pair correlation structure; see Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim} for the illustrative aggregation model).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}\nThe effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: BCS gaps of $-$27.1\\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\\% of Qwen3-32B's gap. Both models converge to $\\approx$51\\% under high confusability regardless of count, while divergence is confined to the low-BCS regime---suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor under high confusability (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Channel~1 replicates fully on Llama-3.1-8B (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}); Channel~2 replicates on RE but shows floor effects on 8B NER (Limitations, item~1).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}\nUnder the v2 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; $n{=}25$; $r = -0.919$; $p < 0.001$)---modestly attenuated from v1's extreme-batch gap but highly significant. NER v2 yields consistent batch-level correlations ($r = +0.58$ vs.\\ $r = +0.50$ under v1). Prompt design modulates magnitude but does not eliminate the effect (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}). The BCS--accuracy correlation holds across four structurally distinct prompt variants at count 32 (all $r < -0.47$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}), confirming that the confusability effect is not an artifact of prompt design.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Channel Degradation}\n\\label{sec:multi_channel}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}\n\\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition. IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS in both tasks. RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic.}\n\\label{fig:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{figure}\n\nError taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.\n\nThese results establish two channels: \\textbf{Channel~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \\textbf{Channel~2} (confusability predicts type confusion), consistent with bounded interaction in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact. The full-range regression ($n = 69$, five count levels) serves as the primary separability test; the $2 \\times 2$ factorial ($n = 54$) as a sensitivity check. TOST equivalence testing confirms bounded interaction (interaction = 0.47\\,pp, $p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}).\n\n\\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}\n\\label{sec:centrality_confound}\n\nPer-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \\emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation (Qwen3 $\\rho = +0.184$, $p = 0.025$; MiniLM $\\rho = +0.208$, $p = 0.011$). This traces to same-domain embedding collapse \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}: MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly, so mean BCS conflates confusability with centrality---high-BCS types are semantically central and easy to extract. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the positive correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}). The pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}); the problem is per-type aggregation, not the underlying relationship. Few-NERD's cross-domain schema does not exhibit this confound ($\\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$). Mean BCS is thus reliable only for cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas require pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment.","numLines":92,"startLine":1,"totalLines":92}}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_019wbeLJgF2Coahw68UDkT9x","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t\\section{Related Work}\n2\t\\label{sec:related_work}\n3\t\n4\t\\paragraph{Training-free LLM IE.}\n5\tTraining-free LLM IE spans instruction tuning \\cite{instructuie-2023}, code-style schema encoding \\cite{knowcoder-2024,codeie-2023}, annotation-guided extraction \\cite{gollie-2024,adelie-2024}, schema-driven decoding \\cite{slot-2025,oneke-2025}, and constrained JSON decoding \\cite{json-schema-bench-2025}. For NER, methods include zero-shot decomposition \\cite{xie-zeroshot-ner-2023,promptner-2023}, distillation \\cite{uniner-2024}, and bidirectional encoders \\cite{gliner-2024,gliner2-2025}. For RE, recent work examines zero-shot relation triplet extraction via prompting \\cite{relationprompt-2022} and revisits relation extraction in the LLM era \\cite{wadhwa-re-llm-2023}. These methods perform well with moderate schemas (4--20 types) but none investigates how performance degrades as schema complexity scales, nor proposes quantitative metrics for inter-type confusability.\n6\t\n7\t\\paragraph{Schema complexity in IE.}\n8\tThe most directly related work is LLM-PEE \\cite{llm-pee-2025}, which partitions large event schemas by type embedding similarity. Our work differs in three ways: (1)~we conduct controlled analysis to understand \\emph{why} extraction degrades rather than developing an engineering solution; (2)~our BCS-controlled experiments isolate count from confusability, which LLM-PEE conflates; (3)~our SDGD experiments reveal that merge amplification---not partitioning quality---is the bottleneck in decompose-then-merge pipelines. Specifically, LLM-PEE employs union merge across sub-schema extractions, which our \\S\\ref{sec:sdgd} analysis shows causes precision to collapse from 0.56 to 0.21 in NER as false positives accumulate across passes; RE avoids this because closed-set classification does not require span reconciliation, explaining why LLM-PEE's decomposition succeeds for relation/event tasks but faces unresolved merge challenges on span-level extraction. Other approaches address schema complexity via hard negative mining \\cite{iepile-2024}, schema definition optimization \\cite{parse-2025}, multi-agent debate \\cite{crossagentie-2025,cmas-ner-2025}, schema-aware decomposition for event extraction \\cite{dee-2024}, and active prompting \\cite{reflect-learn-2026}; all treat it as an engineering problem rather than isolating the underlying degradation mechanisms. Concurrently, \\citet{haystack-to-needle-2025} reduces label spaces via iterative LLM filtering, an engineering complement to our analytical approach. Our framework explains \\emph{why} such reduction works: it implicitly lowers batch confusability.\n9\t\n10\t\\paragraph{Schema complexity in dialogue and IE.} The Schema-Guided Dialogue dataset~\\cite{rastogi2020towards} and SGD-X~\\cite{lee2022sgd} document performance degradation with schema linguistic variation. \\citet{li2023evaluating} observe count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT IE. \\citet{volkanovska2024ner} categorize LLM NER errors including type confusion. The relationship between label similarity and classification confusion has precedent in traditional ML: hierarchical classification suffers sibling confusion proportional to inter-class proximity \\citep{deng-2010-imagenet}, label confusion matrices improve text classification \\citep{guo-2021-label-confusion}, and classification confusion captures semantic similarity \\citep{zhou-2025-rethinking}. Hierarchical label sets reduce class confusion in zero-shot image classification \\citep{novack-2023-chils}; enriched type descriptions improve zero-shot NER \\citep{cocchieri-2025-zeroner}---both orthogonal to our confusability-aware batching.\n11\t\n12\t\\paragraph{SCS novelty.} SCS shares its formal definition---pairwise cosine similarity of type name embeddings---with the partitioning criterion used in LLM-PEE~\\cite{llm-pee-2025} for schema decomposition. Our contribution is not the metric itself but the controlled experimental framework that isolates confusability's predictive relationship with extraction degradation, distinguishing it from the engineering use in prior work. The modest pairwise effect size ($r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$) is consistent with standards for individual-difference predictors in behavioral research \\citep{cohen-1988-power}, where such magnitudes are considered ``small but real'' effects that compound at the aggregate level \\citep{lakens-2013-effect-size}.\n13\t\n14\t\\paragraph{Embedding anisotropy.}\n15\tOur centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}) connects to representation degeneration: embeddings collapse into narrow cones \\cite{gao-repr-degen-2019,ethayarajh-contextual-2019}, but local clusters can be isotropic \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}---explaining why MAVEN's single-ontology types conflate confusability with centrality.\n16\t\n17\t"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"cc5f9726-fbcc-4a23-9c62-e0e005b785d6","timestamp":"2026-05-17T07:59:08.817Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/related_work.tex","content":"\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Training-free LLM IE.}\nTraining-free LLM IE spans instruction tuning \\cite{instructuie-2023}, code-style schema encoding \\cite{knowcoder-2024,codeie-2023}, annotation-guided extraction \\cite{gollie-2024,adelie-2024}, schema-driven decoding \\cite{slot-2025,oneke-2025}, and constrained JSON decoding \\cite{json-schema-bench-2025}. For NER, methods include zero-shot decomposition \\cite{xie-zeroshot-ner-2023,promptner-2023}, distillation \\cite{uniner-2024}, and bidirectional encoders \\cite{gliner-2024,gliner2-2025}. For RE, recent work examines zero-shot relation triplet extraction via prompting \\cite{relationprompt-2022} and revisits relation extraction in the LLM era \\cite{wadhwa-re-llm-2023}. These methods perform well with moderate schemas (4--20 types) but none investigates how performance degrades as schema complexity scales, nor proposes quantitative metrics for inter-type confusability.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema complexity in IE.}\nThe most directly related work is LLM-PEE \\cite{llm-pee-2025}, which partitions large event schemas by type embedding similarity. Our work differs in three ways: (1)~we conduct controlled analysis to understand \\emph{why} extraction degrades rather than developing an engineering solution; (2)~our BCS-controlled experiments isolate count from confusability, which LLM-PEE conflates; (3)~our SDGD experiments reveal that merge amplification---not partitioning quality---is the bottleneck in decompose-then-merge pipelines. Specifically, LLM-PEE employs union merge across sub-schema extractions, which our \\S\\ref{sec:sdgd} analysis shows causes precision to collapse from 0.56 to 0.21 in NER as false positives accumulate across passes; RE avoids this because closed-set classification does not require span reconciliation, explaining why LLM-PEE's decomposition succeeds for relation/event tasks but faces unresolved merge challenges on span-level extraction. Other approaches address schema complexity via hard negative mining \\cite{iepile-2024}, schema definition optimization \\cite{parse-2025}, multi-agent debate \\cite{crossagentie-2025,cmas-ner-2025}, schema-aware decomposition for event extraction \\cite{dee-2024}, and active prompting \\cite{reflect-learn-2026}; all treat it as an engineering problem rather than isolating the underlying degradation mechanisms. Concurrently, \\citet{haystack-to-needle-2025} reduces label spaces via iterative LLM filtering, an engineering complement to our analytical approach. Our framework explains \\emph{why} such reduction works: it implicitly lowers batch confusability.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema complexity in dialogue and IE.} The Schema-Guided Dialogue dataset~\\cite{rastogi2020towards} and SGD-X~\\cite{lee2022sgd} document performance degradation with schema linguistic variation. \\citet{li2023evaluating} observe count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT IE. \\citet{volkanovska2024ner} categorize LLM NER errors including type confusion. The relationship between label similarity and classification confusion has precedent in traditional ML: hierarchical classification suffers sibling confusion proportional to inter-class proximity \\citep{deng-2010-imagenet}, label confusion matrices improve text classification \\citep{guo-2021-label-confusion}, and classification confusion captures semantic similarity \\citep{zhou-2025-rethinking}. Hierarchical label sets reduce class confusion in zero-shot image classification \\citep{novack-2023-chils}; enriched type descriptions improve zero-shot NER \\citep{cocchieri-2025-zeroner}---both orthogonal to our confusability-aware batching.\n\n\\paragraph{SCS novelty.} SCS shares its formal definition---pairwise cosine similarity of type name embeddings---with the partitioning criterion used in LLM-PEE~\\cite{llm-pee-2025} for schema decomposition. Our contribution is not the metric itself but the controlled experimental framework that isolates confusability's predictive relationship with extraction degradation, distinguishing it from the engineering use in prior work. The modest pairwise effect size ($r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$) is consistent with standards for individual-difference predictors in behavioral research \\citep{cohen-1988-power}, where such magnitudes are considered ``small but real'' effects that compound at the aggregate level \\citep{lakens-2013-effect-size}.\n\n\\paragraph{Embedding anisotropy.}\nOur centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}) connects to representation degeneration: embeddings collapse into narrow cones \\cite{gao-repr-degen-2019,ethayarajh-contextual-2019}, but local clusters can be isotropic \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}---explaining why MAVEN's single-ontology types conflate confusability with centrality.\n\n","numLines":17,"startLine":1,"totalLines":17}}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_016s4Qg89mbx2Z6GpCHBLsZd","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t\\section{Schema Decomposition: Failure, Diagnosis, and Mitigation}\n2\t\\label{sec:sdgd}\n3\t\n4\tA natural intervention against confusability-driven degradation is to partition the schema so that confusable types never co-occur in the same prompt. We test this idea with \\textbf{Schema-DAG-Guided Decomposition} (SDGD\\footnote{SDGD (Schema Decomposition and Grouping for Degradation mitigation) is an adopted abbreviation for schema decomposition and grouping; the graph structure is undirected.}), which uses greedy graph coloring on the pairwise SCS graph to assign types to low-confusability batches.\n5\t\n6\tWe construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \\tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\\textsc{largest\\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent---i.e., highly confusable---types share a batch. Each color class forms one extraction batch. For NER, predictions from all batches are merged via set union; for RE, each instance is routed to the batch containing its gold type (oracle routing). We test four NER modes: (i)~\\textbf{single\\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\\textbf{sdgd\\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\\textbf{random\\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\\textbf{per\\_type}, each type extracted in isolation. Every mode falls below the single-pass NER baseline: SDGD obtains F1\\,=\\,0.251, random batching 0.320, per-type 0.292, versus 0.445 for single-pass (Table~\\ref{tab:sdgd_results}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd}).\n7\t\n8\t\\paragraph{Merge amplification in NER.}\n9\tThe core failure mechanism is \\emph{merge amplification}: presenting the same text to the model $k$ times with different type subsets causes each pass to independently generate entity predictions, and union merge accumulates all of them into a single output. Each pass produces an independent set of span predictions; because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes \\cite{llm-calibration-survey-2024}, the same span may receive conflicting type labels from different passes, or hallucinated entities may appear in passes where the correct type is absent. Union merge retains all predictions, so false positives scale with $k$ while true positives are bounded by the fixed set of gold entities.\n10\t\n11\tQuantitatively, single-pass precision (0.563) collapses to 0.210--0.249 after union merge across 8 batches, while recall gains are modest (0.368 $\\to$ 0.314 for sdgd\\_zero, 0.446 for random batch). The net F1 loss is substantial: 0.445 $\\to$ 0.251 (sdgd\\_zero) or 0.320 (random batch). Per-type extraction (66 batches) pushes recall to 0.586 but precision to 0.194 (F1\\,=\\,0.292), confirming that the precision--recall imbalance intensifies with the number of passes. Random batching outperforms confusability-aware SDGD on NER (0.320 vs.\\ 0.251) because the dominant degradation source---merge amplification---is independent of batch composition; any multi-pass scheme suffers from it regardless of grouping quality.\n12\t\n13\t\\paragraph{Confidence gating does not resolve merge amplification.}\n14\tConfidence-based gating (retaining only predictions above threshold) trades recall for precision but does not recover net F1 (0.251$\\to$0.241), confirming that merge artifacts are systemic rather than confined to low-confidence predictions (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd}).\n15\t\n16\t\\paragraph{Why NER fails but RE does not.}\n17\tMerge amplification is specific to span-level tasks. Each NER pass independently proposes entity spans, and union merge must resolve conflicting boundaries and type assignments, degrading systematically with pass count. RE avoids this mechanism entirely: under oracle routing, each relation instance receives a single classification from the batch containing its gold type, requiring no span reconciliation and eliminating the multi-pass interference that causes SDGD to fail on NER. This makes RE a clean testbed for evaluating whether confusability-aware decomposition improves accuracy once merge artifacts are removed.\n18\t\n19\t\\enlargethispage*{9\\baselineskip}\n20\t\\paragraph{Oracle routing as performance ceiling.}\n21\tOracle routing routes each test instance to the batch containing its gold type, eliminating cross-type confusion within each batch. On FewRel 80-relation RE, oracle-routed decomposition achieves 0.802 accuracy versus 0.504 for the single-pass baseline---a gain of +29.8\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}). This is the strongest positive finding of the decomposition experiments: it demonstrates that type confusability is indeed a key driver of degradation in RE, and that performance recovers substantially when confusable types are separated.\n22\t\n23\tHowever, oracle routing requires gold labels and is not deployable. The gap between the oracle ceiling and practical routing reveals an important asymmetry: while \\emph{any} decomposition benefits enormously from removing the 80-type bottleneck (+28.4\\,pp for random batching, +29.8\\,pp for graph coloring), the \\emph{additional} benefit of confusability-aware grouping over random grouping is modest (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$). The confusability-aware strategy ranks 13th out of 15 random seeds, limited by the narrow BCS margin (0.006) between graph-coloring and random batches at this partition granularity. Finer-grained partitioning or alternative confusability metrics may be needed to translate the oracle-demonstrated potential into significant practical gains.\n24\t\n25\t\\paragraph{Practical routing strategies.}\n26\tWe evaluate five non-oracle routing strategies on FewRel 80-relation RE using a coarser 5-group decomposition (oracle ceiling: 72.4\\%, +22.0\\,pp; cf.\\ +29.8\\,pp under 17-group decomposition in Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}): max-confidence (all predictions), max-confidence (positive only), none-aware selection, margin-based routing, and random assignment. All five perform below the single-pass baseline (50.4\\%), with the best practical strategy reaching only 36.5\\% accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:routing_strategies}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_routing}). The root cause is constrained decoding: the enum constraint forces the model to produce a confident type prediction for every sub-schema, even when the correct type is absent from the candidate set. As a result, 73.4\\% of instances receive positive predictions from 3--5 sub-schemas simultaneously, and the none-rate per sub-schema is only 20--40\\%. Because every sub-schema produces high-confidence outputs, confidence signals become unreliable and all confidence-based routing fails. Free-form generation or calibrated confidence estimation may provide more reliable routing signals for future work.\n27\t\n28\t\\begin{table}[t]\n29\t\\centering\n30\t\\small\n31\t\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n32\t\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n33\t\\toprule\n34\t\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{Mean BCS} \\\\\n35\t\\midrule\n36\tSingle-pass (80 types) & 0.504 & 0.557 \\\\\n37\tRandom batching (15 seeds)$^\\dagger$ & 0.788\\,$\\pm$\\,0.018 & 0.559 \\\\\n38\tConfus.-aware (graph coloring) & \\textbf{0.802} & 0.552 \\\\\n39\t\\bottomrule\n40\t\\end{tabular}}\n41\t\\caption{Batch composition on FewRel 80-relation RE with oracle routing. $^\\dagger$Mean $\\pm$ SD across 15 random seeds. Graph coloring yields +1.4\\,pp over random batching ($p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) with a narrow BCS margin (0.006). The +29.8\\,pp gap between single-pass and decomposed conditions confirms that type separation recovers performance when merge amplification is absent.}\n42\t\\label{tab:batch_composition}\n43\t\\end{table}\n44\t"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"6e320e88-7eda-4e39-8a1e-1600e66db2ef","timestamp":"2026-05-17T07:59:09.760Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/sdgd_negative.tex","content":"\\section{Schema Decomposition: Failure, Diagnosis, and Mitigation}\n\\label{sec:sdgd}\n\nA natural intervention against confusability-driven degradation is to partition the schema so that confusable types never co-occur in the same prompt. We test this idea with \\textbf{Schema-DAG-Guided Decomposition} (SDGD\\footnote{SDGD (Schema Decomposition and Grouping for Degradation mitigation) is an adopted abbreviation for schema decomposition and grouping; the graph structure is undirected.}), which uses greedy graph coloring on the pairwise SCS graph to assign types to low-confusability batches.\n\nWe construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \\tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\\textsc{largest\\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent---i.e., highly confusable---types share a batch. Each color class forms one extraction batch. For NER, predictions from all batches are merged via set union; for RE, each instance is routed to the batch containing its gold type (oracle routing). We test four NER modes: (i)~\\textbf{single\\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\\textbf{sdgd\\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\\textbf{random\\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\\textbf{per\\_type}, each type extracted in isolation. Every mode falls below the single-pass NER baseline: SDGD obtains F1\\,=\\,0.251, random batching 0.320, per-type 0.292, versus 0.445 for single-pass (Table~\\ref{tab:sdgd_results}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd}).\n\n\\paragraph{Merge amplification in NER.}\nThe core failure mechanism is \\emph{merge amplification}: presenting the same text to the model $k$ times with different type subsets causes each pass to independently generate entity predictions, and union merge accumulates all of them into a single output. Each pass produces an independent set of span predictions; because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes \\cite{llm-calibration-survey-2024}, the same span may receive conflicting type labels from different passes, or hallucinated entities may appear in passes where the correct type is absent. Union merge retains all predictions, so false positives scale with $k$ while true positives are bounded by the fixed set of gold entities.\n\nQuantitatively, single-pass precision (0.563) collapses to 0.210--0.249 after union merge across 8 batches, while recall gains are modest (0.368 $\\to$ 0.314 for sdgd\\_zero, 0.446 for random batch). The net F1 loss is substantial: 0.445 $\\to$ 0.251 (sdgd\\_zero) or 0.320 (random batch). Per-type extraction (66 batches) pushes recall to 0.586 but precision to 0.194 (F1\\,=\\,0.292), confirming that the precision--recall imbalance intensifies with the number of passes. Random batching outperforms confusability-aware SDGD on NER (0.320 vs.\\ 0.251) because the dominant degradation source---merge amplification---is independent of batch composition; any multi-pass scheme suffers from it regardless of grouping quality.\n\n\\paragraph{Confidence gating does not resolve merge amplification.}\nConfidence-based gating (retaining only predictions above threshold) trades recall for precision but does not recover net F1 (0.251$\\to$0.241), confirming that merge artifacts are systemic rather than confined to low-confidence predictions (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd}).\n\n\\paragraph{Why NER fails but RE does not.}\nMerge amplification is specific to span-level tasks. Each NER pass independently proposes entity spans, and union merge must resolve conflicting boundaries and type assignments, degrading systematically with pass count. RE avoids this mechanism entirely: under oracle routing, each relation instance receives a single classification from the batch containing its gold type, requiring no span reconciliation and eliminating the multi-pass interference that causes SDGD to fail on NER. This makes RE a clean testbed for evaluating whether confusability-aware decomposition improves accuracy once merge artifacts are removed.\n\n\\enlargethispage*{9\\baselineskip}\n\\paragraph{Oracle routing as performance ceiling.}\nOracle routing routes each test instance to the batch containing its gold type, eliminating cross-type confusion within each batch. On FewRel 80-relation RE, oracle-routed decomposition achieves 0.802 accuracy versus 0.504 for the single-pass baseline---a gain of +29.8\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}). This is the strongest positive finding of the decomposition experiments: it demonstrates that type confusability is indeed a key driver of degradation in RE, and that performance recovers substantially when confusable types are separated.\n\nHowever, oracle routing requires gold labels and is not deployable. The gap between the oracle ceiling and practical routing reveals an important asymmetry: while \\emph{any} decomposition benefits enormously from removing the 80-type bottleneck (+28.4\\,pp for random batching, +29.8\\,pp for graph coloring), the \\emph{additional} benefit of confusability-aware grouping over random grouping is modest (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$). The confusability-aware strategy ranks 13th out of 15 random seeds, limited by the narrow BCS margin (0.006) between graph-coloring and random batches at this partition granularity. Finer-grained partitioning or alternative confusability metrics may be needed to translate the oracle-demonstrated potential into significant practical gains.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical routing strategies.}\nWe evaluate five non-oracle routing strategies on FewRel 80-relation RE using a coarser 5-group decomposition (oracle ceiling: 72.4\\%, +22.0\\,pp; cf.\\ +29.8\\,pp under 17-group decomposition in Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}): max-confidence (all predictions), max-confidence (positive only), none-aware selection, margin-based routing, and random assignment. All five perform below the single-pass baseline (50.4\\%), with the best practical strategy reaching only 36.5\\% accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:routing_strategies}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_routing}). The root cause is constrained decoding: the enum constraint forces the model to produce a confident type prediction for every sub-schema, even when the correct type is absent from the candidate set. As a result, 73.4\\% of instances receive positive predictions from 3--5 sub-schemas simultaneously, and the none-rate per sub-schema is only 20--40\\%. Because every sub-schema produces high-confidence outputs, confidence signals become unreliable and all confidence-based routing fails. Free-form generation or calibrated confidence estimation may provide more reliable routing signals for future work.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{Mean BCS} \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-pass (80 types) & 0.504 & 0.557 \\\\\nRandom batching (15 seeds)$^\\dagger$ & 0.788\\,$\\pm$\\,0.018 & 0.559 \\\\\nConfus.-aware (graph coloring) & \\textbf{0.802} & 0.552 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Batch composition on FewRel 80-relation RE with oracle routing. $^\\dagger$Mean $\\pm$ SD across 15 random seeds. Graph coloring yields +1.4\\,pp over random batching ($p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) with a narrow BCS margin (0.006). The +29.8\\,pp gap between single-pass and decomposed conditions confirms that type separation recovers performance when merge amplification is absent.}\n\\label{tab:batch_composition}\n\\end{table}\n","numLines":44,"startLine":1,"totalLines":44}}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01J8fLmjVokUzK3s6iyQfy62","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n2\t\n3\t\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n4\t\n5\t\\usepackage{times}\n6\t\\usepackage{latexsym}\n7\t\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n8\t\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n9\t\\usepackage{microtype}\n10\t\\usepackage{graphicx}\n11\t\\usepackage{booktabs}\n12\t\\usepackage{amsmath}\n13\t\\usepackage{enumitem}\n14\t\\usepackage[normalem]{ulem}\n15\t\\usepackage{listings}\n16\t\n17\t\\title{Does Type Confusability Shape Schema-Induced Degradation\\\\in Training-Free LLM Information Extraction?}\n18\t\n19\t\\author{Anonymous}\n20\t\n21\t\\begin{document}\n22\t\\maketitle\n23\t\n24\t\\input{abstract}\n25\t\n26\t\\input{introduction}\n27\t\n28\t\\input{related_work}\n29\t\n30\t\\input{experimental_setup}\n31\t\n32\t\\input{results}\n33\t\n34\t\\input{sdgd_negative}\n35\t\n36\t\\input{discussion}\n37\t\n38\t\\section*{Limitations}\n39\t\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n40\t\\item \\textbf{Model coverage.} Primary experiments use Qwen3-32B with cross-model validation on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. Whether patterns hold for proprietary models (GPT-4, Gemini) remains unverified. Channel~2 was not robustly detectable on NER at 8B scale, likely due to floor effects.\n41\t\n42\t\\item \\textbf{Modest pairwise effect size.} Pairwise SCS explains 1.3--1.7\\% of type confusion variance; entity frequency, prompt position, mention ambiguity, and type definition quality likely account for the remainder.\n43\t\n44\t\\item \\textbf{Factorial interaction tests.} Within-dataset count $\\times$ BCS interaction on Few-NERD did not reach significance ($p = 0.50$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}). However, a FewRel factorial with TOST equivalence testing confirms bounded interaction ($p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp, interaction = 0.47\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}), and cross-pool slope consistency ($p = 0.60$) corroborates stable rather than amplifying sensitivity.\n45\t\n46\t\\item \\textbf{Decomposition and metric scope.} RE batch composition is tested on FewRel only; NER merge alternatives remain unexplored. SCS relies on embedding cosine similarity; label entropy or ontological depth may capture complementary dimensions. For Few-NERD, SCS is computed from the extraction model's own embeddings (Qwen3), creating a shared-representation dependency. MiniLM cross-check yields a conservative but directionally consistent estimate ($r = 0.172$ vs $r = 0.138$; confusion matrix correlation 0.894), suggesting the effect is not an artifact of shared representations.\n47\t\n48\t\\item \\textbf{NER evaluation metric.} Our exact-match NER evaluation conflates boundary errors (partial span overlap) with type errors (correct span, wrong label). Relaxed matching may yield different confusability effect sizes, as boundary ambiguity may correlate with schema complexity independently of type similarity.\n49\t\n50\t\\item \\textbf{RE format specificity.} RE's clean channel separation may reflect its closed-set classification format. FewRel combines training and validation splits for 80 types and characterizes classification accuracy rather than end-to-end extraction.\n51\t\n52\t\\item \\textbf{Causal mechanism.} The study establishes correlational evidence with controlled stratification; mechanistic analysis at the attention or logit level is needed to confirm the causal pathway. The controlled stratification provides evidence consistent with a directional relationship but does not establish interventional causality.\n53\t\n54\t\\item \\textbf{Data contamination.} FewRel (2018) may appear in pretraining corpora. However, train-origin relations show numerically lower accuracy than val-origin ($-8.8$\\,pp, $p=0.06$, n.s.), opposite to the contamination hypothesis. Moreover, the monotonic count-dependent degradation curve (85.7\\%$\\to$50.4\\%) cannot be explained by uniform contamination, which would elevate all conditions equally. BCS slope consistency across independent and within-pool conditions (permutation $p = 0.60$) provides a robustness check that is difficult to reconcile with non-uniform contamination, which would differentially affect specific type subsets. DocRED (2019) provides a lower-contamination-risk replication with consistent BCS--accuracy slopes ($R^2 = 0.53$). Adding train-origin ratio as a covariate leaves the BCS partial effect unchanged ($p = 0.245$ for origin; Appendix~\\ref{app:split-origin}).\n55\t\\end{enumerate}\n56\t\n57\t\\paragraph{Multiple comparisons.} Following the argument that independent replications do not require family-wise correction \\citep{rothman-1990}, while acknowledging the opposing view \\citep{perneger-1998}, we treat each dataset as a separate test of the same hypothesis. Within-dataset secondary analyses are reported with individual $p$-values; core claims rest on effects reaching $p < 0.001$.\n58\t\n59\t\\input{checklist}\n60\t\n61\t\\bibliography{references}\n62\t\n63\t\\appendix\n64\t\n65\t\\section{Additional Figures}\n66\t\\label{sec:appendix_figures}\n67\t\n68\t\\begin{table}[h]\n69\t\\centering\n70\t\\small\n71\t\\begin{tabular}{lrr}\n72\t\\toprule\n73\t\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Llama-3.1-8B} & \\textbf{Qwen3-32B} \\\\\n74\t\\midrule\n75\t1 & 0.702 & 0.640 \\\\\n76\t64 & 0.273 & 0.304 \\\\\n77\t\\midrule\n78\tDecline & $-$43\\,pp & $-$34\\,pp \\\\\n79\t\\bottomrule\n80\t\\end{tabular}\n81\t\\caption{Cross-model count-curve replication on Few-NERD (text-match recall). Both models show a general declining trend in recall with schema size. Per-batch correlation $r = 0.725$. Qwen3-32B values use the v2 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}) for fair cross-model comparison; the count-curve results in \\S\\ref{sec:count_curves} use the v1 prompt, yielding higher absolute recall (0.72 vs.\\ 0.64 at count${}\\,=\\,1$) due to over-prediction under v1.}\n82\t\\label{tab:cross_model}\n83\t\\end{table}\n84\t\n85\t\\begin{table}[h]\n86\t\\centering\n87\t\\small\n88\t\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n89\t\\begin{tabular}{crrrrrr}\n90\t\\toprule\n91\t& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Llama-3.1-8B}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Qwen3-32B}} \\\\\n92\t\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\n93\t\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Low} & \\textbf{High} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} & \\textbf{Low} & \\textbf{High} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n94\t\\midrule\n95\t8 & 78.1\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$27.1 & 82.3\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$31.2 \\\\\n96\t16 & 75.5\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$23.9 & 82.8\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$31.2 \\\\\n97\t\\bottomrule\n98\t\\end{tabular}}\n99\t\\caption{Cross-model BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy.  High-BCS accuracy converges to $\\approx$51\\% regardless of model, while low-BCS accuracy reflects model capacity differences.  The confusability gap ($\\Delta$, in pp) is 77--87\\% as large on Llama-3.1-8B as on Qwen3-32B.}\n100\t\\label{tab:cross_model_bcs}\n101\t\\end{table}\n102\t\n103\t\\begin{table}[h]\n104\t\\centering\n105\t\\small\n106\t\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n107\t\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr}\n108\t\\toprule\n109\t & & \\multicolumn{5}{c}{\\textbf{BCS Bin}} & & \\\\\n110\t\\cmidrule(lr){3-7}\n111\t\\textbf{Task} & \\textbf{Error} & \\textbf{Solo} & \\textbf{Low} & \\textbf{Mid} & \\textbf{High} & \\textbf{V.High} & \\textbf{$\\rho$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n112\t\\midrule\n113\tNER & IntraSub & 0\\% & 1.6\\% & 10.6\\% & 49.4\\% & --- & 1.000 & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\n114\tRE  & IntraSub & 0\\% & 4.2\\% & 13.0\\% & 20.3\\% & 32.3\\% & 0.954 & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n115\t\\midrule\n116\tNER & Missed & --- & 42.8\\% & 64.8\\% & 49.9\\% & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n117\tRE  & Missed & --- & 10.4\\% & 11.3\\% & 13.1\\% & 15.7\\% & --- & --- \\\\\n118\t\\bottomrule\n119\t\\end{tabular}}\n120\t\\caption{Intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) and missed-entity rates by BCS bin, with Spearman $\\rho$ computed over $n=154$ individual batches (RE) and $n=148$ batches (NER). NER has 4 BCS bins; RE has 5.}\n121\t\\label{tab:error_taxonomy}\n122\t\\end{table}\n123\t\n124\t\\section{SDGD Full Protocol and Results}\n125\t\\label{sec:appendix_sdgd}\n126\t\n127\tWe construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \\tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\\textsc{largest\\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent---i.e., highly confusable---types share a color. Each color class forms one extraction batch, and predictions are merged via set union. We compare four modes: (i)~\\textbf{single\\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\\textbf{sdgd\\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\\textbf{random\\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\\textbf{per\\_type}, the extreme case where each type is extracted in isolation.\n128\t\n129\tSingle-pass extraction achieves F1\\,=\\,0.445 (P\\,=\\,0.563, R\\,=\\,0.368). SDGD obtains F1\\,=\\,0.251 (P\\,=\\,0.210, R\\,=\\,0.314), a 44\\% relative drop. Random batching reaches F1\\,=\\,0.320 (P\\,=\\,0.249, R\\,=\\,0.446), and per-type extraction yields F1\\,=\\,0.292 (P\\,=\\,0.194, R\\,=\\,0.586). Every decomposition mode falls below the single-pass baseline (Table~\\ref{tab:sdgd_results}).\n130\t\n131\t\\begin{table}[h]\n132\t\\centering\n133\t\\small\n134\t\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n135\t\\begin{tabular}{lrccc}\n136\t\\toprule\n137\t\\textbf{Mode} & \\textbf{\\# Batches} & \\textbf{Precision} & \\textbf{Recall} & \\textbf{F1} \\\\\n138\t\\midrule\n139\tsingle\\_pass & 1 & 0.563 & 0.368 & 0.445 \\\\\n140\t\\midrule\n141\tsdgd\\_zero (v1) & 8 & 0.210 & 0.314 & 0.251 \\\\\n142\trandom\\_batch (v1) & 8 & 0.249 & 0.446 & 0.320 \\\\\n143\tper\\_type & 66 & 0.194 & 0.586 & 0.292 \\\\\n144\t\\midrule\n145\tsdgd\\_zero (v2) & 4 & 0.324 & 0.191 & 0.241 \\\\\n146\trandom\\_batch (v2) & 8 & 0.331 & 0.276 & 0.301 \\\\\n147\t\\bottomrule\n148\t\\end{tabular}}\n149\t\\caption{Few-NERD extraction under decomposition modes. V1 uses union merge; v2 uses confidence-gated merge with \\texttt{min\\_batch\\_size=8} (reducing 8$\\to$4 batches for sdgd\\_zero). All decomposition modes produce lower F1 than the single-pass baseline. All modes use the v1 prompt.}\n150\t\\label{tab:sdgd_results}\n151\t\\end{table}\n152\t\n153\tDecomposition boosts recall (per-type recall reaches 0.586 versus single-pass 0.368) but devastates precision (0.194 versus 0.563). When the same text is presented to the model $k$ times with different type subsets, each pass independently generates entity predictions. Because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes, the union merge step accumulates overlapping and contradictory predictions, inflating false positives.\n154\t\n155\tOur v2 merge ranks competing predictions by the LLM's self-reported confidence and retains only the highest-scoring candidate for each span. Confidence gating improves precision (sdgd\\_zero: 0.210$\\to$0.324; random\\_batch: 0.249$\\to$0.331), but at the cost of disproportionate recall loss (0.314$\\to$0.191; 0.446$\\to$0.276), yielding lower F1 in both modes (sdgd\\_zero: 0.251$\\to$0.241; random\\_batch: 0.320$\\to$0.301).\n156\t\n157\t\\subsection{Practical Routing Strategies on RE}\n158\t\\label{sec:appendix_routing}\n159\t\n160\tWe evaluate five non-oracle routing strategies on FewRel 80-relation RE using graph-coloring sub-schema decomposition (same grouping as Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}). Each strategy selects a single sub-schema for each test instance and returns that sub-schema's prediction.\n161\t\n162\t\\begin{table}[h]\n163\t\\centering\n164\t\\small\n165\t\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n166\t\\toprule\n167\t\\textbf{Strategy} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ Single-Pass} \\\\\n168\t\\midrule\n169\tOracle (gold label) & 72.4\\% & +22.0\\,pp \\\\\n170\t\\midrule\n171\tMax-conf (positive only) & 36.5\\% & $-$14.0\\,pp \\\\\n172\tNone-aware & 36.5\\% & $-$14.0\\,pp \\\\\n173\tMargin (positive only) & 35.9\\% & $-$14.5\\,pp \\\\\n174\tMax-conf (all) & 27.7\\% & $-$22.7\\,pp \\\\\n175\tRandom & 14.7\\% & $-$35.7\\,pp \\\\\n176\t\\midrule\n177\tSingle-pass (no decomposition) & 50.4\\% & --- \\\\\n178\t\\bottomrule\n179\t\\end{tabular}\n180\t\\caption{Routing strategy comparison on FewRel 80-relation RE with graph-coloring sub-schema decomposition. Oracle requires gold labels. All practical (non-oracle) strategies perform below the single-pass baseline. The failure stems from constrained decoding (enum constraint), which forces confident predictions in every sub-schema: 73.4\\% of instances receive positive predictions from 3--5 sub-schemas simultaneously, with per-sub-schema none-rates of only 20--40\\%.}\n181\t\\label{tab:routing_strategies}\n182\t\\end{table}\n183\t\n184\t\\section{Factorial Analysis and Separability Test}\n185\t\\label{sec:appendix_factorial}\n186\t\n187\tTo formally test whether count and confusability effects are additive, we fit a multiple regression (accuracy $\\sim$ $\\log_2$\\,count $+$ BCS $+$ count$\\times$BCS) across 69 batches from FewRel spanning five count levels ($k \\in \\{4, 8, 16, 32, 40\\}$). Both main effects are significant (count: $p = 0.008$; BCS: $p = 0.0002$; $R^2 = 0.52$), but the interaction term is negligible (0.47\\,pp). A TOST equivalence test with a $\\pm 3$\\,pp margin confirms that the interaction is bounded well below practical significance ($p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.003$), supporting bounded interaction between count and confusability effects.\n188\t\n189\tA separate within-dataset $2 \\times 2$ factorial on FewRel (count $\\in \\{8, 16\\}$, BCS low/high; $n = 54$) did not reach equivalence ($p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.56$), likely due to limited power from fewer count levels and narrower BCS contrast. The Few-NERD factorial (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}) similarly did not reach significance ($p = 0.50$). The regression approach provides the stronger test by leveraging the full count range.\n190\t\n191\t\\paragraph{Equivalence margin justification.}\n192\tWe set $\\pm 3$\\,pp as the smallest interaction that would materially alter practitioner decisions: a 3\\,pp interaction atop the 2.2\\,pp confusability main effect would represent ${>}130\\%$ amplification, fundamentally changing whether confusability warrants mitigation. At $\\pm 2$\\,pp (marginally equivalent, $p = 0.047$), interaction remains bounded below the main effect itself.\n193\t\n194\t\\begin{table}[h]\n195\t\\centering\n196\t\\small\n197\t\\begin{tabular}{crrl}\n198\t\\toprule\n199\t\\textbf{Margin (pp)} & \\textbf{$t_{\\text{upper}}$} & \\textbf{$p_{\\text{TOST}}$} & \\textbf{Conclusion} \\\\\n200\t\\midrule\n201\t$\\pm 1$ & $-0.59$ & $0.278$ & Inconclusive \\\\\n202\t$\\pm 2$ & $-1.70$ & $0.047$ & Equivalent \\\\\n203\t$\\pm 3$ & $-2.81$ & $0.003$ & Equivalent \\\\\n204\t$\\pm 5$ & $-5.02$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ & Equivalent \\\\\n205\t\\bottomrule\n206\t\\end{tabular}\n207\t\\caption{TOST sensitivity analysis across equivalence margins. The interaction effect (0.47\\,pp, 90\\% CI [$-$1.0, 2.0]\\,pp) is bounded below 2\\,pp at marginal significance ($p = 0.047$), confirming that the $\\pm 3$\\,pp choice does not drive the equivalence conclusion. At $\\pm 1$\\,pp, equivalence is inconclusive ($p = 0.278$), meaning interaction could reach approximately half the main effect magnitude.}\n208\t\\label{tab:tost_sensitivity}\n209\t\\end{table}\n210\t\n211\t\\section{Within-Pool CI Details}\n212\t\\label{sec:appendix_within_pool}\n213\t\n214\tThe quartile $\\Delta$ for 80-pool 16-relation subsets ($-$3.92\\,pp, 95\\% CI [$-$8.17, $+$0.38]) crosses zero, reflecting the compressed BCS range within that pool (0.46--0.68 vs.\\ 0.48--0.71 for the independent pool). Within this narrow band, the quartile contrast lacks statistical power to detect the effect that the regression identifies. The full 80-relation condition ($n = 25$) is underpowered for both the slope (CI: [$-$1.303, $+$1.695]) and quartile analyses; we include it in Table~\\ref{tab:within_pool} for completeness but do not draw inferential conclusions from it.\n215\t\n216\t\\section{Controlling for Centrality Confounds in MAVEN Per-Type Analysis}\n217\t\\label{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}\n218\t\n219\tSection~\\ref{sec:centrality_confound} identifies a centrality confound in MAVEN's per-type BCS--F1 correlation: both embeddings show a \\emph{positive} raw correlation, opposite to the expected direction. Here we formalize this analysis by identifying specific confounders for each embedding and testing whether the BCS--F1 relationship survives partial correlation controlling for these confounders.\n220\t\n221\t\\paragraph{Confound identification.}\n222\tFor each embedding, we computed the correlation between mean BCS and candidate confounders (type frequency, degree centrality at threshold 0.7) across MAVEN's 149 active event types. The dominant confound differs by embedding:\n223\t\\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt,leftmargin=*]\n224\t\\item \\textbf{MiniLM}: BCS correlates with log type frequency ($r = 0.201$). Frequent types have more training signal and are extracted more reliably, producing the positive BCS--F1 association.\n225\t\\item \\textbf{Qwen3}: BCS correlates strongly with degree centrality ($r = 0.854$). In the Qwen3 embedding space, high-BCS types are those with many semantic neighbors---central, well-represented types that the model extracts well.\n226\t\\end{itemize}\n227\t\n228\t\\paragraph{Partial correlation results.}\n229\tTable~\\ref{tab:residualized_bcs} reports raw Spearman $\\rho$ and partial Pearson $r$ after controlling for the identified confounders. Under single-variable control, both correlations weaken to non-significance. Under joint control (log frequency $+$ degree centrality simultaneously), neither embedding retains a significant BCS--F1 association.\n230\t\n231\t\\begin{table}[h]\n232\t\\centering\n233\t\\small\n234\t\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n235\t\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n236\t\\toprule\n237\t\\textbf{Embedding} & \\textbf{Control} & \\textbf{Raw $\\rho$} & \\textbf{Raw $p$} & \\textbf{Partial $r$} & \\textbf{Partial $p$} \\\\\n238\t\\midrule\n239\tMiniLM & --- & $+$0.208 & $0.011$ & --- & --- \\\\\n240\tMiniLM & log freq & --- & --- & $+$0.133 & $0.11$ \\\\\n241\tMiniLM & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.23$ \\\\\n242\t\\midrule\n243\tQwen3 & --- & $+$0.184 & $0.025$ & --- & --- \\\\\n244\tQwen3 & degree centrality & --- & --- & $+$0.063 & $0.44$ \\\\\n245\tQwen3 & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.44$ \\\\\n246\t\\bottomrule\n247\t\\end{tabular}}\n248\t\\caption{MAVEN per-type BCS--F1 correlation before and after controlling for centrality confounders. Raw correlations are Spearman $\\rho$; partial correlations are Pearson $r$ from residualized regression. Degree centrality is computed on the SCS graph at threshold 0.7. All values from experiment \\texttt{exp\\_maven\\_residualized\\_bcs}.}\n249\t\\label{tab:residualized_bcs}\n250\t\\end{table}\n251\t\n252\t\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\n253\tThe two embeddings encode different aspects of the same underlying confound. MiniLM, a smaller sentence encoder, produces a tighter embedding cluster for MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types; here, type frequency is the primary confounder because frequent types accumulate higher mean similarity to the dense cluster. Qwen3, with a larger representation space, differentiates types enough that frequency is not the dominant factor, but degree centrality---the count of strong semantic neighbors---becomes the confound instead. In both cases, the positive raw correlation reflects a centrality artifact: types that are semantically central are both high-BCS and easy to extract.\n254\t\n255\tUnder joint control, both embeddings yield $p > 0.2$, confirming that the raw positive BCS--F1 correlation on MAVEN is fully attributable to centrality-related confounders. This does not invalidate the confusability hypothesis; the pairwise SCS--confusion correlation on MAVEN remains significant (MiniLM $r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}). Rather, it demonstrates that per-type aggregation (mean BCS) conflates confusability with centrality in semantically homogeneous schemas, and that pairwise analysis or explicit confound adjustment is required in such settings.\n256\t\n257\t\\section{Batch Composition Results}\n258\t\\label{sec:appendix_batch_composition}\n259\t\n260\tSee Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition} and the accompanying discussion in \\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}.\n261\t\n262\t\\input{prompt_appendix}\n263\t\n264\t\\section{Prompt Version Sensitivity Analysis}\n265\t\\label{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n266\t\n267\tWe assess whether the confusability effects reported in \\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}--\\ref{sec:multi_channel} are robust to prompt design by replicating key experiments under the v2 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).\n268\t\n269\t\\paragraph{RE (FewRel).}\n270\tUnder the v1 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap (Table~\\ref{tab:bcs_controlled}) is substantial. Under v2, with $n{=}25$ batches per group and wider BCS contrast (low-BCS range [0.356, 0.367]; high-BCS range [0.791, 0.811]), the gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; 95\\% CI: [22.4, 28.6]; $p < 0.001$; Pearson $r = -0.919$). The effect is significant, directionally consistent with v1, and only modestly attenuated in magnitude ($-$5.7\\,pp reduction).
