To control for this, we draw 100 random 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool and evaluate under the same BCS-controlled protocol.\n317\t\n318\t\\begin{table}[h]\n319\t\\centering\n320\t\\small\n321\t\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n322\t\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrr}\n323\t\\toprule\n324\t& \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textbf{Regression (BCS $\\to$ Accuracy)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{Quartile $\\Delta$}} \\\\\n325\t\\cmidrule(lr){2-5} \\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\n326\t\\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Slope} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n327\t\\midrule\n328\tIndependent 16-rel ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}\\,0.001$ & $-$5.31 & [$-$7.44, $-$3.19] \\\\\n329\t80-pool $\\to$ 16-sub ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & $0.027$ & $-$3.92 & [$-$8.17, $+$0.38] \\\\\n330\tFull 80-rel ($n{=}25$)$^\\dagger$ & $+$0.221 & [$-$1.303, $+$1.695] & $+$0.064 & $0.76$ & $-$1.04 & [$-$10.12, $+$8.04] \\\\\n331\t\\midrule\n332\t\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize Permutation test (16-rel vs.\\ 80-pool slopes): $p \\approx 0.60$ (5{,}000 permutations)} \\\\\n333\t\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$BCS range (IQR\\,=\\,0.033) insufficient for regression; slope not interpretable.} \\\\\n334\t\\bottomrule\n335\t\\end{tabular}}\n336\t\\caption{Three-way BCS--accuracy analysis on FewRel. Slope: OLS of batch accuracy on BCS (bootstrap CIs, $B{=}10{,}000$). $\\Delta$: accuracy gap between top- and bottom-quartile BCS batches. See Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool} for CI interpretation.}\n337\t\\label{tab:within_pool}\n338\t\\end{table}\n339\t\n340\tThe within-pool slope ($-$0.401, CI excludes zero) is statistically indistinguishable from the independent 16-relation pool ($-$0.509; permutation $p \\approx 0.60$). The smaller quartile gap ($-$3.92\\,pp vs.\\ $-$5.31\\,pp) reflects the narrower BCS range within the 80-relation pool (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool}).\n341\t\n342\tIn the full 80-relation condition, natural BCS variation is insufficient (IQR${}=0.033$) to yield a detectable effect ($n=25$, slope${}=+0.221$, $p \\approx 0.76$). This does not invalidate the confusability effect---the within-pool slope is significant ($p=0.027$) when BCS is deliberately varied---but establishes that natural schema sampling at this scale does not produce sufficient BCS contrast for detection.\n343\t\n344\t\\section{SCS Embedding Ablation: Name-Only vs. Name+Definition}\n345\t\\label{app:scs-ablation}\n346\t\n347\tTable~\\ref{tab:scs-ablation} compares predictive accuracy of name-only SCS against name+definition SCS across three datasets.\n348\t\n349\t\\begin{table}[h]\n350\t\\centering\n351\t\\small\n352\t\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n353\t\\toprule\n354\tDataset & SCS$_\\text{name}$ $r$ & SCS$_\\text{def}$ $r$ & $\\Delta r$ & Matrix corr. \\\\\n355\t\\midrule\n356\tFew-NERD & 0.172 & 0.149 & $-$0.023 & 0.894 \\\\\n357\tMAVEN & 0.129 & 0.164 & +0.035 & 0.656 \\\\\n358\tFewRel & 0.178 & 0.193 & +0.015 & 0.848 \\\\\n359\t\\bottomrule\n360\t\\end{tabular}\n361\t\\caption{Pearson correlation between SCS and empirical confusion rate. ``Def'' concatenates type name with definition before embedding. All correlations significant ($p < 0.001$). The inconsistent direction of $\\Delta r$ indicates no systematic benefit from including definitions.}\n362\t\\label{tab:scs-ablation}\n363\t\\end{table}\n364\t\n365\tFew-NERD is the only dataset whose prompts include type definitions; for MAVEN and FewRel, prompts use type names alone, making name-only SCS a direct match to model input. The high matrix correlation (0.66--0.89) confirms that type names capture the dominant semantic structure.\n366\t\n367\t\\section{Pair-to-Batch Aggregation Simulation}\n368\t\\label{app:aggregation-sim}\n369\t\n370\tThe following simulation provides an illustrative explanatory model for how pair-level effects aggregate to batch-level variance. It is not the evidence base for our claims---the observed batch-level $R^2 = 0.218$ (\\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}) is a direct empirical measurement independent of this simulation.\n371\t\n372\t\\paragraph{Setup.} We examine whether the pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ (computed over non-zero confusion pairs) can aggregate to the observed batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ via a Monte Carlo simulation. From the FewRel 16-relation experiment, we estimate the inter-pair SCS correlation for pairs sharing a common type ($\\rho_\\text{SCS} = 0.35$). For batches of $k=8$ types ($\\binom{8}{2}=28$ pairs), we simulate 10{,}000 iterations: (1) generate 28 correlated pair-level effects with individual $R^2 = 0.074$; (2) average to obtain batch-level signals; (3) compute batch-level $R^2$.\n373\t\n374\t\\paragraph{Results.} Simulated batch $R^2 = 0.219$ [95\\% CI: 0.092--0.361], matching the observed value of 0.218. The effective amplification factor is $\\sim$3$\\times$, explained by signal correlation within batches preserving variance while independent noise averages out (effective degrees of freedom $\\approx 3.4$, not 28).\n375\t\n376\t\\paragraph{Unconditional baseline.}\n377\tWhen the simulation uses the unconditional pairwise $r^2 = 0.013$ (computed over all type pairs including zero-confusion pairs; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}), the simulated batch-level $R^2$ drops to $0.044$ [95\\% CI: 0.001--0.151]---well below the observed $R^2 = 0.218$. The analytical formula confirms: $\\text{SNR} \\times \\text{EF} / (\\text{SNR} \\times \\text{EF} + 1) = 0.042$ with the same effective factor $\\text{EF} = 3.35$. This ${\\sim}5\\times$ gap reflects the dilution from zero-confusion pairs: approximately 89\\% of type pairs never exhibit mutual confusion, contributing only noise to the unconditional correlation. The conditional model ($R^2 = 0.074 \\to 0.219$) matches the observed value because it isolates the minority of semantically proximate pairs that actually compete during type resolution; BCS predicts batch accuracy through the density of \\emph{active} confusable pairs, not uniform contributions from all pairs.\n378\t\n379\t\\section{Split-Origin Covariate Analysis}\n380\t\\label{app:split-origin}\n381\t\n382\tFewRel combines 64 train-split and 16 val-split relation types into a single 80-type pool.  Train-origin types show numerically lower accuracy ($-8.8$\\,pp, $p = 0.06$; see Limitations), raising the question of whether differential pre-training exposure confounds the BCS--accuracy relationship.  We test this by adding a per-batch \\emph{train-origin ratio} (fraction of relation types drawn from the train split) as a covariate in the BCS--accuracy regression.\n383\t\n384\t\\paragraph{Method.}  Across 213 multi-type batches ($k \\geq 2$) from the batch-composition experiment (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}) and the factorial design (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}), we compute $\\texttt{train\\_origin\\_ratio} = n_{\\text{train}} / k$ for each batch.  The ratio ranges from 0.40 to 1.00 (mean 0.80, reflecting the 64/80 base rate).  We fit:\n385\t\\[\n386\t\\text{accuracy} = \\beta_0 + \\beta_{\\text{BCS}} \\cdot \\text{BCS} + \\beta_{\\text{origin}} \\cdot \\text{train\\_origin\\_ratio} + \\varepsilon\n387\t\\]\n388\t\n389\t\\paragraph{Results.}  The BCS partial effect is unchanged after controlling for split-origin: $\\beta_{\\text{BCS}} = -0.416$, $p < 10^{-16}$ (vs.\\ $\\beta = -0.412$, $p < 10^{-16}$ without the covariate).  Train-origin ratio is not independently significant ($\\beta_{\\text{origin}} = -0.057$, $p = 0.245$), and $\\Delta R^2 = 0.005$.  The BCS--accuracy relationship is therefore not confounded by differential pre-training exposure at the batch level.\n390\t\n391\t\\section{Cross-Domain Connections}\n392\t\\label{app:cross-domain}\n393\t\n394\tThe schema-induced degradation documented here is not unique to information extraction. Schema-Guided Dialogue systems (SGD/SGD-X; \\citealt{rastogi2020towards}) face an analogous challenge: as the number of intent and slot types grows, dialogue state tracking accuracy declines. \\citet{li2023evaluating} observed count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT-based IE, attributing it to prompt overload. Our two-channel decomposition offers a more refined explanation: schema size per se is not the sole driver; rather, larger schemas increase the probability of including high-SCS pairs, which contribute disproportionately to confusion. This distinction has practical implications---a schema of 80 types with low mutual confusability may outperform a 40-type schema with clustered semantics---and suggests that confusability-aware design principles may generalize to any task where an LLM must discriminate among a prompt-specified label inventory.\n395\t\n396\t\\section{Additional Experimental Details}\n397\t\\label{sec:appendix_additional_details}\n398\t\n399\t\\subsection{Cross-Seed Variance Decomposition}\n400\t\\label{app:variance-decomposition}\n401\t\n402\tTo quantify whether performance variability is driven by random batch composition or seed-dependent model inference noise, we decompose total variance into intra-seed (batch) and inter-seed components across three seeds (42, 123, 456), each evaluated on 25 random batches per count level.\n403\t\n404\t\\begin{table}[h]\n405\t\\centering\n406\t\\small\n407\t\\begin{tabular}{rrrr}\n408\t\\toprule\n409\t\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Batch SD} & \\textbf{Seed SD} & \\textbf{Ratio} \\\\\n410\t\\midrule\n411\t4 & 0.0889 & 0.0122 & 53.5 \\\\\n412\t8 & 0.0820 & 0.0098 & 70.0 \\\\\n413\t16 & 0.0626 & 0.0270 & 5.4 \\\\\n414\t32 & 0.0462 & 0.0091 & 26.0 \\\\\n415\t40 & 0.0421 & 0.0019 & 474.4 \\\\\n416\t64 & 0.0245 & 0.0022 & 119.8 \\\\\n417\t80 & 0.0001 & 0.0088 & $<$0.1 \\\\\n418\t\\bottomrule\n419\t\\end{tabular}\n420\t\\caption{Variance decomposition across count levels on FewRel. Batch SD is the root-mean intra-seed variance across 25 random type batches (averaged over 3 seeds). Seed SD is the standard deviation of the 3 per-seed means. Ratio = mean batch variance / seed variance. At count 80, only one batch composition exists (the full schema), so batch variance vanishes by construction.}\n421\t\\label{tab:variance_decomposition}\n422\t\\end{table}\n423\t\n424\t\\begin{table}[h]\n425\t\\centering\n426\t\\small\n427\t\\begin{tabular}{rrrr}\n428\t\\toprule\n429\t\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Batch SD} & \\textbf{Seed SD} & \\textbf{Ratio} \\\\\n430\t\\midrule\n431\t2 & 0.1782 & 0.0557 & 10.2 \\\\\n432\t6 & 0.1233 & 0.0160 & 59.2 \\\\\n433\t12 & 0.1063 & 0.0297 & 12.8 \\\\\n434\t22 & 0.0954 & 0.0096 & 99.2 \\\\\n435\t44 & 0.1214 & 0.0143 & 72.4 \\\\\n436\t66 & 0.0016 & 0.0002 & 46.3 \\\\\n437\t\\bottomrule\n438\t\\end{tabular}\n439\t\\caption{Variance decomposition on Few-NERD (3 seeds $\\times$ 25 batches per count level). Batch SD: root-mean intra-seed standard deviation averaged over seeds. Seed SD: standard deviation of per-seed means. Ratio = batch variance / seed variance. At count 66 (full schema), only one composition exists, so both components collapse to near-zero.}\n440\t\\label{tab:variance_decomposition_ner}\n441\t\\end{table}\n442\t\n443\tIntra-batch variance dominates inter-seed variance by 5--474$\\times$ on FewRel (mean 125$\\times$, counts 4--64) and 10--99$\\times$ on Few-NERD (mean 51$\\times$, counts 2--44), confirming that random batch composition is the primary source of performance variability on both tasks, not seed-dependent randomness in model inference. The wide ratio range reflects variation in how tightly seed means cluster at each count level; even the minimum ratio (5.4$\\times$ on FewRel at count 16, 10.2$\\times$ on Few-NERD at count 2) indicates batch composition contributes the majority of observed variance. At count 80 (FewRel) and count 66 (Few-NERD), the full schema admits only one batch composition, collapsing batch variance by construction.\n444\t\n445\t\\subsection{Per-Count-Level added.]\n462\t\n463\t\\bibliography{references}\n464\t\n465\t\\end{document}\n466\t"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"84c01779-ceaf-4786-b27c-1d81e50c6201","timestamp":"2026-05-17T07:59:11.196Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/supplementary.tex","content":"\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{enumitem}\n\\usepackage[normalem]{ulem}\n\\usepackage{listings}\n\n\\title{Supplementary Figures}\n\\label{sec:appendix_figures}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Llama-3.1-8B} & \\textbf{Qwen3-32B} \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & 0.702 & 0.640 \\\\\n64 & 0.273 & 0.304 \\\\\n\\midrule\nDecline & $-$43\\,pp & $-$34\\,pp \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cross-model count-curve replication on Few-NERD (text-match recall). Both models show a general declining trend in recall with schema size. Per-batch correlation $r = 0.725$. Qwen3-32B values use the v2 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}) for fair cross-model comparison; the count-curve results in \\S\\ref{sec:count_curves} use the v1 prompt, yielding higher absolute recall (0.72 vs.\\ 0.64 at count${}\\,=\\,1$) due to over-prediction under v1.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{crrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Llama-3.1-8B}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Qwen3-32B}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\n\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Low} & \\textbf{High} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} & \\textbf{Low} & \\textbf{High} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n8 & 78.1\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$27.1 & 82.3\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$31.2 \\\\\n16 & 75.5\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$23.9 & 82.8\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$31.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Cross-model BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy.  High-BCS accuracy converges to $\\approx$51\\% regardless of model, while low-BCS accuracy reflects model capacity differences.  The confusability gap ($\\Delta$, in pp) is 77--87\\% as large on Llama-3.1-8B as on Qwen3-32B.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model_bcs}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n & & \\multicolumn{5}{c}{\\textbf{BCS Bin}} & & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-7}\n\\textbf{Task} & \\textbf{Error} & \\textbf{Solo} & \\textbf{Low} & \\textbf{Mid} & \\textbf{High} & \\textbf{V.High} & \\textbf{$\\rho$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nNER & IntraSub & 0\\% & 1.6\\% & 10.6\\% & 49.4\\% & --- & 1.000 & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\nRE  & IntraSub & 0\\% & 4.2\\% & 13.0\\% & 20.3\\% & 32.3\\% & 0.954 & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nNER & Missed & --- & 42.8\\% & 64.8\\% & 49.9\\% & --- & --- & --- \\\\\nRE  & Missed & --- & 10.4\\% & 11.3\\% & 13.1\\% & 15.7\\% & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) and missed-entity rates by BCS bin, with Spearman $\\rho$ computed over $n=154$ individual batches (RE) and $n=148$ batches (NER). NER has 4 BCS bins; RE has 5.}\n\\label{tab:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{SDGD Full Protocol and Results}\n\\label{sec:appendix_sdgd}\n\nWe construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \\tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\\textsc{largest\\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent---i.e., highly confusable---types share a color. Each color class forms one extraction batch, and predictions are merged via set union. We compare four modes: (i)~\\textbf{single\\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\\textbf{sdgd\\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\\textbf{random\\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\\textbf{per\\_type}, the extreme case where each type is extracted in isolation.\n\nSingle-pass extraction achieves F1\\,=\\,0.445 (P\\,=\\,0.563, R\\,=\\,0.368). SDGD obtains F1\\,=\\,0.251 (P\\,=\\,0.210, R\\,=\\,0.314), a 44\\% relative drop. Random batching reaches F1\\,=\\,0.320 (P\\,=\\,0.249, R\\,=\\,0.446), and per-type extraction yields F1\\,=\\,0.292 (P\\,=\\,0.194, R\\,=\\,0.586). Every decomposition mode falls below the single-pass baseline (Table~\\ref{tab:sdgd_results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Mode} & \\textbf{\\# Batches} & \\textbf{Precision} & \\textbf{Recall} & \\textbf{F1} \\\\\n\\midrule\nsingle\\_pass & 1 & 0.563 & 0.368 & 0.445 \\\\\n\\midrule\nsdgd\\_zero (v1) & 8 & 0.210 & 0.314 & 0.251 \\\\\nrandom\\_batch (v1) & 8 & 0.249 & 0.446 & 0.320 \\\\\nper\\_type & 66 & 0.194 & 0.586 & 0.292 \\\\\n\\midrule\nsdgd\\_zero (v2) & 4 & 0.324 & 0.191 & 0.241 \\\\\nrandom\\_batch (v2) & 8 & 0.331 & 0.276 & 0.301 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Few-NERD extraction under decomposition modes. V1 uses union merge; v2 uses confidence-gated merge with \\texttt{min\\_batch\\_size=8} (reducing 8$\\to$4 batches for sdgd\\_zero). All decomposition modes produce lower F1 than the single-pass baseline. All modes use the v1 prompt.}\n\\label{tab:sdgd_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nDecomposition boosts recall (per-type recall reaches 0.586 versus single-pass 0.368) but devastates precision (0.194 versus 0.563). When the same text is presented to the model $k$ times with different type subsets, each pass independently generates entity predictions. Because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes, the union merge step accumulates overlapping and contradictory predictions, inflating false positives.\n\nOur v2 merge ranks competing predictions by the LLM's self-reported confidence and retains only the highest-scoring candidate for each span. Confidence gating improves precision (sdgd\\_zero: 0.210$\\to$0.324; random\\_batch: 0.249$\\to$0.331), but at the cost of disproportionate recall loss (0.314$\\to$0.191; 0.446$\\to$0.276), yielding lower F1 in both modes (sdgd\\_zero: 0.251$\\to$0.241; random\\_batch: 0.320$\\to$0.301).\n\n\\subsection{Practical Routing Strategies on RE}\n\\label{sec:appendix_routing}\n\nWe evaluate five non-oracle routing strategies on FewRel 80-relation RE using graph-coloring sub-schema decomposition (same grouping as Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}). Each strategy selects a single sub-schema for each test instance and returns that sub-schema's prediction.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Strategy} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ Single-Pass} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOracle (gold label) & 72.4\\% & +22.0\\,pp \\\\\n\\midrule\nMax-conf (positive only) & 36.5\\% & $-$14.0\\,pp \\\\\nNone-aware & 36.5\\% & $-$14.0\\,pp \\\\\nMargin (positive only) & 35.9\\% & $-$14.5\\,pp \\\\\nMax-conf (all) & 27.7\\% & $-$22.7\\,pp \\\\\nRandom & 14.7\\% & $-$35.7\\,pp \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-pass (no decomposition) & 50.4\\% & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Routing strategy comparison on FewRel 80-relation RE with graph-coloring sub-schema decomposition. Oracle requires gold labels. All practical (non-oracle) strategies perform below the single-pass baseline. The failure stems from constrained decoding (enum constraint), which forces confident predictions in every sub-schema: 73.4\\% of instances receive positive predictions from 3--5 sub-schemas simultaneously, with per-sub-schema none-rates of only 20--40\\%.}\n\\label{tab:routing_strategies}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Factorial Analysis and Separability Test}\n\\label{sec:appendix_factorial}\n\nTo formally test whether count and confusability effects are additive, we fit a multiple regression (accuracy $\\sim$ $\\log_2$\\,count $+$ BCS $+$ count$\\times$BCS) across 69 batches from FewRel spanning five count levels ($k \\in \\{4, 8, 16, 32, 40\\}$). Both main effects are significant (count: $p = 0.008$; BCS: $p = 0.0002$; $R^2 = 0.52$), but the interaction term is negligible (0.47\\,pp). A TOST equivalence test with a $\\pm 3$\\,pp margin confirms that the interaction is bounded well below practical significance ($p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.003$), supporting bounded interaction between count and confusability effects.\n\nA separate within-dataset $2 \\times 2$ factorial on FewRel (count $\\in \\{8, 16\\}$, BCS low/high; $n = 54$) did not reach equivalence ($p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.56$), likely due to limited power from fewer count levels and narrower BCS contrast. The Few-NERD factorial (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}) similarly did not reach significance ($p = 0.50$). The regression approach provides the stronger test by leveraging the full count range.\n\n\\paragraph{Equivalence margin justification.}\nWe set $\\pm 3$\\,pp as the smallest interaction that would materially alter practitioner decisions: a 3\\,pp interaction atop the 2.2\\,pp confusability main effect would represent ${>}130\\%$ amplification, fundamentally changing whether confusability warrants mitigation. At $\\pm 2$\\,pp (marginally equivalent, $p = 0.047$), interaction remains bounded below the main effect itself.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{crrl}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Margin (pp)} & \\textbf{$t_{\\text{upper}}$} & \\textbf{$p_{\\text{TOST}}$} & \\textbf{Conclusion} \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\pm 1$ & $-0.59$ & $0.278$ & Inconclusive \\\\\n$\\pm 2$ & $-1.70$ & $0.047$ & Equivalent \\\\\n$\\pm 3$ & $-2.81$ & $0.003$ & Equivalent \\\\\n$\\pm 5$ & $-5.02$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ & Equivalent \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{TOST sensitivity analysis across equivalence margins. The interaction effect (0.47\\,pp, 90\\% CI [$-$1.0, 2.0]\\,pp) is bounded below 2\\,pp at marginal significance ($p = 0.047$), confirming that the $\\pm 3$\\,pp choice does not drive the equivalence conclusion. At $\\pm 1$\\,pp, equivalence is inconclusive ($p = 0.278$), meaning interaction could reach approximately half the main effect magnitude.}\n\\label{tab:tost_sensitivity}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Within-Pool CI Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_within_pool}\n\nThe quartile $\\Delta$ for 80-pool 16-relation subsets ($-$3.92\\,pp, 95\\% CI [$-$8.17, $+$0.38]) crosses zero, reflecting the compressed BCS range within that pool (0.46--0.68 vs.\\ 0.48--0.71 for the independent pool). Within this narrow band, the quartile contrast lacks statistical power to detect the effect that the regression identifies. The full 80-relation condition ($n = 25$) is underpowered for both the slope (CI: [$-$1.303, $+$1.695]) and quartile analyses; we include it in Table~\\ref{tab:within_pool} for completeness but do not draw inferential conclusions from it.\n\n\\section{Controlling for Centrality Confounds in MAVEN Per-Type Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:centrality_confound} identifies a centrality confound in MAVEN's per-type BCS--F1 correlation: both embeddings show a \\emph{positive} raw correlation, opposite to the expected direction. Here we formalize this analysis by identifying specific confounders for each embedding and testing whether the BCS--F1 relationship survives partial correlation controlling for these confounders.\n\n\\paragraph{Confound identification.}\nFor each embedding, we computed the correlation between mean BCS and candidate confounders (type frequency, degree centrality at threshold 0.7) across MAVEN's 149 active event types. The dominant confound differs by embedding:\n\\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{MiniLM}: BCS correlates with log type frequency ($r = 0.201$). Frequent types have more training signal and are extracted more reliably, producing the positive BCS--F1 association.\n\\item \\textbf{Qwen3}: BCS correlates strongly with degree centrality ($r = 0.854$). In the Qwen3 embedding space, high-BCS types are those with many semantic neighbors---central, well-represented types that the model extracts well.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{Partial correlation results.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:residualized_bcs} reports raw Spearman $\\rho$ and partial Pearson $r$ after controlling for the identified confounders. Under single-variable control, both correlations weaken to non-significance. Under joint control (log frequency $+$ degree centrality simultaneously), neither embedding retains a significant BCS--F1 association.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Embedding} & \\textbf{Control} & \\textbf{Raw $\\rho$} & \\textbf{Raw $p$} & \\textbf{Partial $r$} & \\textbf{Partial $p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMiniLM & --- & $+$0.208 & $0.011$ & --- & --- \\\\\nMiniLM & log freq & --- & --- & $+$0.133 & $0.11$ \\\\\nMiniLM & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.23$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 & --- & $+$0.184 & $0.025$ & --- & --- \\\\\nQwen3 & degree centrality & --- & --- & $+$0.063 & $0.44$ \\\\\nQwen3 & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.44$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{MAVEN per-type BCS--F1 correlation before and after controlling for centrality confounders. Raw correlations are Spearman $\\rho$; partial correlations are Pearson $r$ from residualized regression. Degree centrality is computed on the SCS graph at threshold 0.7. All values from experiment \\texttt{exp\\_maven\\_residualized\\_bcs}.}\n\\label{tab:residualized_bcs}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThe two embeddings encode different aspects of the same underlying confound. MiniLM, a smaller sentence encoder, produces a tighter embedding cluster for MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types; here, type frequency is the primary confounder because frequent types accumulate higher mean similarity to the dense cluster. Qwen3, with a larger representation space, differentiates types enough that frequency is not the dominant factor, but degree centrality---the count of strong semantic neighbors---becomes the confound instead. In both cases, the positive raw correlation reflects a centrality artifact: types that are semantically central are both high-BCS and easy to extract.\n\nUnder joint control, both embeddings yield $p > 0.2$, confirming that the raw positive BCS--F1 correlation on MAVEN is fully attributable to centrality-related confounders. This does not invalidate the confusability hypothesis; the pairwise SCS--confusion correlation on MAVEN remains significant (MiniLM $r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}). Rather, it demonstrates that per-type aggregation (mean BCS) conflates confusability with centrality in semantically homogeneous schemas, and that pairwise analysis or explicit confound adjustment is required in such settings.\n\n\\section{Batch Composition Results}\n\\label{sec:appendix_batch_composition}\n\nSee Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition} and the accompanying discussion in \\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}.\n\n\\input{prompt_appendix}\n\n\\section{Prompt Version Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\nWe assess whether the confusability effects reported in \\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}--\\ref{sec:multi_channel} are robust to prompt design by replicating key experiments under the v2 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).\n\n\\paragraph{RE (FewRel).}\nUnder the v1 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap (Table~\\ref{tab:bcs_controlled}) is substantial. Under v2, with $n{=}25$ batches per group and wider BCS contrast (low-BCS range [0.356, 0.367]; high-BCS range [0.791, 0.811]), the gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; 95\\% CI: [22.4, 28.6]; $p < 0.001$; Pearson $r = -0.919$). The effect is significant, directionally consistent with v1, and only modestly attenuated in magnitude ($-$5.7\\,pp reduction). An earlier $n{=}5$ evaluation with narrower BCS contrast (0.21) yielded a smaller gap of $-$7.9\\,pp (CI: [$-$5.2, $+$21.5]), illustrating how limited statistical power and restricted BCS range can obscure the underlying signal---a cautionary note for small-sample prompt sensitivity studies.\n\n\\paragraph{NER (Few-NERD).}\nThe batch-level BCS--F1 correlation under v2 is consistent with v1: at count${}\\,=\\,12$, $r = +0.58$ ($p = 0.002$, 95\\% CI: [0.19, 0.81]) vs.\\ v1 $r = +0.50$ ($p = 0.008$). As discussed in \\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}, this positive direction reflects a type-frequency confound at the batch level---high-BCS batches contain high-frequency entity types that models extract more reliably---rather than evidence against the confusability hypothesis. The pairwise SCS--confusion analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}), which is immune to this aggregation artifact, remains the primary evidence for the NER confusability channel.\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThe $n{=}25$ v2 result confirms that the confusability effect is directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude: the gap narrows ($-$5.7\\,pp) but remains highly significant ($p < 0.001$, $r = -0.919$). The earlier $n{=}5$ non-significance reflected insufficient power and narrow BCS contrast, not absence of the effect. V2's stricter format constraints raise the baseline accuracy (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ 82.3\\%), but type confusion under high confusability persists (59.7\\% vs.\\ 51.0\\%). Prompt design modulates the practical magnitude of the confusability effect but does not eliminate it, ruling out prompt-specific confounding as the primary driver. The underlying confusability signal---pairwise SCS--confusion correlation and the directional BCS--accuracy relationship---persists across both prompt versions.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Prompt-Variant BCS Correlation}\n\\label{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}\n\nTo verify that the BCS--accuracy relationship is not an artifact of a specific prompt template, we evaluate four structurally distinct prompt variants on FewRel 80-relation RE at two count levels (8 and 32), with 10 random batches per condition.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & \\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Mean Acc} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nV2 (definition-first) & 8 & 0.769 & $-$0.333 & 0.348 \\\\\nV2 (definition-first) & 32 & 0.564 & $-$0.648 & 0.043 \\\\\nInstruction-rewrite & 8 & 0.847 & $-$0.223 & 0.536 \\\\\nInstruction-rewrite & 32 & 0.662 & $-$0.469 & 0.171 \\\\\nChain-of-thought & 8 & 0.749 & $+$0.254 & 0.478 \\\\\nChain-of-thought & 32 & 0.617 & $-$0.566 & 0.088 \\\\\nMinimal & 8 & 0.852 & $-$0.170 & 0.638 \\\\\nMinimal & 32 & 0.615 & $-$0.771 & 0.009 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{BCS--accuracy Pearson correlation across four prompt variants. At count 32, all four variants show negative BCS--accuracy correlations ($r < -0.47$), with two reaching $p < 0.05$. At count 8, the narrower BCS range reduces statistical power.}\n\\label{tab:prompt_variants}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt count 32, all four variants produce negative BCS--accuracy correlations ($r$ ranges from $-$0.47 to $-$0.77), with V2 ($p = 0.043$) and Minimal ($p = 0.009$) reaching significance and Chain-of-thought approaching it ($p = 0.088$). At count 8, correlations are weaker and nonsignificant across all variants, consistent with the narrower BCS range at lower schema complexity reducing the detectable effect size. These results confirm that the confusability effect arises from schema composition, not prompt-specific phrasing.\n\n\\section{BCS Aggregation Sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}\n\nWe define Batch Confusability Score (BCS) as the \\emph{mean} pairwise SCS across all type pairs in a batch (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}). An alternative definition takes the \\emph{maximum} pairwise SCS within each batch, capturing the single most confusable pair. Here we compare both aggregation methods to verify that core conclusions are robust to this choice.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Aggregation} & \\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Slope} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMEAN (primary) & 16-rel & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMEAN & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMAX & 16-rel & $-$0.449 & [$-$0.552, $-$0.346] & $-$0.637 & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMAX & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{BCS--accuracy regression under mean vs.\\ max pairwise SCS aggregation (FewRel, 16-relation batches). Permutation test for slope equivalence across pools: MEAN $p \\approx 0.60$; MAX $p \\approx 0.44$. The 80-pool row is identical under both definitions because these batches were collected using mean-aggregated BCS; the MAX column reflects the same data for completeness.}\n\\label{tab:bcs_aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\nMAX aggregation yields stronger correlations ($r = -0.637$ vs.\\ $-0.467$ for 16-rel), consistent with the hypothesis that the single most confusable pair in a batch predicts substitution errors. Both definitions produce significant negative slopes across all conditions, and the permutation tests confirm no significant difference between independent and sampled pools under either definition. We adopt MEAN as the primary definition because it better represents average pairwise difficulty across all schema combinations, while MAX may overweight a single outlier pair. The choice does not affect any paper conclusion.\n\n\\section{BCS-Controlled Detailed Results}\n\\label{app:bcs-controlled-table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Schema} & \\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Low-BCS} & \\textbf{High-BCS} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\n16-rel & 8 & 88.9\\% & 79.8\\% & $-$9.1 & --- \\\\\n80-rel & 8 & 82.3\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$31.2 & {[}17.7, 42.7{]} \\\\\n80-rel & 16 & 82.8\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$31.2 & {[}22.9, 39.6{]} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. Low-BCS batches contain maximally dissimilar types; high-BCS batches contain maximally similar types. The 80-relation extreme-batch gap reflects the wider BCS range; regression slopes are consistent across pools ($p = 0.60$; Table~\\ref{tab:within_pool}; see \\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude} for typical-batch magnitudes). The 16-relation condition used a single extreme batch per BCS level; bootstrap CIs require multiple batches.}\n\\label{tab:bcs_controlled}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Within-Pool Analysis Methodology}\n\\label{app:within-pool-method}\n\nA potential confound in the cross-scale comparison is that the 16-relation and 80-relation pools contain different types, so the observed gap could reflect pool composition rather than confusability. To control for this, we draw 100 random 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool and evaluate under the same BCS-controlled protocol.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textbf{Regression (BCS $\\to$ Accuracy)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{Quartile $\\Delta$}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-5} \\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\n\\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Slope} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIndependent 16-rel ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}\\,0.001$ & $-$5.31 & [$-$7.44, $-$3.19] \\\\\n80-pool $\\to$ 16-sub ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & $0.027$ & $-$3.92 & [$-$8.17, $+$0.38] \\\\\nFull 80-rel ($n{=}25$)$^\\dagger$ & $+$0.221 & [$-$1.303, $+$1.695] & $+$0.064 & $0.76$ & $-$1.04 & [$-$10.12, $+$8.04] \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize Permutation test (16-rel vs.\\ 80-pool slopes): $p \\approx 0.60$ (5{,}000 permutations)} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$BCS range (IQR\\,=\\,0.033) insufficient for regression; slope not interpretable.} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Three-way BCS--accuracy analysis on FewRel. Slope: OLS of batch accuracy on BCS (bootstrap CIs, $B{=}10{,}000$). $\\Delta$: accuracy gap between top- and bottom-quartile BCS batches. See Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool} for CI interpretation.}\n\\label{tab:within_pool}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe within-pool slope ($-$0.401, CI excludes zero) is statistically indistinguishable from the independent 16-relation pool ($-$0.509; permutation $p \\approx 0.60$). The smaller quartile gap ($-$3.92\\,pp vs.\\ $-$5.31\\,pp) reflects the narrower BCS range within the 80-relation pool (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool}).\n\nIn the full 80-relation condition, natural BCS variation is insufficient (IQR${}=0.033$) to yield a detectable effect ($n=25$, slope${}=+0.221$, $p \\approx 0.76$). This does not invalidate the confusability effect---the within-pool slope is significant ($p=0.027$) when BCS is deliberately varied---but establishes that natural schema sampling at this scale does not produce sufficient BCS contrast for detection.\n\n\\section{SCS Embedding Ablation: Name-Only vs. Name+Definition}\n\\label{app:scs-ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scs-ablation} compares predictive accuracy of name-only SCS against name+definition SCS across three datasets.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nDataset & SCS$_\\text{name}$ $r$ & SCS$_\\text{def}$ $r$ & $\\Delta r$ & Matrix corr. \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & 0.172 & 0.149 & $-$0.023 & 0.894 \\\\\nMAVEN & 0.129 & 0.164 & +0.035 & 0.656 \\\\\nFewRel & 0.178 & 0.193 & +0.015 & 0.848 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Pearson correlation between SCS and empirical confusion rate. ``Def'' concatenates type name with definition before embedding. All correlations significant ($p < 0.001$). The inconsistent direction of $\\Delta r$ indicates no systematic benefit from including definitions.}\n\\label{tab:scs-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nFew-NERD is the only dataset whose prompts include type definitions; for MAVEN and FewRel, prompts use type names alone, making name-only SCS a direct match to model input. The high matrix correlation (0.66--0.89) confirms that type names capture the dominant semantic structure.\n\n\\section{Pair-to-Batch Aggregation Simulation}\n\\label{app:aggregation-sim}\n\nThe following simulation provides an illustrative explanatory model for how pair-level effects aggregate to batch-level variance. It is not the evidence base for our claims---the observed batch-level $R^2 = 0.218$ (\\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}) is a direct empirical measurement independent of this simulation.\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.} We examine whether the pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ (computed over non-zero confusion pairs) can aggregate to the observed batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ via a Monte Carlo simulation. From the FewRel 16-relation experiment, we estimate the inter-pair SCS correlation for pairs sharing a common type ($\\rho_\\text{SCS} = 0.35$). For batches of $k=8$ types ($\\binom{8}{2}=28$ pairs), we simulate 10{,}000 iterations: (1) generate 28 correlated pair-level effects with individual $R^2 = 0.074$; (2) average to obtain batch-level signals; (3) compute batch-level $R^2$.\n\n\\paragraph{Results.} Simulated batch $R^2 = 0.219$ [95\\% CI: 0.092--0.361], matching the observed value of 0.218. The effective amplification factor is $\\sim$3$\\times$, explained by signal correlation within batches preserving variance while independent noise averages out (effective degrees of freedom $\\approx 3.4$, not 28).\n\n\\paragraph{Unconditional baseline.}\nWhen the simulation uses the unconditional pairwise $r^2 = 0.013$ (computed over all type pairs including zero-confusion pairs; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}), the simulated batch-level $R^2$ drops to $0.044$ [95\\% CI: 0.001--0.151]---well below the observed $R^2 = 0.218$. The analytical formula confirms: $\\text{SNR} \\times \\text{EF} / (\\text{SNR} \\times \\text{EF} + 1) = 0.042$ with the same effective factor $\\text{EF} = 3.35$. This ${\\sim}5\\times$ gap reflects the dilution from zero-confusion pairs: approximately 89\\% of type pairs never exhibit mutual confusion, contributing only noise to the unconditional correlation. The conditional model ($R^2 = 0.074 \\to 0.219$) matches the observed value because it isolates the minority of semantically proximate pairs that actually compete during type resolution; BCS predicts batch accuracy through the density of \\emph{active} confusable pairs, not uniform contributions from all pairs.\n\n\\section{Split-Origin Covariate Analysis}\n\\label{app:split-origin}\n\nFewRel combines 64 train-split and 16 val-split relation types into a single 80-type pool.  Train-origin types show numerically lower accuracy ($-8.8$\\,pp, $p = 0.06$; see Limitations), raising the question of whether differential pre-training exposure confounds the BCS--accuracy relationship.  We test this by adding a per-batch \\emph{train-origin ratio} (fraction of relation types drawn from the train split) as a covariate in the BCS--accuracy regression.\n\n\\paragraph{Method.}  Across 213 multi-type batches ($k \\geq 2$) from the batch-composition experiment (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}) and the factorial design (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}), we compute $\\texttt{train\\_origin\\_ratio} = n_{\\text{train}} / k$ for each batch.  The ratio ranges from 0.40 to 1.00 (mean 0.80, reflecting the 64/80 base rate).  We fit:\n\\[\n\\text{accuracy} = \\beta_0 + \\beta_{\\text{BCS}} \\cdot \\text{BCS} + \\beta_{\\text{origin}} \\cdot \\text{train\\_origin\\_ratio} + \\varepsilon\n\\]\n\n\\paragraph{Results.}  The BCS partial effect is unchanged after controlling for split-origin: $\\beta_{\\text{BCS}} = -0.416$, $p < 10^{-16}$ (vs.\\ $\\beta = -0.412$, $p < 10^{-16}$ without the covariate).  Train-origin ratio is not independently significant ($\\beta_{\\text{origin}} = -0.057$, $p = 0.245$), and $\\Delta R^2 = 0.005$.  The BCS--accuracy relationship is therefore not confounded by differential pre-training exposure at the batch level.\n\n\\section{Cross-Domain Connections}\n\\label{app:cross-domain}\n\nThe schema-induced degradation documented here is not unique to information extraction. Schema-Guided Dialogue systems (SGD/SGD-X; \\citealt{rastogi2020towards}) face an analogous challenge: as the number of intent and slot types grows, dialogue state tracking accuracy declines. \\citet{li2023evaluating} observed count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT-based IE, attributing it to prompt overload. Our two-channel decomposition offers a more refined explanation: schema size per se is not the sole driver; rather, larger schemas increase the probability of including high-SCS pairs, which contribute disproportionately to confusion. This distinction has practical implications---a schema of 80 types with low mutual confusability may outperform a 40-type schema with clustered semantics---and suggests that confusability-aware design principles may generalize to any task where an LLM must discriminate among a prompt-specified label inventory.\n\n\\section{Additional Experimental Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_additional_details}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Seed Variance Decomposition}\n\\label{app:variance-decomposition}\n\nTo quantify whether performance variability is driven by random batch composition or seed-dependent model inference noise, we decompose total variance into intra-seed (batch) and inter-seed components across three seeds (42, 123, 456), each evaluated on 25 random batches per count level.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Batch SD} & \\textbf{Seed SD} & \\textbf{Ratio} \\\\\n\\midrule\n4 & 0.0889 & 0.0122 & 53.5 \\\\\n8 & 0.0820 & 0.0098 & 70.0 \\\\\n16 & 0.0626 & 0.0270 & 5.4 \\\\\n32 & 0.0462 & 0.0091 & 26.0 \\\\\n40 & 0.0421 & 0.0019 & 474.4 \\\\\n64 & 0.0245 & 0.0022 & 119.8 \\\\\n80 & 0.0001 & 0.0088 & $<$0.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition across count levels on FewRel. Batch SD is the root-mean intra-seed variance across 25 random type batches (averaged over 3 seeds). Seed SD is the standard deviation of the 3 per-seed means. Ratio = mean batch variance / seed variance. At count 80, only one batch composition exists (the full schema), so batch variance vanishes by construction.}\n\\label{tab:variance_decomposition}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Batch SD} & \\textbf{Seed SD} & \\textbf{Ratio} \\\\\n\\midrule\n2 & 0.1782 & 0.0557 & 10.2 \\\\\n6 & 0.1233 & 0.0160 & 59.2 \\\\\n12 & 0.1063 & 0.0297 & 12.8 \\\\\n22 & 0.0954 & 0.0096 & 99.2 \\\\\n44 & 0.1214 & 0.0143 & 72.4 \\\\\n66 & 0.0016 & 0.0002 & 46.3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition on Few-NERD (3 seeds $\\times$ 25 batches per count level). Batch SD: root-mean intra-seed standard deviation averaged over seeds. Seed SD: standard deviation of per-seed means. Ratio = batch variance / seed variance. At count 66 (full schema), only one composition exists, so both components collapse to near-zero.}\n\\label{tab:variance_decomposition_ner}\n\\end{table}\n\nIntra-batch variance dominates inter-seed variance by 5--474$\\times$ on FewRel (mean 125$\\times$, counts 4--64) and 10--99$\\times$ on Few-NERD (mean 51$\\times$, counts 2--44), confirming that random batch composition is the primary source of performance variability on both tasks, not seed-dependent randomness in model inference. The wide ratio range reflects variation in how tightly seed means cluster at each count level; even the minimum ratio (5.4$\\times$ on FewRel at count 16, 10.2$\\times$ on Few-NERD at count 2) indicates batch composition contributes the majority of observed variance. At count 80 (FewRel) and count 66 (Few-NERD), the full schema admits only one batch composition, collapsing batch variance by construction.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Count-Level Statistics}\n\\label{sec:appendix_per_count_stats}\n\n[Placeholder: per-count-level detailed statistics to be added.]\n\n\\section{Full Results Tables}\n\\label{sec:appendix_full_results}\n\n\\subsection{Count Curve Raw Data (All Seeds)}\n\\label{sec:appendix_count_curve_raw}\n\n[Placeholder: full count-curve data across all seeds to be added.]\n\n\\subsection{BCS-Controlled Raw Data}\n\\label{sec:appendix_bcs_raw}\n\n[Placeholder: BCS-controlled raw data to be added.]\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\end{document}\n","numLines":466,"startLine":1,"totalLines":466}}}

{"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-opus-4-6","id":"msg_01FjfwAgvitGZEQfXRhYPc33","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_01GebevWnwyjUfPD2wZGqhPW","name":"Edit","input":{"replace_all":false,"file_path":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/abstract.tex","old_string":"We identify two degradation channels: count-driven recall decline and confusability-driven type substitution. In typical random batches, confusability accounts for ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp accuracy difference---markedly smaller than count effects ($-$49\\,pp for document-level RE)---while batch-level BCS explains 22--53\\% of cross-batch variance. The channels exhibit bounded interaction in RE (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) but are coupled in NER where boundary and type ambiguity interact. Effects are directionally consistent across prompt designs and model scales, though Channel~2 attenuates on smaller models. Schema decomposition via oracle routing recovers substantial RE gains, but confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random.","new_string":"We identify two degradation dimensions: count-associated recall decline and confusability-associated type substitution. In typical random batches, confusability accounts for ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp accuracy difference, markedly smaller than count effects ($-$49\\,pp for document-level RE), while batch-level BCS explains 22--53\\% of cross-batch variance. The dimensions exhibit bounded association in RE but are coupled in NER where boundary and type ambiguity interact. Effects are directionally consistent across prompt designs and model scales, though Dimension~2 attenuates on smaller models. Schema decomposition via oracle routing recovers substantial RE gains, but confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random."},"caller":{"type":"direct"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"stop_details":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":1,"cache_creation_input_tokens":543,"cache_read_input_tokens":84727,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":0,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":543},"output_tokens":1,"service_tier":"standard","inference_geo":"not_available"},"diagnostics":null,"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"df5fe4d6-513d-4f5b-8c8e-9a183c842278"}

The channels exhibit bounded interaction in RE (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) but are coupled in NER where boundary and type ambiguity interact. Schema decomposition via oracle routing recovers substantial RE gains, but confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random.","newString":"We identify two degradation dimensions: count-associated recall decline and confusability-associated type substitution. In typical random batches, confusability accounts for ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp accuracy difference, markedly smaller than count effects ($-$49\\,pp for document-level RE), while batch-level BCS explains 22--53\\% of cross-batch variance. The dimensions exhibit bounded association in RE but are coupled in NER where boundary and type ambiguity interact. Schema decomposition via oracle routing recovers substantial RE gains, but confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random.","originalFile":"\\begin{abstract}\nTraining-free LLM information extraction degrades as schema complexity grows. To our knowledge, we present the first controlled study isolating type confusability from type count in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE, and event detection across four datasets. We introduce the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) and two experimental paradigms: count curves (varying schema size while randomizing confusability) and BCS-controlled experiments (fixing size while varying confusability).\n\nWe identify two degradation channels: count-driven recall decline and confusability-driven type substitution. In typical random batches, confusability accounts for ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp accuracy difference---markedly smaller than count effects ($-$49\\,pp for document-level RE)---while batch-level BCS explains 22--53\\% of cross-batch variance. The channels exhibit bounded interaction in RE (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) but are coupled in NER where boundary and type ambiguity interact. Effects are directionally consistent across prompt designs and model scales, though Channel~2 attenuates on smaller models. Schema decomposition via oracle routing recovers substantial RE gains, but confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random.\n\\end{abstract}\n","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":1,"oldLines":5,"newStart":1,"newLines":5,"lines":[" \\begin{abstract}"," Training-free LLM information extraction degrades as schema complexity grows. To our knowledge, we present the first controlled study isolating type confusability from type count in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE, and event detection across four datasets. We introduce the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) and two experimental paradigms: count curves (varying schema size while randomizing confusability) and BCS-controlled experiments (fixing size while varying confusability)."," ","-We identify two degradation channels: count-driven recall decline and confusability-driven type substitution. In typical random batches, confusability accounts for ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp accuracy difference---markedly smaller than count effects ($-$49\\,pp for document-level RE)---while batch-level BCS explains 22--53\\% of cross-batch variance. The channels exhibit bounded interaction in RE (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) but are coupled in NER where boundary and type ambiguity interact. Effects are directionally consistent across prompt designs and model scales, though Channel~2 attenuates on smaller models. Schema decomposition via oracle routing recovers substantial RE gains, but confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random.","+We identify two degradation dimensions: count-associated recall decline and confusability-associated type substitution. In typical random batches, confusability accounts for ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp accuracy difference, markedly smaller than count effects ($-$49\\,pp for document-level RE), while batch-level BCS explains 22--53\\% of cross-batch variance. The dimensions exhibit bounded association in RE but are coupled in NER where boundary and type ambiguity interact. Effects are directionally consistent across prompt designs and model scales, though Dimension~2 attenuates on smaller models. Schema decomposition via oracle routing recovers substantial RE gains, but confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random."," \\end{abstract}"]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

Anonymous review materials: \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/schema-dag-extraction}} LLM information extraction (prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning) enables","originalFile":"\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nTraining-free\\footnote{We use ``training-free'' and ``zero-shot'' interchangeably to denote prompting without parameter updates.}\\footnote{Code and data will be released upon acceptance. Anonymous review materials: \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/schema-dag-extraction}} LLM information extraction---prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning---enables rapid deployment across new domains and ontologies. This zero-shot paradigm eliminates the need for labeled data, making it attractive for applications where annotation is expensive or schemas evolve frequently \\cite{llm4ie-survey-2024}. Yet the paradigm carries a critical limitation: performance degrades sharply as the number of candidate types in the prompt schema grows. On named entity recognition, recall drops from 0.72 with a single candidate type to a plateau of ${\\sim}$0.27 across intermediate schema sizes (22--44 types) before partial recovery at full-schema. On relation extraction, accuracy falls from 85.7\\% with one relation to 49.5\\% with 80 relations (sentence-level) and from 88.0\\% to 38.7\\% with 96 relations (document-level)---near or exceeding halving. This degradation is not academic: real-world ontologies routinely define 50--200+ types, placing them squarely in the regime where training-free extraction becomes unreliable.\n\nWhat explains this degradation? Prior decomposition strategies \\cite{uniner-2024,llm-pee-2025,parse-2025} conflate two mechanisms: having more types to discriminate among (count) and having semantically similar types to confuse (confusability) (hereafter Channel~1 and Channel~2). None isolates which factor predicts the gains (\\S\\ref{sec:related_work}).\n\nWe ask three questions. \\textbf{RQ1}: Does type confusability independently predict extraction degradation beyond the effect of type count? \\textbf{RQ2}: Through what mechanisms do count and confusability each degrade performance? \\textbf{RQ3}: Can confusability-aware schema decomposition improve extraction quality?\n\nTo our knowledge, we present the first controlled study isolating type confusability from type count via stratified design in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE, and event detection (ED). We operationalize confusability via the \\emph{Schema Confusability Score} (SCS)---pairwise embedding cosine similarity---and its batch-level aggregation, the \\emph{Batch Confusability Score} (BCS). We evaluate on Few-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021}, FewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018}, DocRED \\cite{docred-2019}, and MAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} using Qwen3-32B (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental_setup}).\n\nOur experiments yield five findings: (1)~degradation operates through two channels---count-driven recall decline (up to $-$49\\,pp at intermediate schema sizes for document-level RE) and confusability-driven type substitution (typical-batch gap ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp; quartile $\\Delta = -5.3$\\,pp)---an asymmetric relationship where count dominates, consistent with bounded interaction in RE (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) but coupled in NER where NER recall exhibits non-monotonic recovery at full-schema; (2)~pairwise SCS predicts type confusion ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), the first factor empirically isolated in controlled zero-shot IE; (3)~effects are consistent across schema sizes and granularities (BCS slopes: $p = 0.60$; DocRED: slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), and directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts; (4)~per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas; (5)~NER decomposition fails due to merge amplification; RE confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) despite oracle routing recovering +29.8\\,pp.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt,topsep=2pt]\n    \\item \\textbf{Count dominates confusability by ${\\sim}20\\times$ in typical batches.} Through stratified design isolating count from confusability, we show count effects ($-$42\\% to $-$56\\% recall) dwarf typical confusability effects (2.2\\,pp), counter to decomposition literature's implicit assumption that confusability is the primary bottleneck. Interaction is bounded (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) in RE but not in NER, where boundary and type ambiguity couple (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}).\n\\item \\textbf{Pairwise embedding similarity predicts type confusion.} SCS correlates with confusion rates ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), with centrality confound identified on homogeneous schemas---invalidating naive per-type aggregation (\\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}).\n\\item \\textbf{BCS generalizes across tasks.} BCS--accuracy slopes are consistent across schema sizes ($p = 0.60$) and replicate at document level (DocRED: $R^2 = 0.22\\text{--}0.53$) (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\\item \\textbf{Schema decomposition exposes systematic routing failure.} Naive merge strategies (union, confidence-gated) collapse NER precision from 0.56 to 0.21. On RE, we evaluate seven routing strategies (oracle, five practical heuristics, random); all practical strategies fall below the single-pass baseline (best 36.5\\% vs.\\ 50.4\\%), because constrained decoding eliminates reliable sub-schema confidence signals. Oracle routing recovers +29.8\\,pp, but confusability-aware grouping adds only +1.4\\,pp ($p \\approx 0.19$) over random, leaving deployable routing as an open challenge (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}).\n\\end{enumerate}","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":1,"oldLines":7,"newStart":1,"newLines":7,"lines":[" \\section{Introduction}"," \\label{sec:introduction}"," ","-Training-free\\footnote{We use ``training-free'' and ``zero-shot'' interchangeably to denote prompting without parameter updates.}\\footnote{Code and data will be released upon acceptance. Anonymous review materials: \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/schema-dag-extraction}} LLM information extraction---prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning---enables rapid deployment across new domains and ontologies. This zero-shot paradigm eliminates the need for labeled data, making it attractive for applications where annotation is expensive or schemas evolve frequently \\cite{llm4ie-survey-2024}. Yet the paradigm carries a critical limitation: performance degrades sharply as the number of candidate types in the prompt schema grows. On named entity recognition, recall drops from 0.72 with a single candidate type to a plateau of ${\\sim}$0.27 across intermediate schema sizes (22--44 types) before partial recovery at full-schema. On relation extraction, accuracy falls from 85.7\\% with one relation to 49.5\\% with 80 relations (sentence-level) and from 88.0\\% to 38.7\\% with 96 relations (document-level)---near or exceeding halving. This degradation is not academic: real-world ontologies routinely define 50--200+ types, placing them squarely in the regime where training-free extraction becomes unreliable.","+Training-free\\footnote{We use ``training-free'' and ``zero-shot'' interchangeably to denote prompting without parameter updates.}\\footnote{Code and data will be released upon acceptance. Anonymous review materials: \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/schema-dag-extraction}} LLM information extraction (prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning) enables rapid deployment across new domains and ontologies. This zero-shot paradigm eliminates the need for labeled data, making it attractive for applications where annotation is expensive or schemas evolve frequently \\cite{llm4ie-survey-2024}. Yet the paradigm carries a critical limitation: performance degrades sharply as the number of candidate types in the prompt schema grows. On named entity recognition, recall drops from 0.72 with a single candidate type to a plateau of ${\\sim}$0.27 across intermediate schema sizes (22--44 types) before partial recovery at full-schema. On relation extraction, accuracy falls from 85.7\\% with one relation to 49.5\\% with 80 relations (sentence-level) and from 88.0\\% to 38.7\\% with 96 relations (document-level)---near or exceeding halving. This degradation is not academic: real-world ontologies routinely define 50--200+ types, placing them squarely in the regime where training-free extraction becomes unreliable."," "," What explains this degradation? Prior decomposition strategies \\cite{uniner-2024,llm-pee-2025,parse-2025} conflate two mechanisms: having more types to discriminate among (count) and having semantically similar types to confuse (confusability) (hereafter Channel~1 and Channel~2). None isolates which factor predicts the gains (\\S\\ref{sec:related_work})."," "]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01UUS1RX9eMU9uBoj3xoqbbi","type":"tool_result","content":"The file /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/introduction.tex has been updated successfully."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"509fea43-a3b4-499d-896a-1afe56d58379","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:04:59.954Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/introduction.tex","oldString":"We ask three questions.","newString":"This study combines correlational analyses with quasi-experimental manipulation (BCS-controlled batches) to characterize how schema properties predict extraction accuracy.\n\nWe ask three questions.","originalFile":"\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nTraining-free\\footnote{We use ``training-free'' and ``zero-shot'' interchangeably to denote prompting without parameter updates.}\\footnote{Code and data will be released upon acceptance. Anonymous review materials: \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/schema-dag-extraction}} LLM information extraction (prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning) enables rapid deployment across new domains and ontologies. This zero-shot paradigm eliminates the need for labeled data, making it attractive for applications where annotation is expensive or schemas evolve frequently \\cite{llm4ie-survey-2024}. Yet the paradigm carries a critical limitation: performance degrades sharply as the number of candidate types in the prompt schema grows. On named entity recognition, recall drops from 0.72 with a single candidate type to a plateau of ${\\sim}$0.27 across intermediate schema sizes (22--44 types) before partial recovery at full-schema. On relation extraction, accuracy falls from 85.7\\% with one relation to 49.5\\% with 80 relations (sentence-level) and from 88.0\\% to 38.7\\% with 96 relations (document-level)---near or exceeding halving. This degradation is not academic: real-world ontologies routinely define 50--200+ types, placing them squarely in the regime where training-free extraction becomes unreliable.\n\nWhat explains this degradation? Prior decomposition strategies \\cite{uniner-2024,llm-pee-2025,parse-2025} conflate two factors: having more types to discriminate among (count) and having semantically similar types to confuse (confusability) (hereafter Dimension~1 and Dimension~2). None isolates which factor predicts the gains (\\S\\ref{sec:related_work}).\n\nWe ask three questions. \\textbf{RQ1}: Does type confusability independently predict extraction degradation beyond the effect of type count? \\textbf{RQ2}: Through what mechanisms do count and confusability each degrade performance? \\textbf{RQ3}: Can confusability-aware schema decomposition improve extraction quality?\n\nTo our knowledge, we present the first controlled study isolating type confusability from type count via stratified design in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE, and event detection (ED). We operationalize confusability via the \\emph{Schema Confusability Score} (SCS)---pairwise embedding cosine similarity---and its batch-level aggregation, the \\emph{Batch Confusability Score} (BCS). We evaluate on Few-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021}, FewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018}, DocRED \\cite{docred-2019}, and MAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} using Qwen3-32B (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental_setup}).\n\nOur experiments yield five findings: (1)~degradation operates through two channels---count-driven recall decline (up to $-$49\\,pp at intermediate schema sizes for document-level RE) and confusability-driven type substitution (typical-batch gap ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp; quartile $\\Delta = -5.3$\\,pp)---an asymmetric relationship where count dominates, consistent with bounded interaction in RE (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) but coupled in NER where NER recall exhibits non-monotonic recovery at full-schema; (2)~pairwise SCS predicts type confusion ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), the first factor empirically isolated in controlled zero-shot IE; (3)~effects are consistent across schema sizes and granularities (BCS slopes: $p = 0.60$; DocRED: slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), and directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts; (4)~per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas; (5)~NER decomposition fails due to merge amplification; RE confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) despite oracle routing recovering +29.8\\,pp.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt,topsep=2pt]\n    \\item \\textbf{Count dominates confusability by ${\\sim}20\\times$ in typical batches.} Through stratified design isolating count from confusability, we show count effects ($-$42\\% to $-$56\\% recall) dwarf typical confusability effects (2.2\\,pp), counter to decomposition literature's implicit assumption that confusability is the primary bottleneck. Interaction is bounded (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) in RE but not in NER, where boundary and type ambiguity couple (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}).\n\\item \\textbf{Pairwise embedding similarity predicts type confusion.} SCS correlates with confusion rates ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), with centrality confound identified on homogeneous schemas---invalidating naive per-type aggregation (\\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}).\n\\item \\textbf{BCS generalizes across tasks.} BCS--accuracy slopes are consistent across schema sizes ($p = 0.60$) and replicate at document level (DocRED: $R^2 = 0.22\\text{--}0.53$) (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\\item \\textbf{Schema decomposition exposes systematic routing failure.} Naive merge strategies (union, confidence-gated) collapse NER precision from 0.56 to 0.21. On RE, we evaluate seven routing strategies (oracle, five practical heuristics, random); all practical strategies fall below the single-pass baseline (best 36.5\\% vs.\\ 50.4\\%), because constrained decoding eliminates reliable sub-schema confidence signals. Oracle routing recovers +29.8\\,pp, but confusability-aware grouping adds only +1.4\\,pp ($p \\approx 0.19$) over random, leaving deployable routing as an open challenge (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}).\n\\end{enumerate}","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":5,"oldLines":6,"newStart":5,"newLines":8,"lines":[" "," What explains this degradation? Prior decomposition strategies \\cite{uniner-2024,llm-pee-2025,parse-2025} conflate two factors: having more types to discriminate among (count) and having semantically similar types to confuse (confusability) (hereafter Dimension~1 and Dimension~2). None isolates which factor predicts the gains (\\S\\ref{sec:related_work})."," ","+This study combines correlational analyses with quasi-experimental manipulation (BCS-controlled batches) to characterize how schema properties predict extraction accuracy.","+"," We ask three questions. \\textbf{RQ1}: Does type confusability independently predict extraction degradation beyond the effect of type count? \\textbf{RQ2}: Through what mechanisms do count and confusability each degrade performance? \\textbf{RQ3}: Can confusability-aware schema decomposition improve extraction quality?"," "," To our knowledge, we present the first controlled study isolating type confusability from type count via stratified design in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE, and event detection (ED). We operationalize confusability via the \\emph{Schema Confusability Score} (SCS)---pairwise embedding cosine similarity---and its batch-level aggregation, the \\emph{Batch Confusability Score} (BCS). We evaluate on Few-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021}, FewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018}, DocRED \\cite{docred-2019}, and MAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} using Qwen3-32B (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental_setup})."]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}
