{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01GdieCCLeNEGMUfzrr3yZq7","type":"tool_result","content":"The file /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/introduction.tex has been updated successfully."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"25a72122-2237-4625-83a4-52ccc4644ced","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:05:02.938Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/introduction.tex","oldString":"(SCS)---pairwise embedding cosine similarity---and","newString":"(SCS), defined as pairwise embedding cosine similarity, and","originalFile":"\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nTraining-free\\footnote{We use ``training-free'' and ``zero-shot'' interchangeably to denote prompting without parameter updates.}\\footnote{Code and data will be released upon acceptance. Anonymous review materials: \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/schema-dag-extraction}} LLM information extraction (prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning) enables rapid deployment across new domains and ontologies. This zero-shot paradigm eliminates the need for labeled data, making it attractive for applications where annotation is expensive or schemas evolve frequently \\cite{llm4ie-survey-2024}. Yet the paradigm carries a critical limitation: performance degrades sharply as the number of candidate types in the prompt schema grows. On named entity recognition, recall drops from 0.72 with a single candidate type to a plateau of ${\\sim}$0.27 across intermediate schema sizes (22--44 types) before partial recovery at full-schema. On relation extraction, accuracy falls from 85.7\\% with one relation to 49.5\\% with 80 relations (sentence-level) and from 88.0\\% to 38.7\\% with 96 relations (document-level)---near or exceeding halving. This degradation is not academic: real-world ontologies routinely define 50--200+ types, placing them squarely in the regime where training-free extraction becomes unreliable.\n\nWhat explains this degradation? Prior decomposition strategies \\cite{uniner-2024,llm-pee-2025,parse-2025} conflate two factors: having more types to discriminate among (count) and having semantically similar types to confuse (confusability) (hereafter Dimension~1 and Dimension~2). None isolates which factor predicts the gains (\\S\\ref{sec:related_work}).\n\nThis study combines correlational analyses with quasi-experimental manipulation (BCS-controlled batches) to characterize how schema properties predict extraction accuracy.\n\nWe ask three questions. \\textbf{RQ1}: Does type confusability independently predict extraction degradation beyond the effect of type count? \\textbf{RQ2}: Through what mechanisms do count and confusability each degrade performance? \\textbf{RQ3}: Can confusability-aware schema decomposition improve extraction quality?\n\nTo our knowledge, we present the first controlled study isolating type confusability from type count via stratified design in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE, and event detection (ED). We operationalize confusability via the \\emph{Schema Confusability Score} (SCS)---pairwise embedding cosine similarity---and its batch-level aggregation, the \\emph{Batch Confusability Score} (BCS). We evaluate on Few-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021}, FewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018}, DocRED \\cite{docred-2019}, and MAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} using Qwen3-32B (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental_setup}).\n\nOur experiments yield five findings: (1)~degradation operates through two channels---count-driven recall decline (up to $-$49\\,pp at intermediate schema sizes for document-level RE) and confusability-driven type substitution (typical-batch gap ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp; quartile $\\Delta = -5.3$\\,pp)---an asymmetric relationship where count dominates, consistent with bounded interaction in RE (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) but coupled in NER where NER recall exhibits non-monotonic recovery at full-schema; (2)~pairwise SCS predicts type confusion ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), the first factor empirically isolated in controlled zero-shot IE; (3)~effects are consistent across schema sizes and granularities (BCS slopes: $p = 0.60$; DocRED: slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), and directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts; (4)~per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas; (5)~NER decomposition fails due to merge amplification; RE confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) despite oracle routing recovering +29.8\\,pp.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt,topsep=2pt]\n    \\item \\textbf{Count dominates confusability by ${\\sim}20\\times$ in typical batches.} Through stratified design isolating count from confusability, we show count effects ($-$42\\% to $-$56\\% recall) dwarf typical confusability effects (2.2\\,pp), counter to decomposition literature's implicit assumption that confusability is the primary bottleneck. Interaction is bounded (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) in RE but not in NER, where boundary and type ambiguity couple (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}).\n\\item \\textbf{Pairwise embedding similarity predicts type confusion.} SCS correlates with confusion rates ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), with centrality confound identified on homogeneous schemas---invalidating naive per-type aggregation (\\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}).\n\\item \\textbf{BCS generalizes across tasks.} BCS--accuracy slopes are consistent across schema sizes ($p = 0.60$) and replicate at document level (DocRED: $R^2 = 0.22\\text{--}0.53$) (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\\item \\textbf{Schema decomposition exposes systematic routing failure.} Naive merge strategies (union, confidence-gated) collapse NER precision from 0.56 to 0.21. On RE, we evaluate seven routing strategies (oracle, five practical heuristics, random); all practical strategies fall below the single-pass baseline (best 36.5\\% vs.\\ 50.4\\%), because constrained decoding eliminates reliable sub-schema confidence signals. Oracle routing recovers +29.8\\,pp, but confusability-aware grouping adds only +1.4\\,pp ($p \\approx 0.19$) over random, leaving deployable routing as an open challenge (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}).\n\\end{enumerate}","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":9,"oldLines":7,"newStart":9,"newLines":7,"lines":[" "," We ask three questions. \\textbf{RQ1}: Does type confusability independently predict extraction degradation beyond the effect of type count? \\textbf{RQ2}: Through what mechanisms do count and confusability each degrade performance? \\textbf{RQ3}: Can confusability-aware schema decomposition improve extraction quality?"," ","-To our knowledge, we present the first controlled study isolating type confusability from type count via stratified design in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE, and event detection (ED). We operationalize confusability via the \\emph{Schema Confusability Score} (SCS)---pairwise embedding cosine similarity---and its batch-level aggregation, the \\emph{Batch Confusability Score} (BCS). We evaluate on Few-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021}, FewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018}, DocRED \\cite{docred-2019}, and MAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} using Qwen3-32B (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental_setup}).","+To our knowledge, we present the first controlled study isolating type confusability from type count via stratified design in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE, and event detection (ED). We operationalize confusability via the \\emph{Schema Confusability Score} (SCS), defined as pairwise embedding cosine similarity, and its batch-level aggregation, the \\emph{Batch Confusability Score} (BCS). We evaluate on Few-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021}, FewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018}, DocRED \\cite{docred-2019}, and MAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} using Qwen3-32B (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental_setup})."," "," Our experiments yield five findings: (1)~degradation operates through two channels---count-driven recall decline (up to $-$49\\,pp at intermediate schema sizes for document-level RE) and confusability-driven type substitution (typical-batch gap ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp; quartile $\\Delta = -5.3$\\,pp)---an asymmetric relationship where count dominates, consistent with bounded interaction in RE (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) but coupled in NER where NER recall exhibits non-monotonic recovery at full-schema; (2)~pairwise SCS predicts type confusion ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), the first factor empirically isolated in controlled zero-shot IE; (3)~effects are consistent across schema sizes and granularities (BCS slopes: $p = 0.60$; DocRED: slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), and directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts; (4)~per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas; (5)~NER decomposition fails due to merge amplification; RE confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) despite oracle routing recovering +29.8\\,pp."," "]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

(2)~pairwise SCS predicts type confusion ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), the first factor empirically isolated in controlled zero-shot IE; (3)~effects are consistent across schema sizes and granularities (BCS slopes: $p = 0.60$; DocRED: slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), and directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts; (4)~per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas; (5)~NER decomposition fails due to merge amplification; RE confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) despite oracle routing recovering +29.8\\,pp.","+Our experiments yield five findings: (1)~degradation operates along two dimensions: count-associated recall decline (up to $-$49\\,pp at intermediate schema sizes for document-level RE) and confusability-associated type substitution (typical-batch gap ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp; quartile $\\Delta = -5.3$\\,pp), an asymmetric relationship where count dominates, consistent with bounded association in RE (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) but coupled in NER where NER recall exhibits non-monotonic recovery at full-schema; (2)~pairwise SCS predicts type confusion ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), the first factor empirically isolated in controlled zero-shot IE; (3)~effects are consistent across schema sizes and granularities (BCS slopes: $p = 0.60$; DocRED: slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), and directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts; (4)~per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas; (5)~NER decomposition fails due to merge amplification; RE confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) despite oracle routing recovering +29.8\\,pp."," "," Our contributions are:"," \\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt,topsep=2pt]"]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01C1AUvJcqhFr7yK4njbmWn3","type":"tool_result","content":"The file /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/introduction.tex has been updated successfully."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"fc48671c-c849-4aca-bbdb-a97a0fd3cc13","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:05:29.309Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/introduction.tex","oldString":"    \\item \\textbf{Count dominates confusability by ${\\sim}20\\times$ in typical batches.} Through stratified design isolating count from confusability, we show count effects ($-$42\\% to $-$56\\% recall) dwarf typical confusability effects (2.2\\,pp), counter to decomposition literature's implicit assumption that confusability is the primary bottleneck. Interaction is bounded (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) in RE but not in NER, where boundary and type ambiguity couple (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}).","newString":"    \\item \\textbf{Count dominates confusability by ${\\sim}20\\times$ in typical batches.} Stratified design shows count effects ($-$42\\% to $-$56\\% recall) dwarf typical confusability effects (2.2\\,pp). Association is bounded in RE but not in NER, where boundary and type ambiguity couple (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}).","originalFile":"\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nTraining-free\\footnote{We use ``training-free'' and ``zero-shot'' interchangeably to denote prompting without parameter updates.}\\footnote{Code and data will be released upon acceptance. Anonymous review materials: \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/schema-dag-extraction}} LLM information extraction (prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning) enables rapid deployment across new domains and ontologies. This zero-shot paradigm eliminates the need for labeled data, making it attractive for applications where annotation is expensive or schemas evolve frequently \\cite{llm4ie-survey-2024}. Yet the paradigm carries a critical limitation: performance degrades sharply as the number of candidate types in the prompt schema grows. On named entity recognition, recall drops from 0.72 with a single candidate type to a plateau of ${\\sim}$0.27 across intermediate schema sizes (22--44 types) before partial recovery at full-schema. On relation extraction, accuracy falls from 85.7\\% with one relation to 49.5\\% with 80 relations (sentence-level) and from 88.0\\% to 38.7\\% with 96 relations (document-level)---near or exceeding halving. This degradation is not academic: real-world ontologies routinely define 50--200+ types, placing them squarely in the regime where training-free extraction becomes unreliable.\n\nWhat explains this degradation? Prior decomposition strategies \\cite{uniner-2024,llm-pee-2025,parse-2025} conflate two factors: having more types to discriminate among (count) and having semantically similar types to confuse (confusability) (hereafter Dimension~1 and Dimension~2). None isolates which factor predicts the gains (\\S\\ref{sec:related_work}).\n\nThis study combines correlational analyses with quasi-experimental manipulation (BCS-controlled batches) to characterize how schema properties predict extraction accuracy.\n\nWe ask three questions. \\textbf{RQ1}: Does type confusability independently predict extraction degradation beyond the effect of type count? \\textbf{RQ2}: Through what mechanisms do count and confusability each degrade performance? \\textbf{RQ3}: Can confusability-aware schema decomposition improve extraction quality?\n\nTo our knowledge, we present the first controlled study isolating type confusability from type count via stratified design in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE, and event detection (ED). We operationalize confusability via the \\emph{Schema Confusability Score} (SCS), defined as pairwise embedding cosine similarity, and its batch-level aggregation, the \\emph{Batch Confusability Score} (BCS). We evaluate on Few-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021}, FewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018}, DocRED \\cite{docred-2019}, and MAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} using Qwen3-32B (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental_setup}).\n\nOur experiments yield five findings: (1)~degradation operates along two dimensions: count-associated recall decline (up to $-$49\\,pp at intermediate schema sizes for document-level RE) and confusability-associated type substitution (typical-batch gap ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp; quartile $\\Delta = -5.3$\\,pp), an asymmetric relationship where count dominates, consistent with bounded association in RE (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) but coupled in NER where NER recall exhibits non-monotonic recovery at full-schema; (2)~pairwise SCS predicts type confusion ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), the first factor empirically isolated in controlled zero-shot IE; (3)~effects are consistent across schema sizes and granularities (BCS slopes: $p = 0.60$; DocRED: slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), and directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts; (4)~per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas; (5)~NER decomposition fails due to merge amplification; RE confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) despite oracle routing recovering +29.8\\,pp.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt,topsep=2pt]\n    \\item \\textbf{Count dominates confusability by ${\\sim}20\\times$ in typical batches.} Through stratified design isolating count from confusability, we show count effects ($-$42\\% to $-$56\\% recall) dwarf typical confusability effects (2.2\\,pp), counter to decomposition literature's implicit assumption that confusability is the primary bottleneck. Interaction is bounded (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) in RE but not in NER, where boundary and type ambiguity couple (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}).\n\\item \\textbf{Pairwise embedding similarity predicts type confusion.} SCS correlates with confusion rates ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), with centrality confound identified on homogeneous schemas---invalidating naive per-type aggregation (\\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}).\n\\item \\textbf{BCS generalizes across tasks.} BCS--accuracy slopes are consistent across schema sizes ($p = 0.60$) and replicate at document level (DocRED: $R^2 = 0.22\\text{--}0.53$) (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\\item \\textbf{Schema decomposition exposes systematic routing failure.} Naive merge strategies (union, confidence-gated) collapse NER precision from 0.56 to 0.21. On RE, we evaluate seven routing strategies (oracle, five practical heuristics, random); all practical strategies fall below the single-pass baseline (best 36.5\\% vs.\\ 50.4\\%), because constrained decoding eliminates reliable sub-schema confidence signals. Oracle routing recovers +29.8\\,pp, but confusability-aware grouping adds only +1.4\\,pp ($p \\approx 0.19$) over random, leaving deployable routing as an open challenge (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}).\n\\end{enumerate}","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":15,"oldLines":7,"newStart":15,"newLines":7,"lines":[" "," Our contributions are:"," \\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt,topsep=2pt]","-    \\item \\textbf{Count dominates confusability by ${\\sim}20\\times$ in typical batches.} Through stratified design isolating count from confusability, we show count effects ($-$42\\% to $-$56\\% recall) dwarf typical confusability effects (2.2\\,pp), counter to decomposition literature's implicit assumption that confusability is the primary bottleneck. Interaction is bounded (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) in RE but not in NER, where boundary and type ambiguity couple (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}).","+    \\item \\textbf{Count dominates confusability by ${\\sim}20\\times$ in typical batches.} Stratified design shows count effects ($-$42\\% to $-$56\\% recall) dwarf typical confusability effects (2.2\\,pp). Association is bounded in RE but not in NER, where boundary and type ambiguity couple (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel})."," \\item \\textbf{Pairwise embedding similarity predicts type confusion.} SCS correlates with confusion rates ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), with centrality confound identified on homogeneous schemas---invalidating naive per-type aggregation (\\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation})."," \\item \\textbf{BCS generalizes across tasks.} BCS--accuracy slopes are consistent across schema sizes ($p = 0.60$) and replicate at document level (DocRED: $R^2 = 0.22\\text{--}0.53$) (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled})."," \\item \\textbf{Schema decomposition exposes systematic routing failure.} Naive merge strategies (union, confidence-gated) collapse NER precision from 0.56 to 0.21. On RE, we evaluate seven routing strategies (oracle, five practical heuristics, random); all practical strategies fall below the single-pass baseline (best 36.5\\% vs.\\ 50.4\\%), because constrained decoding eliminates reliable sub-schema confidence signals. Oracle routing recovers +29.8\\,pp, but confusability-aware grouping adds only +1.4\\,pp ($p \\approx 0.19$) over random, leaving deployable routing as an open challenge (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd})."]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

Anonymous review materials: \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/schema-dag-extraction}} LLM information extraction (prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning) enables rapid deployment across new domains and ontologies. On relation extraction, accuracy falls from 85.7\\% with one relation to 49.5\\% with 80 relations (sentence-level) and from 88.0\\% to 38.7\\% with 96 relations (document-level)---near or exceeding halving. We operationalize confusability via the \\emph{Schema Confusability Score} (SCS), defined as pairwise embedding cosine similarity, and its batch-level aggregation, the \\emph{Batch Confusability Score} (BCS). We evaluate on Few-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021}, FewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018}, DocRED \\cite{docred-2019}, and MAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} using Qwen3-32B (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental_setup}).\n\nOur experiments yield five findings: (1)~degradation operates along two dimensions: count-associated recall decline (up to $-$49\\,pp at intermediate schema sizes for document-level RE) and confusability-associated type substitution (typical-batch gap ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp; quartile $\\Delta = -5.3$\\,pp), an asymmetric relationship where count dominates, consistent with bounded association in RE (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) but coupled in NER where NER recall exhibits non-monotonic recovery at full-schema; (2)~pairwise SCS predicts type confusion ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), the first factor empirically isolated in controlled zero-shot IE; (3)~effects are consistent across schema sizes and granularities (BCS slopes: $p = 0.60$; DocRED: slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), and directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts; (4)~per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas; (5)~NER decomposition fails due to merge amplification; RE confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) despite oracle routing recovering +29.8\\,pp.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt,topsep=2pt]\n    \\item \\textbf{Count dominates confusability by ${\\sim}20\\times$ in typical batches.} Stratified design shows count effects ($-$42\\% to $-$56\\% recall) dwarf typical confusability effects (2.2\\,pp). Association is bounded in RE but not in NER, where boundary and type ambiguity couple (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}).\n\\item \\textbf{Pairwise embedding similarity predicts type confusion.} SCS correlates with confusion rates ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), with centrality confound identified on homogeneous schemas---invalidating naive per-type aggregation (\\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}).\n\\item \\textbf{BCS generalizes across tasks.} BCS--accuracy slopes are consistent across schema sizes ($p = 0.60$) and replicate at document level (DocRED: $R^2 = 0.22\\text{--}0.53$) (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\\item \\textbf{Schema decomposition exposes systematic routing failure.} Naive merge strategies (union, confidence-gated) collapse NER precision from 0.56 to 0.21. None isolates which factor predicts the gains (\\S\\ref{sec:related_work}).\n\nThis study combines correlational analyses with quasi-experimental manipulation (BCS-controlled batches) to characterize how schema properties predict extraction accuracy.\n\nWe ask three questions. \\textbf{RQ1}: Does type confusability independently predict extraction degradation beyond the effect of type count? \\textbf{RQ2}: Through what mechanisms do count and confusability each degrade performance? \\textbf{RQ3}: Can confusability-aware schema decomposition improve extraction quality?\n\nTo our knowledge, we present the first controlled study isolating type confusability from type count via stratified design in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE, and event detection (ED). We operationalize confusability via the \\emph{Schema Confusability Score} (SCS), defined as pairwise embedding cosine similarity, and its batch-level aggregation, the \\emph{Batch Confusability Score} (BCS). We evaluate on Few-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021}, FewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018}, DocRED \\cite{docred-2019}, and MAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} using Qwen3-32B (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental_setup}).\n\nOur experiments yield five findings: (1)~degradation operates along two dimensions: count-associated recall decline (up to $-$49\\,pp at intermediate schema sizes for document-level RE) and confusability-associated type substitution (typical-batch gap ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp; quartile $\\Delta = -5.3$\\,pp), an asymmetric relationship where count dominates, consistent with bounded association in RE (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) but coupled in NER where NER recall exhibits non-monotonic recovery at full-schema; (2)~pairwise SCS predicts type confusion ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), the first factor empirically isolated in controlled zero-shot IE; (3)~effects are consistent across schema sizes and granularities (BCS slopes: $p = 0.60$; DocRED: slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), and directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts; (4)~per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas; (5)~NER decomposition fails due to merge amplification; RE confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) despite oracle routing recovering +29.8\\,pp.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt,topsep=2pt]\n    \\item \\textbf{Count dominates confusability by ${\\sim}20\\times$ in typical batches.} Stratified design shows count effects ($-$42\\% to $-$56\\% recall) dwarf typical confusability effects (2.2\\,pp). Association is bounded in RE but not in NER, where boundary and type ambiguity couple (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}).\n\\item \\textbf{First controlled validation that embedding similarity predicts type confusion in zero-shot IE.} SCS correlates with confusion rates ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), with centrality confound identified on homogeneous schemas, invalidating naive per-type aggregation (\\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}).\n\\item \\textbf{BCS generalizes across tasks.} BCS--accuracy slopes are consistent across schema sizes ($p = 0.60$) and replicate at document level (DocRED: $R^2 = 0.22\\text{--}0.53$) (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\\item \\textbf{Schema decomposition exposes systematic routing failure.} Naive merge strategies (union, confidence-gated) collapse NER precision from 0.56 to 0.21. On RE, we evaluate seven routing strategies (oracle, five practical heuristics, random); all practical strategies fall below the single-pass baseline (best 36.5\\% vs.\\ 50.4\\%), because constrained decoding eliminates reliable sub-schema confidence signals. Oracle routing recovers +29.8\\,pp, but confusability-aware grouping adds only +1.4\\,pp ($p \\approx 0.19$) over random, leaving deployable routing as an open challenge (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}).\n\\end{enumerate}","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":17,"oldLines":6,"newStart":17,"newLines":6,"lines":[" \\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt,topsep=2pt]","     \\item \\textbf{Count dominates confusability by ${\\sim}20\\times$ in typical batches.} Stratified design shows count effects ($-$42\\% to $-$56\\% recall) dwarf typical confusability effects (2.2\\,pp). Association is bounded in RE but not in NER, where boundary and type ambiguity couple (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel})."," \\item \\textbf{First controlled validation that embedding similarity predicts type confusion in zero-shot IE.} SCS correlates with confusion rates ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), with centrality confound identified on homogeneous schemas, invalidating naive per-type aggregation (\\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}).","-\\item \\textbf{BCS generalizes across tasks.} BCS--accuracy slopes are consistent across schema sizes ($p = 0.60$) and replicate at document level (DocRED: $R^2 = 0.22\\text{--}0.53$) (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).","+\\item \\textbf{BCS generalizes across RE tasks and schema sizes.} BCS--accuracy slopes are consistent across schema sizes ($p = 0.60$) and replicate at document level (DocRED: $R^2 = 0.22\\text{--}0.53$); applicability to span-level tasks (NER, ED) requires confound adjustment (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled})."," \\item \\textbf{Schema decomposition exposes systematic routing failure.} Naive merge strategies (union, confidence-gated) collapse NER precision from 0.56 to 0.21. On RE, we evaluate seven routing strategies (oracle, five practical heuristics, random); all practical strategies fall below the single-pass baseline (best 36.5\\% vs.\\ 50.4\\%), because constrained decoding eliminates reliable sub-schema confidence signals. Oracle routing recovers +29.8\\,pp, but confusability-aware grouping adds only +1.4\\,pp ($p \\approx 0.19$) over random, leaving deployable routing as an open challenge (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd})."," \\end{enumerate}","\\ No newline at end of file"]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01YYxK415vAXYEEQoThzmTzD","type":"tool_result","content":"The file /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/introduction.tex has been updated successfully."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"5fd839d8-6ed1-413a-86e5-ce8014d44bcd","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:05:54.109Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/introduction.tex","oldString":"\\item \\textbf{Schema decomposition exposes systematic routing failure.} Naive merge strategies (union, confidence-gated) collapse NER precision from 0.56 to 0.21. On RE, we evaluate seven routing strategies (oracle, five practical heuristics, random); all practical strategies fall below the single-pass baseline (best 36.5\\% vs.\\ 50.4\\%), because constrained decoding eliminates reliable sub-schema confidence signals. Oracle routing recovers +29.8\\,pp, but confusability-aware grouping adds only +1.4\\,pp ($p \\approx 0.19$) over random, leaving deployable routing as an open challenge (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}).","newString":"\\item \\textbf{Schema decomposition exposes systematic routing failure.} NER merge strategies collapse precision; all practical RE routing strategies fall below the single-pass baseline despite oracle routing recovering +29.8\\,pp, because constrained decoding eliminates reliable confidence signals (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}).","originalFile":"\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nTraining-free\\footnote{We use ``training-free'' and ``zero-shot'' interchangeably to denote prompting without parameter updates.}\\footnote{Code and data will be released upon acceptance. Anonymous review materials: \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/schema-dag-extraction}} LLM information extraction (prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning) enables rapid deployment across new domains and ontologies. This zero-shot paradigm eliminates the need for labeled data, making it attractive for applications where annotation is expensive or schemas evolve frequently \\cite{llm4ie-survey-2024}. Yet the paradigm carries a critical limitation: performance degrades sharply as the number of candidate types in the prompt schema grows. On named entity recognition, recall drops from 0.72 with a single candidate type to a plateau of ${\\sim}$0.27 across intermediate schema sizes (22--44 types) before partial recovery at full-schema. On relation extraction, accuracy falls from 85.7\\% with one relation to 49.5\\% with 80 relations (sentence-level) and from 88.0\\% to 38.7\\% with 96 relations (document-level)---near or exceeding halving. This degradation is not academic: real-world ontologies routinely define 50--200+ types, placing them squarely in the regime where training-free extraction becomes unreliable.\n\nWhat explains this degradation? Prior decomposition strategies \\cite{uniner-2024,llm-pee-2025,parse-2025} conflate two factors: having more types to discriminate among (count) and having semantically similar types to confuse (confusability) (hereafter Dimension~1 and Dimension~2). None isolates which factor predicts the gains (\\S\\ref{sec:related_work}).\n\nThis study combines correlational analyses with quasi-experimental manipulation (BCS-controlled batches) to characterize how schema properties predict extraction accuracy.\n\nWe ask three questions. \\textbf{RQ1}: Does type confusability independently predict extraction degradation beyond the effect of type count? \\textbf{RQ2}: Through what mechanisms do count and confusability each degrade performance? \\textbf{RQ3}: Can confusability-aware schema decomposition improve extraction quality?\n\nTo our knowledge, we present the first controlled study isolating type confusability from type count via stratified design in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE, and event detection (ED). We operationalize confusability via the \\emph{Schema Confusability Score} (SCS), defined as pairwise embedding cosine similarity, and its batch-level aggregation, the \\emph{Batch Confusability Score} (BCS). We evaluate on Few-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021}, FewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018}, DocRED \\cite{docred-2019}, and MAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} using Qwen3-32B (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental_setup}).\n\nOur experiments yield five findings: (1)~degradation operates along two dimensions: count-associated recall decline (up to $-$49\\,pp at intermediate schema sizes for document-level RE) and confusability-associated type substitution (typical-batch gap ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp; quartile $\\Delta = -5.3$\\,pp), an asymmetric relationship where count dominates, consistent with bounded association in RE (TOST $p = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp) but coupled in NER where NER recall exhibits non-monotonic recovery at full-schema; (2)~pairwise SCS predicts type confusion ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), the first factor empirically isolated in controlled zero-shot IE; (3)~effects are consistent across schema sizes and granularities (BCS slopes: $p = 0.60$; DocRED: slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), and directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts; (4)~per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas; (5)~NER decomposition fails due to merge amplification; RE confusability-aware grouping yields no significant improvement over random (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) despite oracle routing recovering +29.8\\,pp.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt,topsep=2pt]\n    \\item \\textbf{Count dominates confusability by ${\\sim}20\\times$ in typical batches.} Stratified design shows count effects ($-$42\\% to $-$56\\% recall) dwarf typical confusability effects (2.2\\,pp). Association is bounded in RE but not in NER, where boundary and type ambiguity couple (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}).\n\\item \\textbf{First controlled validation that embedding similarity predicts type confusion in zero-shot IE.} SCS correlates with confusion rates ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), with centrality confound identified on homogeneous schemas, invalidating naive per-type aggregation (\\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}).\n\\item \\textbf{BCS generalizes across RE tasks and schema sizes.} BCS--accuracy slopes are consistent across schema sizes ($p = 0.60$) and replicate at document level (DocRED: $R^2 = 0.22\\text{--}0.53$); applicability to span-level tasks (NER, ED) requires confound adjustment (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\\item \\textbf{Schema decomposition exposes systematic routing failure.} Naive merge strategies (union, confidence-gated) collapse NER precision from 0.56 to 0.21. On RE, we evaluate seven routing strategies (oracle, five practical heuristics, random); all practical strategies fall below the single-pass baseline (best 36.5\\% vs.\\ 50.4\\%), because constrained decoding eliminates reliable sub-schema confidence signals. Oracle routing recovers +29.8\\,pp, but confusability-aware grouping adds only +1.4\\,pp ($p \\approx 0.19$) over random, leaving deployable routing as an open challenge (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}).\n\\end{enumerate}","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":18,"oldLines":5,"newStart":18,"newLines":5,"lines":["     \\item \\textbf{Count dominates confusability by ${\\sim}20\\times$ in typical batches.} Stratified design shows count effects ($-$42\\% to $-$56\\% recall) dwarf typical confusability effects (2.2\\,pp). Association is bounded in RE but not in NER, where boundary and type ambiguity couple (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel})."," \\item \\textbf{First controlled validation that embedding similarity predicts type confusion in zero-shot IE.} SCS correlates with confusion rates ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), with centrality confound identified on homogeneous schemas, invalidating naive per-type aggregation (\\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation})."," \\item \\textbf{BCS generalizes across RE tasks and schema sizes.} BCS--accuracy slopes are consistent across schema sizes ($p = 0.60$) and replicate at document level (DocRED: $R^2 = 0.22\\text{--}0.53$); applicability to span-level tasks (NER, ED) requires confound adjustment (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).","-\\item \\textbf{Schema decomposition exposes systematic routing failure.} Naive merge strategies (union, confidence-gated) collapse NER precision from 0.56 to 0.21. On RE, we evaluate seven routing strategies (oracle, five practical heuristics, random); all practical strategies fall below the single-pass baseline (best 36.5\\% vs.\\ 50.4\\%), because constrained decoding eliminates reliable sub-schema confidence signals. Oracle routing recovers +29.8\\,pp, but confusability-aware grouping adds only +1.4\\,pp ($p \\approx 0.19$) over random, leaving deployable routing as an open challenge (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}).","+\\item \\textbf{Schema decomposition exposes systematic routing failure.} NER merge strategies collapse precision; all practical RE routing strategies fall below the single-pass baseline despite oracle routing recovering +29.8\\,pp, because constrained decoding eliminates reliable confidence signals (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd})."," \\end{enumerate}","\\ No newline at end of file"]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

Count-associated degradation","originalFile":"\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Boundary conditions.}\nThe decomposition applies to cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas exhibit centrality confounding (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). High-BCS accuracy converges to $\\approx$51\\% across models, suggesting a difficulty ceiling. RE's separation may partly reflect its closed-set format; the effect generalizes to document-level RE (DocRED slope $-$0.71). Prompt design modulates but does not eliminate the effect (v2 gap: $-$25.5\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}). Cross-scale evidence (32B vs.\\ 8B) shows Channel~1 replicates fully while Channel~2 attenuates on smaller models, dropping to near-zero $R^2$ for NER on Llama-3.1-8B (Tables~\\ref{tab:cross_model}--\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Count-driven degradation may partly reflect prompt length (per-type recall 0.586 vs.\\ single-pass 0.368, consistent with lost-in-middle effects; \\citealt{lost-in-middle-2024}).\n\n\\paragraph{Why does embedding similarity predict LLM confusion?}\nSCS captures semantic proximity in LLMs' type-resolution space. The unconditional pair-level effect is modest ($r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$, as ${\\sim}89\\%$ of pairs never produce mutual confusion); among active-confusion pairs, $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ amplifies ${\\sim}3\\times$ at batch level (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim}), yielding $R^2$ of 22--53\\%. The NER U-shape (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}) reflects a dual-task burden absent in RE: partial schemas require simultaneous span detection and type-membership filtering; at full-schema, filtering becomes vacuous and recall recovers, confirming task-specific mechanistic pathways.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nSchema-induced degradation in training-free LLM extraction operates through two channels (count-driven recall decline, confusability-driven type confusion) with bounded interaction in RE but coupling in NER. Channel~1 replicates across scales; Channel~2 attenuates on smaller models. BCS enables pre-hoc screening.\n","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":2,"oldLines":7,"newStart":2,"newLines":7,"lines":[" \\label{sec:discussion}"," "," \\paragraph{Boundary conditions.}","-The decomposition applies to cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas exhibit centrality confounding (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). RE's separation may partly reflect its closed-set format; the effect generalizes to document-level RE (DocRED slope $-$0.71). Prompt design modulates but does not eliminate the effect (v2 gap: $-$25.5\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}). Cross-scale evidence (32B vs.\\ 8B) shows Channel~1 replicates fully while Channel~2 attenuates on smaller models, dropping to near-zero $R^2$ for NER on Llama-3.1-8B (Tables~\\ref{tab:cross_model}--\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Cross-scale evidence (32B vs.\\ 8B) shows Dimension~1 replicates fully while Dimension~2 attenuates on smaller models, dropping to near-zero $R^2$ for NER on Llama-3.1-8B (Tables~\\ref{tab:cross_model}--\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Count-associated degradation may partly reflect prompt length (per-type recall 0.586 vs.\\ single-pass 0.368, consistent with lost-in-middle effects; \\citealt{lost-in-middle-2024})."," "," \\paragraph{Why does embedding similarity predict LLM confusion?}"," SCS captures semantic proximity in LLMs' type-resolution space. {"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-opus-4-6","id":"msg_01XeD8qg9LbnfEtpCjFqYzEH","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_019EhHvBGZPHXUd2fCQBbzQ8","name":"Edit","input":{"replace_all":false,"file_path":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/discussion.tex","old_string":"task-specific mechanistic pathways","new_string":"task-specific pathways"},"caller":{"type":"direct"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"stop_details":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":1,"cache_creation_input_tokens":408,"cache_read_input_tokens":89811,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":0,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":408},"output_tokens":72,"service_tier":"standard","inference_geo":"not_available"},"diagnostics":null,"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"2adc6aaa-a4b0-43f0-8190-d89b29d5af3e"}
{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_019EhHvBGZPHXUd2fCQBbzQ8","type":"tool_result","content":"The file /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/discussion.tex has been updated successfully."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"8ce0090e-7d6b-4e07-8b9d-1b1b674a73ef","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:06:03.704Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/discussion.tex","oldString":"task-specific mechanistic pathways","newString":"task-specific pathways","originalFile":"\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Boundary conditions.}\nThe decomposition applies to cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas exhibit centrality confounding (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). High-BCS accuracy converges to $\\approx$51\\% across models, suggesting a difficulty ceiling. RE's separation may partly reflect its closed-set format; the effect generalizes to document-level RE (DocRED slope $-$0.71). Prompt design modulates but does not eliminate the effect (v2 gap: $-$25.5\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}). Cross-scale evidence (32B vs.\\ 8B) shows Dimension~1 replicates fully while Dimension~2 attenuates on smaller models, dropping to near-zero $R^2$ for NER on Llama-3.1-8B (Tables~\\ref{tab:cross_model}--\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Count-associated degradation may partly reflect prompt length (per-type recall 0.586 vs.\\ single-pass 0.368, consistent with lost-in-middle effects; \\citealt{lost-in-middle-2024}).\n\n\\paragraph{Why does embedding similarity predict LLM confusion?}\nSCS captures semantic proximity in LLMs' type-resolution space. The unconditional pair-level effect is modest ($r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$, as ${\\sim}89\\%$ of pairs never produce mutual confusion); among active-confusion pairs, $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ amplifies ${\\sim}3\\times$ at batch level (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim}), yielding $R^2$ of 22--53\\%. The NER U-shape (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}) reflects a dual-task burden absent in RE: partial schemas require simultaneous span detection and type-membership filtering; at full-schema, filtering becomes vacuous and recall recovers, confirming task-specific mechanistic pathways.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nSchema-induced degradation in training-free LLM extraction operates through two channels (count-driven recall decline, confusability-driven type confusion) with bounded interaction in RE but coupling in NER. Channel~1 replicates across scales; Channel~2 attenuates on smaller models. BCS enables pre-hoc screening.\n","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":5,"oldLines":7,"newStart":5,"newLines":7,"lines":[" The decomposition applies to cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas exhibit centrality confounding (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). RE's separation may partly reflect its closed-set format; the effect generalizes to document-level RE (DocRED slope $-$0.71). Prompt design modulates but does not eliminate the effect (v2 gap: $-$25.5\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}). Cross-scale evidence (32B vs.\\ 8B) shows Dimension~1 replicates fully while Dimension~2 attenuates on smaller models, dropping to near-zero $R^2$ for NER on Llama-3.1-8B (Tables~\\ref{tab:cross_model}--\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Count-associated degradation may partly reflect prompt length (per-type recall 0.586 vs.\\ single-pass 0.368, consistent with lost-in-middle effects; \\citealt{lost-in-middle-2024})."," "," \\paragraph{Why does embedding similarity predict LLM confusion?}","-SCS captures semantic proximity in LLMs' type-resolution space. The NER U-shape (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}) reflects a dual-task burden absent in RE: partial schemas require simultaneous span detection and type-membership filtering; at full-schema, filtering becomes vacuous and recall recovers, confirming task-specific mechanistic pathways.","+SCS captures semantic proximity in LLMs' type-resolution space. The NER U-shape (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}) reflects a dual-task burden absent in RE: partial schemas require simultaneous span detection and type-membership filtering; at full-schema, filtering becomes vacuous and recall recovers, confirming task-specific pathways."," "," \\section{Conclusion}"," \\label{sec:conclusion}"]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_016gd4KnLQAKFKzJNBk2gQBb","type":"tool_result","content":"The file /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/results.tex has been updated successfully."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"17694c4a-80f0-4029-80c4-d2384196c736","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:06:14.376Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/results.tex","oldString":"confusion rate---the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$","newString":"confusion rate (the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$)","originalFile":"\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nUnless otherwise noted, all experiments in this section use the v1 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).\n\n\\subsection{Count-Degradation Curves}\n\\label{sec:count_curves}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\columnwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_count_curves.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\columnwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_bcs_scaling.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\caption{\\textbf{(a)} Count-degradation curves for NER and RE. Error bars show $\\pm 1$\\,SD across 3 seeds (42, 123, 456) $\\times$ 25 batches per seed. RE accuracy declines monotonically with count; NER recall exhibits a U-shaped pattern with minimum at intermediate counts. Full-schema points (rightmost, hollow markers) represent the complete type inventory with no batch sampling variance. \\textbf{(b)} BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. The BCS--accuracy slope is consistent across the independent 16-relation pool and within-pool 16-relation subsets (permutation $p = 0.60$); the wider BCS range available at 80 relations produces larger absolute gaps.}\n\\label{fig:main_results}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:main_results}a shows RE accuracy declines monotonically with schema size (0.86$\\to$0.50 for FewRel). NER recall declines through intermediate counts (0.51$\\to$0.27, plateauing across counts 22--44) before partial recovery at full-schema (0.41), where the elimination of type-filtering overhead restores extraction performance. DocRED replicates the monotonic RE pattern (88.0\\%$\\to$38.7\\%, $-$56\\%; Appendix). MAVEN exhibits a reversed trend attributable to centrality confounding (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). The effect replicates on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($-$43\\,pp on Few-NERD; per-batch $r = 0.725$; Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}), indicating schema configuration rather than model-specific factors predicts difficulty. The pattern replicates across three seeds (42, 123, 456; max $\\Delta = 10.7$\\,pp, within 1\\,SD); intra-batch variance exceeds inter-seed variance by ${>}$5$\\times$ at every count level (Appendix~\\ref{app:variance-decomposition}). Formal trend analysis confirms monotonic RE decline (Jonckheere-Terpstra $J = 109{,}707$, $p < 0.001$, pooled across three seeds). Count curves (25 batches per level) establish trend direction with error bars; magnitude estimation relies on the higher-powered BCS-controlled experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\n\\subsection{Pairwise Confusability and Type Confusion}\n\\label{sec:pairwise}\n\nWe test whether pairwise SCS predicts type-level confusion rates. For each type pair $(t_i, t_j)$, we compute the confusion rate---the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$---and correlate it with $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)$. Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Pearson correlations with bootstrap 95\\% CIs.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Embed.} & \\textbf{$n$ pairs} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$r^2$} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & Qwen3 & 1{,}953 & 0.112 & 0.013 & {[}0.076, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMAVEN & MiniLM & 11{,}026 & 0.129 & 0.017 & {[}0.104, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Pairwise SCS--confusion correlation. $r^2$ indicates the fraction of variance in type confusion explained by embedding similarity. Both correlations are modest in magnitude but robust across datasets and embedding models (both $p \\ll 0.001$). Confusion rate for each ordered pair $(t_i, t_j)$ is computed as the total bidirectional confusion count $C(t_i{\\to}t_j) + C(t_j{\\to}t_i)$ divided by the sum of gold frequencies, aggregated over all count conditions; CIs are pair-level percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$). FewRel ($r = 0.187$; \\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}) is excluded as RE's per-instance format precludes direct comparison of confusion rates. Type-level cluster bootstrap CIs: Few-NERD [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN [0.081, 0.185]; both exclude zero, confirming robustness to within-type correlation.}\n\\label{tab:pairwise}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe correlations are statistically significant but modest: $r^2 \\approx 1.3\\text{--}1.7\\%$. Type-level cluster bootstrap yields wider but still significant CIs (Few-NERD: [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN: [0.081, 0.185]; $B = 10{,}000$). Both datasets show significant positive correlations between pairwise SCS and type confusion rates. Spearman correlations corroborate: $\\rho = 0.144\\text{--}0.227$ across all pairs, strengthening to $\\rho = 0.27\\text{--}0.31$ among pairs with nonzero confusion (though conditioning on the dependent variable may inflate this).\n\nThe remaining variance likely reflects entity frequency, prompt position \\cite{lost-in-middle-2024}, mention ambiguity, and definition quality. Although pairwise $r^2$ is modest, the signal compounds at the batch level into quartile gaps of 3.9--5.3\\,pp under typical batching (\\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}). Notably, the independent MiniLM embedding yields a \\emph{stronger} SCS--confusion correlation on Few-NERD ($r = 0.172$ vs.\\ Qwen3 $r = 0.138$; Appendix), suggesting that shared-representation bias is conservative rather than inflationary.\n\n\\subsection{BCS-Controlled Experiments}\n\\label{sec:bcs_controlled}\n\nWe fix type count and vary BCS. FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per type) eliminates type frequency as a confound; the within-pool analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:within_pool}) controls for pool composition.\n\nIn BCS-controlled FewRel subsets, the high-BCS quartile shows $\\Delta=-5.3$\\,pp lower accuracy than low-BCS (quartile comparison; full data in Appendix~\\ref{app:bcs-controlled-table}). At 80 relations, extreme-batch construction amplifies this to $-$31.2\\,pp (CI: [17.7, 42.7]), stable across count levels. An intermediate count${}\\,=\\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\\,pp at count 16), confirming monotonic decline.\n\n\\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}\nThe effect replicates on DocRED's 96-relation schema: BCS gap $-$23.3\\,pp at count 16 (slope $= -0.713$, $\\rho = -0.661$, $p < 0.001$) and $-$17.6\\,pp at count 32 (slope $= -0.690$, $\\rho = -0.509$, $p = 0.002$). BCS explains 44--53\\% of cross-batch variance ($R^2 = 0.525$ at count 16), substantially higher than FewRel ($R^2 = 0.218$), possibly reflecting a wider confusability range in the DocRED schema, though document-level context may also provide richer cues for type confusion. Across all RE settings, BCS slopes range from $-$0.40 to $-$0.71, significant in all conditions with sufficient BCS range.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-pool controlled comparison.}\n\\label{sec:within_pool}\nWithin-pool analysis confirms slope consistency across pools (permutation $p \\approx 0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:within-pool-method}). The 80-relation extreme-batch gap (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) reflects wider BCS range, not stronger per-unit sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}\n\\label{sec:practical_magnitude}\nFor representative batches from the 80-relation pool ($n{=}100$ random 16-type subsets), the typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\\,pp (95\\% CI: [0.4, 4.0])---roughly 7\\% of the extreme-batch gap. Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\\% vs.\\ 78.9\\% for top-quartile ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp). The 31.2\\,pp figure reflects maximum BCS contrast, not the typical batch experience. Conditional pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ among non-zero confusion pairs (vs.\\ unconditional $r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$ across all pairs; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}; we note the conditioning inflates this estimate) aggregates to batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ ($\\sim$3$\\times$ amplification, consistent with a Monte Carlo simulation accounting for inter-pair correlation structure; see Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim} for the illustrative aggregation model).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}\nThe effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: BCS gaps of $-$27.1\\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\\% of Qwen3-32B's gap. Both models converge to $\\approx$51\\% under high confusability regardless of count, while divergence is confined to the low-BCS regime---suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor under high confusability (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Channel~1 replicates fully on Llama-3.1-8B (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}); Channel~2 replicates on RE but shows floor effects on 8B NER (Limitations, item~1).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}\nUnder the v2 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; $n{=}25$; $r = -0.919$; $p < 0.001$)---modestly attenuated from v1's extreme-batch gap but highly significant. NER v2 yields consistent batch-level correlations ($r = +0.58$ vs.\\ $r = +0.50$ under v1). Prompt design modulates magnitude but does not eliminate the effect (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}). The BCS--accuracy correlation holds across four structurally distinct prompt variants at count 32 (all $r < -0.47$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}), confirming that the confusability effect is not an artifact of prompt design.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Channel Degradation}\n\\label{sec:multi_channel}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}\n\\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition. IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS in both tasks. RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic.}\n\\label{fig:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{figure}\n\nError taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.\n\nThese results establish two channels: \\textbf{Channel~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \\textbf{Channel~2} (confusability predicts type confusion), consistent with bounded interaction in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact. The full-range regression ($n = 69$, five count levels) serves as the primary separability test; the $2 \\times 2$ factorial ($n = 54$) as a sensitivity check. TOST equivalence testing confirms bounded interaction (interaction = 0.47\\,pp, $p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}).\n\n\\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}\n\\label{sec:centrality_confound}\n\nPer-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \\emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation (Qwen3 $\\rho = +0.184$, $p = 0.025$; MiniLM $\\rho = +0.208$, $p = 0.011$). This traces to same-domain embedding collapse \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}: MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly, so mean BCS conflates confusability with centrality---high-BCS types are semantically central and easy to extract. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the positive correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}). The pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}); the problem is per-type aggregation, not the underlying relationship. Few-NERD's cross-domain schema does not exhibit this confound ($\\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$). Mean BCS is thus reliable only for cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas require pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment.","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":26,"oldLines":7,"newStart":26,"newLines":7,"lines":[" \\subsection{Pairwise Confusability and Type Confusion}"," \\label{sec:pairwise}"," ","-We test whether pairwise SCS predicts type-level confusion rates. For each type pair $(t_i, t_j)$, we compute the confusion rate---the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$---and correlate it with $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)$. Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Pearson correlations with bootstrap 95\\% CIs.","+We test whether pairwise SCS predicts type-level confusion rates. For each type pair $(t_i, t_j)$, we compute the confusion rate (the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$)---and correlate it with $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)$. Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Pearson correlations with bootstrap 95\\% CIs."," "," \\begin{table}[t]"," \\centering"]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

Error bars show $\\pm 1$\\,SD across 3 seeds (42, 123, 456) $\\times$ 25 batches per seed. RE accuracy declines monotonically with count; NER recall exhibits a U-shaped pattern with minimum at intermediate counts. Full-schema points (rightmost, hollow markers) represent the complete type inventory with no batch sampling variance. \\textbf{(b)} BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. The BCS--accuracy slope is consistent across the independent 16-relation pool and within-pool 16-relation subsets (permutation $p = 0.60$); the wider BCS range available at 80 relations produces larger absolute gaps.}\n\\label{fig:main_results}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:main_results}a shows RE accuracy declines monotonically with schema size (0.86$\\to$0.50 for FewRel). NER recall declines through intermediate counts (0.51$\\to$0.27, plateauing across counts 22--44) before partial recovery at full-schema (0.41), where the elimination of type-filtering overhead restores extraction performance. DocRED replicates the monotonic RE pattern (88.0\\%$\\to$38.7\\%, $-$56\\%; Appendix). MAVEN exhibits a reversed trend attributable to centrality confounding (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). The effect replicates on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($-$43\\,pp on Few-NERD; per-batch $r = 0.725$; Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}), indicating schema configuration rather than model-specific factors predicts difficulty. The pattern replicates across three seeds (42, 123, 456; max $\\Delta = 10.7$\\,pp, within 1\\,SD); intra-batch variance exceeds inter-seed variance by ${>}$5$\\times$ at every count level (Appendix~\\ref{app:variance-decomposition}). Formal trend analysis confirms monotonic RE decline (Jonckheere-Terpstra $J = 109{,}707$, $p < 0.001$, pooled across three seeds). Count curves (25 batches per level) establish trend direction with error bars; magnitude estimation relies on the higher-powered BCS-controlled experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\n\\subsection{Pairwise Confusability and Type Confusion}\n\\label{sec:pairwise}\n\nWe test whether pairwise SCS predicts type-level confusion rates. For each type pair $(t_i, t_j)$, we compute the confusion rate (the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$)---and correlate it with $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)$. Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Pearson correlations with bootstrap 95\\% CIs.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Embed.} & \\textbf{$n$ pairs} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$r^2$} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & Qwen3 & 1{,}953 & 0.112 & 0.013 & {[}0.076, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMAVEN & MiniLM & 11{,}026 & 0.129 & 0.017 & {[}0.104, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Pairwise SCS--confusion correlation. $r^2$ indicates the fraction of variance in type confusion explained by embedding similarity. Both correlations are modest in magnitude but robust across datasets and embedding models (both $p \\ll 0.001$). Confusion rate for each ordered pair $(t_i, t_j)$ is computed as the total bidirectional confusion count $C(t_i{\\to}t_j) + C(t_j{\\to}t_i)$ divided by the sum of gold frequencies, aggregated over all count conditions; CIs are pair-level percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$). FewRel ($r = 0.187$; \\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}) is excluded as RE's per-instance format precludes direct comparison of confusion rates. Type-level cluster bootstrap CIs: Few-NERD [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN [0.081, 0.185]; both exclude zero, confirming robustness to within-type correlation.}\n\\label{tab:pairwise}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe correlations are statistically significant but modest: $r^2 \\approx 1.3\\text{--}1.7\\%$. Type-level cluster bootstrap yields wider but still significant CIs (Few-NERD: [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN: [0.081, 0.185]; $B = 10{,}000$). Both datasets show significant positive correlations between pairwise SCS and type confusion rates. Spearman correlations corroborate: $\\rho = 0.144\\text{--}0.227$ across all pairs, strengthening to $\\rho = 0.27\\text{--}0.31$ among pairs with nonzero confusion (though conditioning on the dependent variable may inflate this).\n\nThe remaining variance likely reflects entity frequency, prompt position \\cite{lost-in-middle-2024}, mention ambiguity, and definition quality. Although pairwise $r^2$ is modest, the signal compounds at the batch level into quartile gaps of 3.9--5.3\\,pp under typical batching (\\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}). Notably, the independent MiniLM embedding yields a \\emph{stronger} SCS--confusion correlation on Few-NERD ($r = 0.172$ vs.\\ Qwen3 $r = 0.138$; Appendix), suggesting that shared-representation bias is conservative rather than inflationary.\n\n\\subsection{BCS-Controlled Experiments}\n\\label{sec:bcs_controlled}\n\nWe fix type count and vary BCS. FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per type) eliminates type frequency as a confound; the within-pool analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:within_pool}) controls for pool composition.\n\nIn BCS-controlled FewRel subsets, the high-BCS quartile shows $\\Delta=-5.3$\\,pp lower accuracy than low-BCS (quartile comparison; full data in Appendix~\\ref{app:bcs-controlled-table}). At 80 relations, extreme-batch construction amplifies this to $-$31.2\\,pp (CI: [17.7, 42.7]), stable across count levels. An intermediate count${}\\,=\\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\\,pp at count 16), confirming monotonic decline.\n\n\\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}\nThe effect replicates on DocRED's 96-relation schema: BCS gap $-$23.3\\,pp at count 16 (slope $= -0.713$, $\\rho = -0.661$, $p < 0.001$) and $-$17.6\\,pp at count 32 (slope $= -0.690$, $\\rho = -0.509$, $p = 0.002$). BCS explains 44--53\\% of cross-batch variance ($R^2 = 0.525$ at count 16), substantially higher than FewRel ($R^2 = 0.218$), possibly reflecting a wider confusability range in the DocRED schema, though document-level context may also provide richer cues for type confusion. Across all RE settings, BCS slopes range from $-$0.40 to $-$0.71, significant in all conditions with sufficient BCS range.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-pool controlled comparison.}\n\\label{sec:within_pool}\nWithin-pool analysis confirms slope consistency across pools (permutation $p \\approx 0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:within-pool-method}). The 80-relation extreme-batch gap (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) reflects wider BCS range, not stronger per-unit sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}\n\\label{sec:practical_magnitude}\nFor representative batches from the 80-relation pool ($n{=}100$ random 16-type subsets), the typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\\,pp (95\\% CI: [0.4, 4.0])---roughly 7\\% of the extreme-batch gap. Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\\% vs.\\ 78.9\\% for top-quartile ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp). The 31.2\\,pp figure reflects maximum BCS contrast, not the typical batch experience. Conditional pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ among non-zero confusion pairs (vs.\\ unconditional $r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$ across all pairs; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}; we note the conditioning inflates this estimate) aggregates to batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ ($\\sim$3$\\times$ amplification, consistent with a Monte Carlo simulation accounting for inter-pair correlation structure; see Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim} for the illustrative aggregation model).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}\nThe effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: BCS gaps of $-$27.1\\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\\% of Qwen3-32B's gap. Both models converge to $\\approx$51\\% under high confusability regardless of count, while divergence is confined to the low-BCS regime---suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor under high confusability (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Channel~1 replicates fully on Llama-3.1-8B (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}); Channel~2 replicates on RE but shows floor effects on 8B NER (Limitations, item~1).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}\nUnder the v2 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; $n{=}25$; $r = -0.919$; $p < 0.001$)---modestly attenuated from v1's extreme-batch gap but highly significant. NER v2 yields consistent batch-level correlations ($r = +0.58$ vs.\\ $r = +0.50$ under v1). Prompt design modulates magnitude but does not eliminate the effect (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}). The BCS--accuracy correlation holds across four structurally distinct prompt variants at count 32 (all $r < -0.47$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}), confirming that the confusability effect is not an artifact of prompt design.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Channel Degradation}\n\\label{sec:multi_channel}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}\n\\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition. IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS in both tasks. RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic.}\n\\label{fig:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{figure}\n\nError taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.\n\nThese results establish two channels: \\textbf{Channel~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \\textbf{Channel~2} (confusability predicts type confusion), consistent with bounded interaction in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact. The full-range regression ($n = 69$, five count levels) serves as the primary separability test; the $2 \\times 2$ factorial ($n = 54$) as a sensitivity check. TOST equivalence testing confirms bounded interaction (interaction = 0.47\\,pp, $p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}).\n\n\\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}\n\\label{sec:centrality_confound}\n\nPer-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \\emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation (Qwen3 $\\rho = +0.184$, $p = 0.025$; MiniLM $\\rho = +0.208$, $p = 0.011$). This traces to same-domain embedding collapse \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}: MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly, so mean BCS conflates confusability with centrality---high-BCS types are semantically central and easy to extract. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the positive correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}). The pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}); the problem is per-type aggregation, not the underlying relationship. Few-NERD's cross-domain schema does not exhibit this confound ($\\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$). Mean BCS is thus reliable only for cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas require pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment.","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":64,"oldLines":7,"newStart":64,"newLines":7,"lines":[" "," \\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}"," \\label{sec:practical_magnitude}","-For representative batches from the 80-relation pool ($n{=}100$ random 16-type subsets), the typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\\,pp (95\\% CI: [0.4, 4.0])---roughly 7\\% of the extreme-batch gap. Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\\% vs.\\ 78.9\\% for top-quartile ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp).
