{"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-opus-4-6","id":"msg_01BsHKPzyzn8BQcCxys7GTmW","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_017HuCADt8u41Mm8KZSj7LCd","name":"Edit","input":{"replace_all":false,"file_path":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/sdgd_negative.tex","old_string":"confusability-driven degradation","new_string":"confusability-associated degradation"},"caller":{"type":"direct"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"stop_details":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":1,"cache_creation_input_tokens":238,"cache_read_input_tokens":94321,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":0,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":238},"output_tokens":1,"service_tier":"standard","inference_geo":"not_available"},"diagnostics":null,"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"f333e9d4-4b6d-4a8f-a509-cacb7606b5a5"}
{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_017HuCADt8u41Mm8KZSj7LCd","type":"tool_result","content":"The file /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/sdgd_negative.tex has been updated successfully."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"daaadcf5-4e97-4c1c-8780-d798724843de","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:06:59.604Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/sdgd_negative.tex","oldString":"confusability-driven degradation","newString":"confusability-associated degradation","originalFile":"\\section{Schema Decomposition: Failure, Diagnosis, and Mitigation}\n\\label{sec:sdgd}\n\nA natural intervention against confusability-driven degradation is to partition the schema so that confusable types never co-occur in the same prompt. We test this idea with \\textbf{Schema-DAG-Guided Decomposition} (SDGD\\footnote{SDGD (Schema Decomposition and Grouping for Degradation mitigation) is an adopted abbreviation for schema decomposition and grouping; the graph structure is undirected.}), which uses greedy graph coloring on the pairwise SCS graph to assign types to low-confusability batches.\n\nWe construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \\tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\\textsc{largest\\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent---i.e., highly confusable---types share a batch. Each color class forms one extraction batch. For NER, predictions from all batches are merged via set union; for RE, each instance is routed to the batch containing its gold type (oracle routing). We test four NER modes: (i)~\\textbf{single\\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\\textbf{sdgd\\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\\textbf{random\\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\\textbf{per\\_type}, each type extracted in isolation. Every mode falls below the single-pass NER baseline: SDGD obtains F1\\,=\\,0.251, random batching 0.320, per-type 0.292, versus 0.445 for single-pass (Table~\\ref{tab:sdgd_results}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd}).\n\n\\paragraph{Merge amplification in NER.}\nThe core failure mechanism is \\emph{merge amplification}: presenting the same text to the model $k$ times with different type subsets causes each pass to independently generate entity predictions, and union merge accumulates all of them into a single output. Each pass produces an independent set of span predictions; because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes \\cite{llm-calibration-survey-2024}, the same span may receive conflicting type labels from different passes, or hallucinated entities may appear in passes where the correct type is absent. Union merge retains all predictions, so false positives scale with $k$ while true positives are bounded by the fixed set of gold entities.\n\nQuantitatively, single-pass precision (0.563) collapses to 0.210--0.249 after union merge across 8 batches, while recall gains are modest (0.368 $\\to$ 0.314 for sdgd\\_zero, 0.446 for random batch). The net F1 loss is substantial: 0.445 $\\to$ 0.251 (sdgd\\_zero) or 0.320 (random batch). Per-type extraction (66 batches) pushes recall to 0.586 but precision to 0.194 (F1\\,=\\,0.292), confirming that the precision--recall imbalance intensifies with the number of passes. Random batching outperforms confusability-aware SDGD on NER (0.320 vs.\\ 0.251) because the dominant degradation source---merge amplification---is independent of batch composition; any multi-pass scheme suffers from it regardless of grouping quality.\n\n\\paragraph{Confidence gating does not resolve merge amplification.}\nConfidence-based gating (retaining only predictions above threshold) trades recall for precision but does not recover net F1 (0.251$\\to$0.241), confirming that merge artifacts are systemic rather than confined to low-confidence predictions (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd}).\n\n\\paragraph{Why NER fails but RE does not.}\nMerge amplification is specific to span-level tasks. Each NER pass independently proposes entity spans, and union merge must resolve conflicting boundaries and type assignments, degrading systematically with pass count. RE avoids this mechanism entirely: under oracle routing, each relation instance receives a single classification from the batch containing its gold type, requiring no span reconciliation and eliminating the multi-pass interference that causes SDGD to fail on NER. This makes RE a clean testbed for evaluating whether confusability-aware decomposition improves accuracy once merge artifacts are removed.\n\n\\enlargethispage*{9\\baselineskip}\n\\paragraph{Oracle routing as performance ceiling.}\nOracle routing routes each test instance to the batch containing its gold type, eliminating cross-type confusion within each batch. On FewRel 80-relation RE, oracle-routed decomposition achieves 0.802 accuracy versus 0.504 for the single-pass baseline---a gain of +29.8\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}). This is the strongest positive finding of the decomposition experiments: it demonstrates that type confusability is indeed a key driver of degradation in RE, and that performance recovers substantially when confusable types are separated.\n\nHowever, oracle routing requires gold labels and is not deployable. The gap between the oracle ceiling and practical routing reveals an important asymmetry: while \\emph{any} decomposition benefits enormously from removing the 80-type bottleneck (+28.4\\,pp for random batching, +29.8\\,pp for graph coloring), the \\emph{additional} benefit of confusability-aware grouping over random grouping is modest (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$). The confusability-aware strategy ranks 13th out of 15 random seeds, limited by the narrow BCS margin (0.006) between graph-coloring and random batches at this partition granularity. Finer-grained partitioning or alternative confusability metrics may be needed to translate the oracle-demonstrated potential into significant practical gains.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical routing strategies.}\nWe evaluate five non-oracle routing strategies on FewRel 80-relation RE using a coarser 5-group decomposition (oracle ceiling: 72.4\\%, +22.0\\,pp; cf.\\ +29.8\\,pp under 17-group decomposition in Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}): max-confidence (all predictions), max-confidence (positive only), none-aware selection, margin-based routing, and random assignment. All five perform below the single-pass baseline (50.4\\%), with the best practical strategy reaching only 36.5\\% accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:routing_strategies}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_routing}). The root cause is constrained decoding: the enum constraint forces the model to produce a confident type prediction for every sub-schema, even when the correct type is absent from the candidate set. As a result, 73.4\\% of instances receive positive predictions from 3--5 sub-schemas simultaneously, and the none-rate per sub-schema is only 20--40\\%. Because every sub-schema produces high-confidence outputs, confidence signals become unreliable and all confidence-based routing fails. Free-form generation or calibrated confidence estimation may provide more reliable routing signals for future work.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{Mean BCS} \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-pass (80 types) & 0.504 & 0.557 \\\\\nRandom batching (15 seeds)$^\\dagger$ & 0.788\\,$\\pm$\\,0.018 & 0.559 \\\\\nConfus.-aware (graph coloring) & \\textbf{0.802} & 0.552 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Batch composition on FewRel 80-relation RE with oracle routing. $^\\dagger$Mean $\\pm$ SD across 15 random seeds. Graph coloring yields +1.4\\,pp over random batching ($p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) with a narrow BCS margin (0.006). The +29.8\\,pp gap between single-pass and decomposed conditions confirms that type separation recovers performance when merge amplification is absent.}\n\\label{tab:batch_composition}\n\\end{table}\n","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":1,"oldLines":7,"newStart":1,"newLines":7,"lines":[" \\section{Schema Decomposition: Failure, Diagnosis, and Mitigation}"," \\label{sec:sdgd}"," ","-A natural intervention against confusability-driven degradation is to partition the schema so that confusable types never co-occur in the same prompt. We test this idea with \\textbf{Schema-DAG-Guided Decomposition} (SDGD\\footnote{SDGD (Schema Decomposition and Grouping for Degradation mitigation) is an adopted abbreviation for schema decomposition and grouping; the graph structure is undirected.}), which uses greedy graph coloring on the pairwise SCS graph to assign types to low-confusability batches.","+A natural intervention against confusability-associated degradation is to partition the schema so that confusable types never co-occur in the same prompt. We test this idea with \\textbf{Schema-DAG-Guided Decomposition} (SDGD\\footnote{SDGD (Schema Decomposition and Grouping for Degradation mitigation) is an adopted abbreviation for schema decomposition and grouping; the graph structure is undirected.}), which uses greedy graph coloring on the pairwise SCS graph to assign types to low-confusability batches."," "," We construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \\tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\\textsc{largest\\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent---i.e., highly confusable---types share a batch. Each color class forms one extraction batch. For NER, predictions from all batches are merged via set union; for RE, each instance is routed to the batch containing its gold type (oracle routing). We test four NER modes: (i)~\\textbf{single\\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\\textbf{sdgd\\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\\textbf{random\\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\\textbf{per\\_type}, each type extracted in isolation. Every mode falls below the single-pass NER baseline: SDGD obtains F1\\,=\\,0.251, random batching 0.320, per-type 0.292, versus 0.445 for single-pass (Table~\\ref{tab:sdgd_results}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd})."," "]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01XJSUApHjPKVsj9zmMRRC2P","type":"tool_result","content":"The file /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/sdgd_negative.tex has been updated successfully."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"fd60e364-a66f-45a6-a9ce-37c0ebcc7a86","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:07:05.011Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/sdgd_negative.tex","oldString":"core failure mechanism","newString":"core failure mode","originalFile":"\\section{Schema Decomposition: Failure, Diagnosis, and Mitigation}\n\\label{sec:sdgd}\n\nA natural intervention against confusability-associated degradation is to partition the schema so that confusable types never co-occur in the same prompt. We test this idea with \\textbf{Schema-DAG-Guided Decomposition} (SDGD\\footnote{SDGD (Schema Decomposition and Grouping for Degradation mitigation) is an adopted abbreviation for schema decomposition and grouping; the graph structure is undirected.}), which uses greedy graph coloring on the pairwise SCS graph to assign types to low-confusability batches.\n\nWe construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \\tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\\textsc{largest\\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent (i.e., highly confusable) types share a batch. Each color class forms one extraction batch. For NER, predictions from all batches are merged via set union; for RE, each instance is routed to the batch containing its gold type (oracle routing). We test four NER modes: (i)~\\textbf{single\\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\\textbf{sdgd\\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\\textbf{random\\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\\textbf{per\\_type}, each type extracted in isolation. Every mode falls below the single-pass NER baseline: SDGD obtains F1\\,=\\,0.251, random batching 0.320, per-type 0.292, versus 0.445 for single-pass (Table~\\ref{tab:sdgd_results}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd}).\n\n\\paragraph{Merge amplification in NER.}\nThe core failure mechanism is \\emph{merge amplification}: presenting the same text to the model $k$ times with different type subsets causes each pass to independently generate entity predictions, and union merge accumulates all of them into a single output. Each pass produces an independent set of span predictions; because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes \\cite{llm-calibration-survey-2024}, the same span may receive conflicting type labels from different passes, or hallucinated entities may appear in passes where the correct type is absent. Union merge retains all predictions, so false positives scale with $k$ while true positives are bounded by the fixed set of gold entities.\n\nQuantitatively, single-pass precision (0.563) collapses to 0.210--0.249 after union merge across 8 batches, while recall gains are modest (0.368 $\\to$ 0.314 for sdgd\\_zero, 0.446 for random batch). The net F1 loss is substantial: 0.445 $\\to$ 0.251 (sdgd\\_zero) or 0.320 (random batch). Per-type extraction (66 batches) pushes recall to 0.586 but precision to 0.194 (F1\\,=\\,0.292), confirming that the precision--recall imbalance intensifies with the number of passes. Random batching outperforms confusability-aware SDGD on NER (0.320 vs.\\ 0.251) because the dominant degradation source---merge amplification---is independent of batch composition; any multi-pass scheme suffers from it regardless of grouping quality.\n\n\\paragraph{Confidence gating does not resolve merge amplification.}\nConfidence-based gating (retaining only predictions above threshold) trades recall for precision but does not recover net F1 (0.251$\\to$0.241), confirming that merge artifacts are systemic rather than confined to low-confidence predictions (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd}).\n\n\\paragraph{Why NER fails but RE does not.}\nMerge amplification is specific to span-level tasks. Each NER pass independently proposes entity spans, and union merge must resolve conflicting boundaries and type assignments, degrading systematically with pass count. RE avoids this mechanism entirely: under oracle routing, each relation instance receives a single classification from the batch containing its gold type, requiring no span reconciliation and eliminating the multi-pass interference that causes SDGD to fail on NER. This makes RE a clean testbed for evaluating whether confusability-aware decomposition improves accuracy once merge artifacts are removed.\n\n\\enlargethispage*{9\\baselineskip}\n\\paragraph{Oracle routing as performance ceiling.}\nOracle routing routes each test instance to the batch containing its gold type, eliminating cross-type confusion within each batch. On FewRel 80-relation RE, oracle-routed decomposition achieves 0.802 accuracy versus 0.504 for the single-pass baseline---a gain of +29.8\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}). This is the strongest positive finding of the decomposition experiments: it demonstrates that type confusability is indeed a key driver of degradation in RE, and that performance recovers substantially when confusable types are separated.\n\nHowever, oracle routing requires gold labels and is not deployable. The gap between the oracle ceiling and practical routing reveals an important asymmetry: while \\emph{any} decomposition benefits enormously from removing the 80-type bottleneck (+28.4\\,pp for random batching, +29.8\\,pp for graph coloring), the \\emph{additional} benefit of confusability-aware grouping over random grouping is modest (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$). The confusability-aware strategy ranks 13th out of 15 random seeds, limited by the narrow BCS margin (0.006) between graph-coloring and random batches at this partition granularity. Finer-grained partitioning or alternative confusability metrics may be needed to translate the oracle-demonstrated potential into significant practical gains.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical routing strategies.}\nWe evaluate five non-oracle routing strategies on FewRel 80-relation RE using a coarser 5-group decomposition (oracle ceiling: 72.4\\%, +22.0\\,pp; cf.\\ +29.8\\,pp under 17-group decomposition in Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}): max-confidence (all predictions), max-confidence (positive only), none-aware selection, margin-based routing, and random assignment. All five perform below the single-pass baseline (50.4\\%), with the best practical strategy reaching only 36.5\\% accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:routing_strategies}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_routing}). The root cause is constrained decoding: the enum constraint forces the model to produce a confident type prediction for every sub-schema, even when the correct type is absent from the candidate set. As a result, 73.4\\% of instances receive positive predictions from 3--5 sub-schemas simultaneously, and the none-rate per sub-schema is only 20--40\\%. Because every sub-schema produces high-confidence outputs, confidence signals become unreliable and all confidence-based routing fails. Free-form generation or calibrated confidence estimation may provide more reliable routing signals for future work.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{Mean BCS} \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-pass (80 types) & 0.504 & 0.557 \\\\\nRandom batching (15 seeds)$^\\dagger$ & 0.788\\,$\\pm$\\,0.018 & 0.559 \\\\\nConfus.-aware (graph coloring) & \\textbf{0.802} & 0.552 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Batch composition on FewRel 80-relation RE with oracle routing. $^\\dagger$Mean $\\pm$ SD across 15 random seeds. Graph coloring yields +1.4\\,pp over random batching ($p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) with a narrow BCS margin (0.006). The +29.8\\,pp gap between single-pass and decomposed conditions confirms that type separation recovers performance when merge amplification is absent.}\n\\label{tab:batch_composition}\n\\end{table}\n","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":6,"oldLines":7,"newStart":6,"newLines":7,"lines":[" We construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \\tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\\textsc{largest\\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent (i.e., highly confusable) types share a batch. Each color class forms one extraction batch. For NER, predictions from all batches are merged via set union; for RE, each instance is routed to the batch containing its gold type (oracle routing). We test four NER modes: (i)~\\textbf{single\\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\\textbf{sdgd\\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\\textbf{random\\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\\textbf{per\\_type}, each type extracted in isolation. Every mode falls below the single-pass NER baseline: SDGD obtains F1\\,=\\,0.251, random batching 0.320, per-type 0.292, versus 0.445 for single-pass (Table~\\ref{tab:sdgd_results}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd})."," "," \\paragraph{Merge amplification in NER.}","-The core failure mechanism is \\emph{merge amplification}: presenting the same text to the model $k$ times with different type subsets causes each pass to independently generate entity predictions, and union merge accumulates all of them into a single output. Each pass produces an independent set of span predictions; because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes \\cite{llm-calibration-survey-2024}, the same span may receive conflicting type labels from different passes, or hallucinated entities may appear in passes where the correct type is absent. Union merge retains all predictions, so false positives scale with $k$ while true positives are bounded by the fixed set of gold entities.","+The core failure mode is \\emph{merge amplification}: presenting the same text to the model $k$ times with different type subsets causes each pass to independently generate entity predictions, and union merge accumulates all of them into a single output. Union merge retains all predictions, so false positives scale with $k$ while true positives are bounded by the fixed set of gold entities."," "," Quantitatively, single-pass precision (0.563) collapses to 0.210--0.249 after union merge across 8 batches, while recall gains are modest (0.368 $\\to$ 0.314 for sdgd\\_zero, 0.446 for random batch). This makes RE a clean testbed for evaluating whether confusability-aware decomposition improves accuracy once merge artifacts are removed.\n\n\\enlargethispage*{9\\baselineskip}\n\\paragraph{Oracle routing as performance ceiling.}\nOracle routing routes each test instance to the batch containing its gold type, eliminating cross-type confusion within each batch. On FewRel 80-relation RE, oracle-routed decomposition achieves 0.802 accuracy versus 0.504 for the single-pass baseline---a gain of +29.8\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}). This is the strongest positive finding of the decomposition experiments: it demonstrates that type confusability is indeed a key driver of degradation in RE, and that performance recovers substantially when confusable types are separated.\n\nHowever, oracle routing requires gold labels and is not deployable. The gap between the oracle ceiling and practical routing reveals an important asymmetry: while \\emph{any} decomposition benefits enormously from removing the 80-type bottleneck (+28.4\\,pp for random batching, +29.8\\,pp for graph coloring), the \\emph{additional} benefit of confusability-aware grouping over random grouping is modest (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$). The confusability-aware strategy ranks 13th out of 15 random seeds, limited by the narrow BCS margin (0.006) between graph-coloring and random batches at this partition granularity. Finer-grained partitioning or alternative confusability metrics may be needed to translate the oracle-demonstrated potential into significant practical gains.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical routing strategies.}\nWe evaluate five non-oracle routing strategies on FewRel 80-relation RE using a coarser 5-group decomposition (oracle ceiling: 72.4\\%, +22.0\\,pp; cf.\\ +29.8\\,pp under 17-group decomposition in Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}): max-confidence (all predictions), max-confidence (positive only), none-aware selection, margin-based routing, and random assignment. All five perform below the single-pass baseline (50.4\\%), with the best practical strategy reaching only 36.5\\% accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:routing_strategies}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_routing}). The root cause is constrained decoding: the enum constraint forces the model to produce a confident type prediction for every sub-schema, even when the correct type is absent from the candidate set. As a result, 73.4\\% of instances receive positive predictions from 3--5 sub-schemas simultaneously, and the none-rate per sub-schema is only 20--40\\%. Because every sub-schema produces high-confidence outputs, confidence signals become unreliable and all confidence-based routing fails. Free-form generation or calibrated confidence estimation may provide more reliable routing signals for future work.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{Mean BCS} \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-pass (80 types) & 0.504 & 0.557 \\\\\nRandom batching (15 seeds)$^\\dagger$ & 0.788\\,$\\pm$\\,0.018 & 0.559 \\\\\nConfus.-aware (graph coloring) & \\textbf{0.802} & 0.552 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Batch composition on FewRel 80-relation RE with oracle routing. $^\\dagger$Mean $\\pm$ SD across 15 random seeds. Graph coloring yields +1.4\\,pp over random batching ($p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) with a narrow BCS margin (0.006). The +29.8\\,pp gap between single-pass and decomposed conditions confirms that type separation recovers performance when merge amplification is absent.}\n\\label{tab:batch_composition}\n\\end{table}\n","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":14,"oldLines":7,"newStart":14,"newLines":7,"lines":[" Confidence-based gating (retaining only predictions above threshold) trades recall for precision but does not recover net F1 (0.251$\\to$0.241), confirming that merge artifacts are systemic rather than confined to low-confidence predictions (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd})."," "," \\paragraph{Why NER fails but RE does not.}","-Merge amplification is specific to span-level tasks. Each NER pass independently proposes entity spans, and union merge must resolve conflicting boundaries and type assignments, degrading systematically with pass count. RE avoids this mechanism entirely: under oracle routing, each relation instance receives a single classification from the batch containing its gold type, requiring no span reconciliation and eliminating the multi-pass interference that causes SDGD to fail on NER. This makes RE a clean testbed for evaluating whether confusability-aware decomposition improves accuracy once merge artifacts are removed.","+Merge amplification is specific to span-level tasks. {"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-opus-4-6","id":"msg_01TU7Whs8iNqJ3rEmjJ3GKpu","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_012Mo4w9j8X26EidXBa1sVUo","name":"Edit","input":{"replace_all":false,"file_path":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/sdgd_negative.tex","old_string":"a key driver of degradation in RE","new_string":"a key predictor of degradation in RE"},"caller":{"type":"direct"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"stop_details":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":1,"cache_creation_input_tokens":214,"cache_read_input_tokens":95445,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":0,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":214},"output_tokens":72,"service_tier":"standard","inference_geo":"not_available"},"diagnostics":null,"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"c0d2a83e-bee3-4465-b917-1814e1f370ea"}
{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_012Mo4w9j8X26EidXBa1sVUo","type":"tool_result","content":"The file /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/sdgd_negative.tex has been updated successfully."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"1479b8dc-78dd-40e0-9ff0-5e102c78a7c4","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:07:13.085Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/sdgd_negative.tex","oldString":"a key driver of degradation in RE","newString":"a key predictor of degradation in RE","originalFile":"\\section{Schema Decomposition: Failure, Diagnosis, and Mitigation}\n\\label{sec:sdgd}\n\nA natural intervention against confusability-associated degradation is to partition the schema so that confusable types never co-occur in the same prompt. We test this idea with \\textbf{Schema-DAG-Guided Decomposition} (SDGD\\footnote{SDGD (Schema Decomposition and Grouping for Degradation mitigation) is an adopted abbreviation for schema decomposition and grouping; the graph structure is undirected.}), which uses greedy graph coloring on the pairwise SCS graph to assign types to low-confusability batches.\n\nWe construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \\tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\\textsc{largest\\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent (i.e., highly confusable) types share a batch. Each color class forms one extraction batch. For NER, predictions from all batches are merged via set union; for RE, each instance is routed to the batch containing its gold type (oracle routing). We test four NER modes: (i)~\\textbf{single\\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\\textbf{sdgd\\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\\textbf{random\\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\\textbf{per\\_type}, each type extracted in isolation. Every mode falls below the single-pass NER baseline: SDGD obtains F1\\,=\\,0.251, random batching 0.320, per-type 0.292, versus 0.445 for single-pass (Table~\\ref{tab:sdgd_results}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd}).\n\n\\paragraph{Merge amplification in NER.}\nThe core failure mode is \\emph{merge amplification}: presenting the same text to the model $k$ times with different type subsets causes each pass to independently generate entity predictions, and union merge accumulates all of them into a single output. Each pass produces an independent set of span predictions; because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes \\cite{llm-calibration-survey-2024}, the same span may receive conflicting type labels from different passes, or hallucinated entities may appear in passes where the correct type is absent. Union merge retains all predictions, so false positives scale with $k$ while true positives are bounded by the fixed set of gold entities.\n\nQuantitatively, single-pass precision (0.563) collapses to 0.210--0.249 after union merge across 8 batches, while recall gains are modest (0.368 $\\to$ 0.314 for sdgd\\_zero, 0.446 for random batch). The net F1 loss is substantial: 0.445 $\\to$ 0.251 (sdgd\\_zero) or 0.320 (random batch). Per-type extraction (66 batches) pushes recall to 0.586 but precision to 0.194 (F1\\,=\\,0.292), confirming that the precision--recall imbalance intensifies with the number of passes. Random batching outperforms confusability-aware SDGD on NER (0.320 vs.\\ 0.251) because the dominant degradation source (merge amplification) is independent of batch composition; any multi-pass scheme suffers from it regardless of grouping quality.\n\n\\paragraph{Confidence gating does not resolve merge amplification.}\nConfidence-based gating (retaining only predictions above threshold) trades recall for precision but does not recover net F1 (0.251$\\to$0.241), confirming that merge artifacts are systemic rather than confined to low-confidence predictions (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd}).\n\n\\paragraph{Why NER fails but RE does not.}\nMerge amplification is specific to span-level tasks. Each NER pass independently proposes entity spans, and union merge must resolve conflicting boundaries and type assignments, degrading systematically with pass count. RE avoids this failure mode entirely: under oracle routing, each relation instance receives a single classification from the batch containing its gold type, requiring no span reconciliation and eliminating the multi-pass interference that causes SDGD to fail on NER. This makes RE a clean testbed for evaluating whether confusability-aware decomposition improves accuracy once merge artifacts are removed.\n\n\\enlargethispage*{9\\baselineskip}\n\\paragraph{Oracle routing as performance ceiling.}\nOracle routing routes each test instance to the batch containing its gold type, eliminating cross-type confusion within each batch. On FewRel 80-relation RE, oracle-routed decomposition achieves 0.802 accuracy versus 0.504 for the single-pass baseline---a gain of +29.8\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}). This is the strongest positive finding of the decomposition experiments: it demonstrates that type confusability is indeed a key driver of degradation in RE, and that performance recovers substantially when confusable types are separated.\n\nHowever, oracle routing requires gold labels and is not deployable. The gap between the oracle ceiling and practical routing reveals an important asymmetry: while \\emph{any} decomposition benefits enormously from removing the 80-type bottleneck (+28.4\\,pp for random batching, +29.8\\,pp for graph coloring), the \\emph{additional} benefit of confusability-aware grouping over random grouping is modest (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$). The confusability-aware strategy ranks 13th out of 15 random seeds, limited by the narrow BCS margin (0.006) between graph-coloring and random batches at this partition granularity. Finer-grained partitioning or alternative confusability metrics may be needed to translate the oracle-demonstrated potential into significant practical gains.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical routing strategies.}\nWe evaluate five non-oracle routing strategies on FewRel 80-relation RE using a coarser 5-group decomposition (oracle ceiling: 72.4\\%, +22.0\\,pp; cf.\\ +29.8\\,pp under 17-group decomposition in Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}): max-confidence (all predictions), max-confidence (positive only), none-aware selection, margin-based routing, and random assignment. All five perform below the single-pass baseline (50.4\\%), with the best practical strategy reaching only 36.5\\% accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:routing_strategies}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_routing}). The root cause is constrained decoding: the enum constraint forces the model to produce a confident type prediction for every sub-schema, even when the correct type is absent from the candidate set. As a result, 73.4\\% of instances receive positive predictions from 3--5 sub-schemas simultaneously, and the none-rate per sub-schema is only 20--40\\%. Because every sub-schema produces high-confidence outputs, confidence signals become unreliable and all confidence-based routing fails. Free-form generation or calibrated confidence estimation may provide more reliable routing signals for future work.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{Mean BCS} \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-pass (80 types) & 0.504 & 0.557 \\\\\nRandom batching (15 seeds)$^\\dagger$ & 0.788\\,$\\pm$\\,0.018 & 0.559 \\\\\nConfus.-aware (graph coloring) & \\textbf{0.802} & 0.552 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Batch composition on FewRel 80-relation RE with oracle routing. $^\\dagger$Mean $\\pm$ SD across 15 random seeds. Graph coloring yields +1.4\\,pp over random batching ($p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) with a narrow BCS margin (0.006). The +29.8\\,pp gap between single-pass and decomposed conditions confirms that type separation recovers performance when merge amplification is absent.}\n\\label{tab:batch_composition}\n\\end{table}\n","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":18,"oldLines":7,"newStart":18,"newLines":7,"lines":[" "," \\enlargethispage*{9\\baselineskip}"," \\paragraph{Oracle routing as performance ceiling.}","-Oracle routing routes each test instance to the batch containing its gold type, eliminating cross-type confusion within each batch. On FewRel 80-relation RE, oracle-routed decomposition achieves 0.802 accuracy versus 0.504 for the single-pass baseline---a gain of +29.8\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}). This is the strongest positive finding of the decomposition experiments: it demonstrates that type confusability is indeed a key driver of degradation in RE, and that performance recovers substantially when confusable types are separated.","+Oracle routing routes each test instance to the batch containing its gold type, eliminating cross-type confusion within each batch. On FewRel 80-relation RE, oracle-routed decomposition achieves 0.802 accuracy versus 0.504 for the single-pass baseline---a gain of +29.8\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}). This is the strongest positive finding of the decomposition experiments: it demonstrates that type confusability is indeed a key predictor of degradation in RE, and that performance recovers substantially when confusable types are separated."," "," However, oracle routing requires gold labels and is not deployable. The gap between the oracle ceiling and practical routing reveals an important asymmetry: while \\emph{any} decomposition benefits enormously from removing the 80-type bottleneck (+28.4\\,pp for random batching, +29.8\\,pp for graph coloring), the \\emph{additional} benefit of confusability-aware grouping over random grouping is modest (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$). The confusability-aware strategy ranks 13th out of 15 random seeds, limited by the narrow BCS margin (0.006) between graph-coloring and random batches at this partition granularity. Finer-grained partitioning or alternative confusability metrics may be needed to translate the oracle-demonstrated potential into significant practical gains."," "]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01P6kWXBRnH3sZHVgAj4Tebe","type":"tool_result","content":"The file /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/checklist.tex has been updated successfully."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"90846268-bc36-4324-9009-c8751031cca1","uuid":"9469420e-0c50-4c42-bfbc-dd1d438f1574","timestamp":"2026-05-17T08:07:16.228Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/checklist.tex","oldString":"two-channel degradation pattern","newString":"two-dimensional degradation pattern","originalFile":"\\section*{Responsible NLP Research Checklist}\n\nFor each question, the answer is \\textbf{Yes}, \\textbf{No}, or \\textbf{N/A}. If Yes, the relevant section is indicated. If No, a justification is provided.\n\n\\subsection*{A \\quad For Every Submission}\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=\\textbf{A\\arabic*.},leftmargin=*,itemsep=4pt]\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you describe the limitations of your work?}\n\nYes. The Limitations section (after \\S\\ref{sec:conclusion}) discusses single-model reliance, modest pairwise effect sizes, cross-pool slope consistency, factorial interaction tests with TOST equivalence, decomposition scope, and metric scope.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you discuss any potential risks of your work?}\n\nN/A. This is a purely analytical study of schema-induced degradation in existing LLM IE systems. It does not introduce new models, datasets, or deployment-facing artifacts. The findings characterize failure modes and are intended to inform safer schema design.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Do the abstract and introduction summarize the paper's main claims?}\n\nYes. The abstract and \\S\\ref{sec:introduction} state the two-channel degradation pattern (count and confusability), the cross-model consistency check, and the negative SDGD result.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you use AI assistants (e.g., ChatGPT, Copilot) when working on this paper?}\n\nYes. We used Claude (Anthropic) for writing assistance and experimental code development. All scientific claims, experimental designs, and analyses were conducted by the authors.\n\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\subsection*{B \\quad Scientific Artifacts}\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=\\textbf{B\\arabic*.},leftmargin=*,itemsep=4pt]\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you cite the creators of artifacts you used?}\n\nYes. Few-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021}, FewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018}, DocRED \\cite{docred-2019}, MAVEN \\cite{maven-2020}, Qwen3-32B \\cite{qwen3-2025}, Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\cite{llama3-2024}, vLLM \\cite{vllm-2023}, and all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \\cite{minilm-2020} are cited in \\S\\ref{sec:datasets} and \\S\\ref{sec:model}.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you discuss the license or terms for use and/or distribution of any artifacts?}\n\nYes. All four datasets are publicly available research benchmarks. Few-NERD is released under CC-BY-SA 4.0; FewRel, DocRED, and MAVEN are publicly released for research use. Models are used under their respective open-weight licenses.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you discuss if your use of existing artifacts is consistent with their intended use?}\n\nYes. All datasets are used for their intended purpose (IE evaluation). No data is used outside the scope specified by the dataset creators.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you discuss whether the data contains personally identifiable information or offensive content?}\n\nN/A. We use established benchmarks that have been vetted by the research community. No new data collection or annotation was performed.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you provide documentation of the artifacts, e.g., coverage of languages, coverage of domains, etc.?}\n\nYes. Table~\\ref{tab:datasets} reports type counts, evaluation unit counts, domain characteristics, and BCS ranges for each dataset. \\S\\ref{sec:datasets} describes dataset provenance and sampling procedures.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you report relevant statistics for your dataset(s)?}\n\nYes. \\S\\ref{sec:datasets} and Table~\\ref{tab:datasets} report evaluation set sizes, type counts, and sampling methodology. Count levels and batch sizes are specified in \\S\\ref{sec:exp_design}.\n\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\subsection*{C \\quad Computational Experiments}\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=\\textbf{C\\arabic*.},leftmargin=*,itemsep=4pt]\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you report the number of parameters in the models used, the total computational budget, and computing infrastructure?}\n\nYes. \\S\\ref{sec:model}: Qwen3-32B (32B parameters, FP8) and Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (8B parameters). Inference was conducted on a single RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPU (96\\,GB) served via vLLM. Total compute was approximately 48 GPU-hours of inference (no training).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you discuss the experimental setup, including hyperparameter search and best-found values?}\n\nYes. \\S\\ref{sec:exp_design} describes both experimental paradigms (count curves and BCS-controlled). \\S\\ref{sec:model} reports decoding parameters (greedy, temperature${}=0$, seed${}=42$) and prompt versions. No hyperparameter search was conducted as this is a zero-shot evaluation study.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you report descriptive statistics about your results?}\n\nYes. All key metrics include 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals (10{,}000 resamples, percentile method) as stated in \\S\\ref{sec:model}. Spearman correlations are reported with $p$-values throughout \\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}--\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}. Permutation tests are used for slope comparisons.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you report implementation details, e.g., versions of packages?}\n\nYes. \\S\\ref{sec:model} specifies vLLM v0.20.2 for model serving. Embedding models are named with version identifiers (\\S\\ref{sec:scs}). Code and scripts will be released upon acceptance.\n\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\subsection*{D \\quad Human Annotators or Crowdworkers}\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=\\textbf{D\\arabic*.},leftmargin=*,itemsep=4pt]\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you report the full text of instructions given to participants?}\n\nN/A. No human annotation or crowdsourcing was conducted.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you report information about how you recruited and paid participants?}\n\nN/A.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you discuss whether and how consent was obtained?}\n\nN/A.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you report whether the data collection was approved by an institutional review board (IRB)?}\n\nN/A.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you report the basic demographic and geographic characteristics of the annotator population?}\n\nN/A.\n\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\subsection*{E \\quad AI-Assisted Research}\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=\\textbf{E\\arabic*.},leftmargin=*,itemsep=4pt]\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you report the use of AI assistants?}\n\nYes. See A4 above. AI assistants were used for code development and document formatting. The research design, execution, and interpretation are the authors' own work.\n\n\\end{enumerate}\n","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":16,"oldLines":7,"newStart":16,"newLines":7,"lines":[" "," \\item \\textbf{Do the abstract and introduction summarize the paper's main claims?}"," ","-Yes. The abstract and \\S\\ref{sec:introduction} state the two-channel degradation pattern (count and confusability), the cross-model consistency check, and the negative SDGD result.","+Yes. The abstract and \\S\\ref{sec:introduction} state the two-dimensional degradation pattern (count and confusability), the cross-model consistency check, and the negative SDGD result."," "," \\item \\textbf{Did you use AI assistants (e.g., ChatGPT, Copilot) when working on this paper?}"," "]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}
